->**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**<-
<note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"; This refers to a philosophical and magical tradition based on the purported writings of Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on the relationship between the divine, the cosmos, and humanity.</note>

*The center of the page features a symbolic illustration that serves as a visual summary of the manuscript’s themes:*

In the heart of the emblem, a **pelican in its piety** <term>Pelican in its Piety: A legendary image of a mother pelican wounding her own breast to feed her young with her blood; in alchemy, it symbolizes the "Great Work" of purification and the life-giving nature of the philosopher's stone</term> sits upon a nest shaped like a crescent moon. Above the pelican, a **radiant golden sun** pours down beams of light, representing divine wisdom and the "gold" of spiritual perfection.

Below this scene is a square block or pedestal. On its face are **four red roses** arranged around **two crossed golden staves**. This specific combination of the rose and the staves suggests a **Rosicrucian** influence <term>Rosicrucianism: A secret spiritual movement of the 17th century that sought to reform society through the study of alchemy and "divine" sciences</term>. 

The entire composition is framed by a lush green circular border—resembling a wreath—which holds the elements together in a cycle of eternal life.

# NOBLE PRINCE

# DUKE OF UPPER

*So that I may not be said to have sworn an oath to the words of* Paracelsus <note>original: "in verba solius Paracelsi iurasse." Paracelsus was a controversial 16th-century reformer of medicine and alchemy.</note> *alone—as I might be accused by those who slander the truth, of whom in these times (to my great sorrow) there is an infinite number—I have likewise wished,* most illustrious Prince, *to translate certain writings and collect others from both ancient and modern authors who write in this art. I do this so that the studious* Readers of this Philosophy <term>philosophy: here referring to "Natural Philosophy," the precursor to modern science, specifically alchemy and medicine.</term> *may see through a comparison of one with the other how beautifully these writings correspond to those of the* Paracelsians. *It is as if one and the same image of truth is being depicted, though in different colors; the matter and the form of their work differ not at all. The only distinction is this: that* Paracelsus *has treated these matters more briefly and efficiently than the ancients. Nevertheless, they all agree in this: that they bring to perfection the wonderful, hidden principles of physical things, where indeed the prepara-* <note>The text ends mid-word (præparationem), likely continuing onto the next page regarding the preparation of chemical substances.</note>

<summary>This page is a formal dedication to Richard, Count Palatine of the Rhine. The author argues that Paracelsus's controversial teachings are actually in agreement with ancient wisdom, aiming to validate chemical medicine through historical comparison.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Richard Count Palatine of the Rhine, dedication, natural philosophy, alchemy, ancient authority, medicine</keywords>

### EPISTLE

*tions of artificial works, which have been made to conform to natural things through imitation. Then at last, nothing else is required than the introduction of the most noble natural form and the most divine potentiality into the purest matter. By this, the hidden* power <term>power: "virtus" in Latin, referring to the inherent medicinal or spiritual efficacy of a substance.</term> *in lower things may be manifested by the action of higher things, so that it might exert its power in act upon all other things produced simply by nature, as much by the artifice of nature as of art. For a force that is concentrated and compressed is far more powerful than any force that is scattered,* <note>The author is arguing that concentrated alchemical extracts are more potent than raw natural materials.</note> *and especially when nature, aided by human* ingenuity <term>ingenuity: "ingenium," the innate creative intelligence of the artist or scientist.</term> *and art, works more simply. Those who are skilled in these matters should wonder at nothing more than the blindness in this regard of learned men and doctors (to say nothing for now of the common folk), who do not see those things that are right before their eyes and obvious to their hands. Nevertheless, they presume to judge and discourse much upon things that are as far apart as the whole sky* <note>original: "toto caelo distant." A Latin idiom meaning diametrically opposed or poles apart.</note> *and things that lie hidden under the earth; and though they do not see those things, they presume to assert opinions, even though they do not understand what they clearly see. Thus it happens that they think everything they have not learned is a mere dream, not unlike how the wise men of the world once judged the wisdom of Christ; and now our contemporaries judge the secret power of divine creation. By this alone I wish to strike their* conscience... <note>original: "conscientiam." The text ends mid-sentence, with the catchword "volo" (I wish) appearing at the bottom of the original page to guide the reader to the next.</note>

<summary>This page argues that alchemical art, by concentrating the natural "virtue" or power of substances into pure matter, creates more potent effects than nature alone. The author criticizes traditional academics for their blindness to these observable truths, comparing their skepticism to those who rejected the wisdom of Christ.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, nature vs art, concentrated force, academic criticism, divine creation, potentiality, medicinal power</keywords>

## EPISTLE

*more than they* **wish.** <note>original: "volo." This completes the thought from the previous page that skeptics dismiss alchemical truths simply because they do not wish to understand them.</note> *If the* **power** <term>power: "virtus," referring to the divine potency or efficacy inherent in the natural world.</term> *of God joined to nature is known by those who perceive it, it is not despised, but rather received with reverence and thanksgiving. By others, however, it is neither perceived nor received, as it does not even pertain to them. True chemistry* <note>original: "Chimica vera." Dorn frequently distinguishes his philosophical alchemical science from "vulgar" chemistry or mere gold-making.</note> *is of such a nature that it does not seem probable to those whose intellect is obscured by darkness. But indeed, we do not write for such people, nor do we labor for them, but only for men of a fortunate and sharp intellect. For them, we wished to publish these few words under your patronage, Most Illustrious Prince, for whom we pray for good health of both mind and body. Given at Frankfurt, in the year from the birth of Christ 1583, in the month of February.*

# THE BOOK OF THE MOST NOBLE LORD BERNARD, COUNT OF THE MARCH OF TREVISO.

->*Concerning the most secret chemical work of the Philosophers, to be perfected through nature and art.*<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'Q' featuring a fleur-de-lis motif within a decorative square border.</image-desc>

**IN ORDER TO** lead true seekers of this art away from common errors and onto the true path—lest they squander their resources and suffer the loss of their labor, name, and reputation by relying on the false recipes of books such as those of Geber, Rasis, Albertus Magnus, the *Path*, the *Light*, the *Canon of the Pandects*, Demophon, the *Summa*, and other deceptive men—I shall first recount my own errors, and then how I reached the desired end, and how I have crafted and completed the stone <term>the Stone: The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and prolonging life.</term> four times with these very hands. <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490) was a famous alchemist who claimed to have spent sixty years and a massive fortune pursuing the art before finding success at age 82.</note> Thus, those who read of my process may likewise be able to achieve it through contemplation and the imitation of the works of nature. It would be foolish to judge along with the common crowd that this art is false because of the sophists and impostors we see busying themselves in it daily, or to think that its true authors and sages wished to deceive others in their writings because they themselves were deluded; rather, they wished to leave behind a memory of themselves after death that brought fame, not infamy or reproach. But the ignorant masses possess this foolish quality: that immediately upon the first reading,

<summary>Bernard of Trevisan begins his treatise by warning students against following the confusing or false recipes found in famous alchemical texts, promising to reveal the truth based on his own successes.</summary>
<keywords>Bernard of Trevisan, alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Geber, Rasis, Albertus Magnus, alchemical errors, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory remarks of Bernard of Treviso. The previous page ended with the observation that the ignorant masses possess a specific character trait, which is completed here: they condemn what they do not understand.</meta>

<header>BERNARD TREVISAN ON</header>

this trait: that whatever they do not understand, they condemn. Therefore, anyone who follows the principles of nature through steadfast contemplations will benefit far more than from the vain and dead letter found under countless enigmatic and sophisticated figures. 

However, so that I may gather for you the opinions of honest men who treat this subject sincerely and purely, I wished to write this little book and divide it into four parts: 

The first of these shall tell of the inventors of this art, and especially those who attained it according to their desire, and through whom I myself achieved it. 

In the second part, recounting my own history, I shall tell at length whatever happened to me in my search over time, and all the burdens of my labors from beginning to end, without any envy <note>original: "citra vllam invidiam." In alchemical literature, "envy" refers to the practice of intentionally obscuring secrets to prevent the unworthy from succeeding. Bernard claims he will be uncommonly transparent.</note>. 

The third shall discuss the roots and principles of metals, using the most evident and philosophical demonstrations. 

The fourth indeed will contain the practice itself, presented under a parable that is not so obscure that it cannot be perfectly understood. 

Each part is written in such a way that, unless you as a Reader understand through this book of mine, I would not easily believe that you will ever perceive it through any other. Nor should you think that you can understand it immediately at first glance; barely by the tenth reading—and the more often you repeat it—the better you will be able to uncover the matter day by day. No one should grow weary of the labors or regret them if they believe that by these means intolerable poverty and all diseases of mind and body can be avoided—as I myself have experienced in many who were leprous, epileptic <term>caducis: those suffering from the "falling sickness."</term>, dropsical <term>hydropicis: suffering from edema or fluid retention.</term>, consumptive <term>ethicis: likely referring to "hectic" fever or wasting diseases like tuberculosis.</term>, apoplectic, suffering from colic <term>yliacis: severe abdominal or intestinal pain.</term>, possessed, senseless, raging, and many others. 

In learning any mechanical art or any liberal art, six or seven years must be spent at a minimum, but

<summary>Bernard outlines the four-part structure of his treatise, which includes the history of alchemy, his personal journey, the theory of metals, and the practical "work" hidden in a parable. He also claims the resulting "Stone" has miraculous healing powers for a wide range of physical and mental ailments.</summary>

<keywords>curative powers, four parts of the treatise, alchemical envy, nature vs. sophistry, medical alchemy, disease list</keywords>

It is exceedingly easy; so much so that if I were to describe it in clear words, or demonstrate it before your eyes, you could hardly believe it. The entire difficulty consists in our terminology and our intention <note>Alchemists often used "Cover Names" or Decknamen to hide their meanings. Bernard suggests the "difficulty" is not in the chemistry itself, but in decoding the language of the masters.</note>.

# THE FIRST PART OF THE CHEMISTRY OF
## Lord Bernard, Count of the March of Treviso.
### *Concerning the first inventors*
### *of this art.*

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'A' decorated with scrolling foliage and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>The first restorer of the Alchemical art <term>Artis Chimisticæ: The chemical or alchemical art, specifically the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, after it was forgotten following the Great Flood, was HERMES TRISMEGISTUS, as is read in the commemorative books of the histories of ancient deeds, in the *Imperialis* <note>Likely referring to the *Speculum Historiale* or similar medieval chronicles</note>, and in the commentary on the *Emerald Tablet* <note>The Tabula Smaragdina, a foundation text of Western alchemy</note> by Clavetus. This man was called HERMES or MERCURY because he was the wisest of all in his time. He was called Trismegistus—"Thrice-Great" or "Thrice-Wise"—because he mastered and taught all worldly wisdom, or the threefold natural philosophy: the vegetable, the mineral, and the animal, which are all one stone. He is called the "Father" of this art because of its restoration, as is held in all the books of the *Hermetic Assembly* <note>original: "Hermeticæ Turbæ," referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>, in which he is mentioned even before Pythagoras. Furthermore, as many as shall hold to this art shall be called the "Sons of Hermes." It is read of this man in the Bibles <note>The author likely refers to the *Histories* or apocryphal texts often associated with biblical lore in the Middle Ages</note> that he was the first to enter the Valley of Hebron, and there he found seven stone tablets. Upon these, the seven liberal arts had been carved by Wise Men before the flood of waters...

<summary>Bernard introduces Hermes Trismegistus as the legendary founder of alchemy who rediscovered lost antediluvian knowledge on stone tablets in Hebron. He defines the "Thrice-Great" title as mastery over the three kingdoms of nature—vegetable, mineral, and animal—which comprise the one Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Thrice-Great, Emerald Tablet, Turba Philosophorum, Hebron, Seven Liberal Arts, Alchemy origins, Bernard of Treviso</keywords>

Bern. Trevisanus on 
<page-num>4</page-num>

...only his own principles, lest they pass into oblivion. For at that time, although they were sufficiently instructed to foresee that the world would not perish entirely, but would be preserved <note>This refers to the common medieval belief that the world was cleansed by the Flood but would endure until the final judgment.</note>, they nevertheless did not possess such perfect wisdom as to consider that God, who had given it to them at that time, could have given it to others and elsewhere as often as He wished.

He is said to have lived before the Law and after <note>original: "ante Legem, & pòst" — likely referring to the period before and after the Mosaic Law was given to the Israelites.</note>, at a time when many wise men lived. Aros <note>Aros is a figure frequently mentioned in medieval alchemical texts, often appearing as an interlocutor in dialogues regarding the secrets of the art.</note> relates in a certain letter to King Messohe that this art was revealed by God to several of the children of Israel for the purpose of decorating the Ark of the Lord with gold and perfecting it, as is found in the prophets Ezekiel and Daniel, and also in Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the Jewish historian; alchemists often cited him to claim that the ancient Israelites possessed chemical secrets.</note>. Some [learned this] without manifest revelation or books, but secretly through meditations on the visible works of God in His creatures, just as Phithomea, Rebecca, Solomon, and Philip of Macedon did. 

Yet Hermes preceded all of these in the invention of this art before the Flood, through the tablets said to have been found by him in the valley of Hebron <note>original: "valle Ebron." Hebron is traditionally associated with the burial of Adam and the Patriarchs; alchemical legend claims the "Emerald Tablet" was discovered there in the hands of the corpse of Hermes.</note>, in the place where Adam had been placed after his exile from the Paradise of Eden. From Hermes, it was passed down to many others through a small book which he titled the *Emerald Tablet* <note>The *Tabula Smaragdina*, the most famous and cryptic foundational text of alchemy.</note>, containing these words: 

"It is true, certain, and most true. That which is above has the nature of that which is below, and that which ascends has the nature of that which descends. Join them together by a single path and arrangement. The Sun is the father of this union, and the white Moon its mother; fire follows as the third, acting as the governor. Make the coarse subtle, and render this thick again; in this way, you shall possess the glory of this world." 

In this brief book, the whole work is contained. Many have pursued this art, just as one reads...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the transmission of alchemical knowledge, following the discovery of the Emerald Tablet of Hermes. <page-num>5</page-num>
-> THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY. <-

...[therefore found by] <note>This sentence completes the thought from the previous page regarding the discovery of the Hermetic secrets.</note> King Calib, in the midst of the Bendagid Ternary <note>This refers to a legendary or mystical location often mentioned in alchemical lore as the site where the secret books were found.</note>. Aristotle the Chemist, Plato, and Pythagoras—a disciple of Hermes and author of the Code—[all testify to] this book, which contains the truth of the matter itself without excess or lack, though it is written obscurely enough. In Arabia, King Haly, a most great Astrologer, is said to have possessed knowledge of it; he taught Morienus <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary hermit of Jerusalem who supposedly taught alchemy to the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note>, and he in turn taught another King of Arabia, previously called Calib. Likewise, Aros taught it to his brother Nephandin, and Saturn taught Luncabur and his sister Medera. Their number is infinite, and it is useless to recount them all. There is a huge abundance of books written about this art, but they are composed under metaphorical words and figures so that they cannot easily be understood by anyone except the <term>Sons of the Art</term> <note>original: "filiis artis"—a traditional term for initiated alchemists who have the "eyes to see" the hidden meaning in texts.</note>. For many, reading these books leads them away from the right path into various errors rather than directing them to the goal. Among such authors are Scotus, Arnaldus, Raymundus, Jean de Meung, Hortulanus, Veridicus, and others <note>The author is critiquing famous figures like Michael Scot, Arnald of Villanova, and Ramon Lull, suggesting their writing is too confusing for the uninitiated.</note>. Therefore, so that I may be able to lead students back to the right path, I wished to write this little work for them.

## SECOND PART, IN WHICH are described the great labors of the author, and his maximum expenses, along with individual operations from the beginning until the end, following the truth of his most persistent success.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'C' containing intricate floral and leaf scrollwork within a squared border.</image-desc>

WHEN I first approached this work, there fell into my hands the book of Rhazes <note>Rhazes (Abū Bakr al-Rāzī) was a Persian physician and alchemist; his "Book of Secrets" was a foundational laboratory manual in the West.</note>, in testing which I spent four years of my life and eight hundred crowns <note>A "crown" (coronatus) was a gold coin; the author is emphasizing the massive personal fortune he squandered on failed experiments.</note>. Likewise, I spent more than two [thousand/years] <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to the time or money spent on the works of Geber.</note> on the books of Geber...

<summary>The author traces the legendary lineage of alchemy from Hermes through Arabic masters like Haly and Morienus, before warning that most famous alchemical texts are dangerously misleading. He then begins a personal account of the vast time and money he wasted following the complex recipes of Rhazes and Geber.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bernard of Treviso's autobiographical account of his alchemical journey. On the previous page, he listed various authoritative authors; here, he describes the practical application of their theories, his subsequent failures, and the exhaustion of his personal fortune.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>BERNARD OF TREVISO ON</header>

...[I suffered] a loss of [six] thousand <note>The text begins mid-sentence; the preceding page likely specified the currency, which the author later identifies as "crowns."</note> for a start: many impostors urged and induced me to this, so that they might drain my wealth. Afterward, I looked into the books of Archelaus for three years, in which I worked together with a certain monk, and in the books of Rupescissa <note>Johannes de Rupescissa (c. 1310–1366), a Franciscan friar known for his works on alchemy and quintessence.</note> and Johannes de Sacrobosco <note>Johannes de Sacrobosco (c. 1195–1256), a scholar famous for his works on astronomy and mathematics.</note>, using "waters of life" <note>original: "aquas vitæ"—distilled alcohol or strong solvents used in alchemical preparations.</note> rectified thirty times with their dregs, until it reached such a sharpness that no glass could contain it; in that labor, another three hundred crowns were lost. 

After spending twelve or fifteen years in this manner, and countless gold pieces, without any fruit, and after many experiments with recipes for dissolving and congealing—such as common salt, sal ammoniac, salt of pine, Saracen salt, metallic salt, and other salts—then calcining them <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile substances or to oxidize it.</note> more than a hundred times over the space of two years; likewise with rock alums, ice alum, <term>scagliola</term> <note>original: "scaiolæ"—likely referring to a gypsum-based mineral or a specific crystalline formation.</note>, and feather alum; with marcasites <note>Iron pyrites, often called "fool's gold."</note>, blood, hair, urine, human dung, semen, animals and vegetables, herbs, copperas <note>Green vitriol or ferrous sulfate.</note>, vitriols, <term>inks</term> <note>original: "atramentis"—in this context, black metallic salts or vitriolic substances rather than writing ink.</note>, and eggs. 

I worked through the separation of elements in an <term>athanor</term> <note>A self-feeding furnace used by alchemists to maintain a steady, prolonged heat.</note>, by way of an <term>alembic</term> <note>The cap or head of a distilling apparatus.</note>, a <term>pelican</term> <note>A specialized vessel used for "circulatory" distillation, where the vapors condense and flow back into the original liquid.</note>, by circulation, cooking, reverberation, ascension, descension, fusion, ignition, elementation, rectification, evaporation, conjunction, elevation, and subtilization, through mixing and infinite other regimens of sophistication. I had clung to these for twelve years, and having reached thirty-eight years of age, still insisting on the extraction of mercuries from herbs and animals, I had thus far uselessly squandered—partly through my own folly and partly through the seductions of impostors—about six thousand crowns, so that I had almost lost heart. But in truth...

He laments the loss of 6,000 crowns and fifteen years of his life to these fruitless "sophistications" and the trickery of fraudsters.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bernard of Treviso's autobiographical account of his alchemical failures. After exhausting his efforts with various salts and alums mentioned on the previous page, he now describes his experiments with common salt and metallic solutions involving "strong water" (nitric acid).</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.</header>

Meanwhile, I never forgot to pour out my prayers to God, that He might deign to assist my efforts. Next, I happened upon a certain Governor of our country, who was also an investigator of these secrets. He was attempting to construct the Stone from common salt <note>original: *sal commune* — table salt, which many alchemists believed held a "spirit" capable of transformation.</note>, dissolving it in the open air and congealing it in the heat of the Sun, along with many other processes too long to describe. In this work, a year and a half passed in vain labor alongside others, because we were not working with the true matter. It is sought in vain in that which does not contain what is sought, as is read in the *Assembly of Truth* <note>original: *Codice Turbæ* — referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts.</note>. Since, therefore, common salt certainly did not contain what we desired—even after being subjected to fifteen repetitions of the process—it never showed us any change in its natural properties. Therefore, we abandoned it and attempted it no further.

Moreover, we saw others dissolving the finest silver, copper, and other metals in "very strong water" <term>aqua fortissima</term> <note>Nitric acid; a powerful mineral acid used by alchemists to dissolve and separate metals.</note>. They also dissolved quicksilver <note>original: *argentum viuum* — mercury.</note>, each metal separately in its own vessel with that same water. Finally, after all these solutions had sat undisturbed for twelve whole months, they mixed them all together into a single vessel. They claimed this mixture was the "marriage of spirit and body" <note>An alchemical metaphor describing the union of volatile substances (spirit) with fixed, solid ones (body).</note>. They kept this vessel over hot ashes until a third of the water had evaporated, then exposed the residue to the sun's rays. They believed that small crystalline stones would be produced from it: white, frozen, and meltable stones for the "white work" <note>The stage of the Great Work intended to produce silver.</note>, and red ones from the red metal for the "red tincture" <note>The final stage of the Great Work intended to produce gold.</note>. Out of twenty-two vials half-filled with this liquid, they gave us three. We all awaited the outcome of the birth of these little stones...

<summary>Bernard describes his fruitless eighteen-month experiment with common salt and his observation of others attempting to create the Philosopher's Stone by dissolving metals in nitric acid.</summary>
<keywords>Common Salt, Nitric Acid, Turba Philosophorum, Mercury, Alchemical Marriage, White Work, Red Tincture, Bernard of Treviso</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bernard of Treviso's autobiographical account of his alchemical failures. Having just described experiments with salts and metallic solutions on the previous page, he now recounts his efforts using organic matter and his collaboration with new partners.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>BERNARD OF TREVISO ON</header>

...[solutions] in the bottoms of vessels for five years, but in vain. This was because, as is said in the *Assembly* <note>original: *Turba* — referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, a core alchemical text.</note>, nothing external is needed for that stone, for it is perfected by itself within its own metallic matter. At that time, I had completed the forty-sixth year of my age, when I, together with a doctor and monk named Godefridus Lepore, began work on the Stone according to his premeditated plan. For we knew that any work other than that of the Stone was vain and useless; therefore, we attempted to fabricate it in the following manner.

We took no fewer than two thousand hens' eggs, which we boiled in water until hard and then separated. We burned the shells <note>original: *calcinantes* — calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder.</note> until they reached the highest degree of whiteness. The yolks and the whites, however, we allowed to putrefy separately in horse manure <note>Alchemists used the gentle, steady heat of decomposing manure as a natural incubator for their mixtures.</note>. Afterward, we distilled them thirty times to produce "white water" and "red oil" separately, along with many other things which I will omit for the sake of brevity. 

In this vain work, two and a half years were likewise lost to us without any profit—indeed, at the greatest expense. Consequently, we became so nearly filled with despair that we would have utterly abandoned everything, had we not been supported by a new hope. We began again to learn the sublimations of spirits, the distillations of "strong waters" <term>aquarum fortium</term>, the separations of elements, and the various structures of furnaces and fires, in which we were occupied for eight years.

Then there arrived another man, a learned theologian and Prothonotary of Bergen. With him, we also wished to labor to construct the Stone. By his inclination, we thought to achieve the matter from vitriol <note>original: *vitriolo* — metallic sulfates, often used to create powerful acids.</note> alone. First, we distilled the sharpest vinegar eight times, in which we dissolved the aforementioned calcined vitriol and then drew it off by distillation. Again, it was poured...

<page-num>9</page-num>
->### THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.<-

We distilled what had been poured off fifteen times every day for the space of two months. Because of its most powerful odor, I suffered from a **quartan fever** <note>A fever where paroxysms occur every 72 hours; Bernard likely suffered mercury or sulfur poisoning from the fumes.</note> for fourteen months. We allowed this mixture to rest for an entire year, but we allowed it to sit with no fruit: for it was extraneous matter. It was later reported to us by a certain learned man that the Emperor's Confessor, whose name was **Master Henry**, most certainly possessed and held the mastery of the Stone. Therefore, so that we might gain knowledge of it, we required the mediation of great friends, and moreover an expenditure of more than two hundred crowns, before we could make him a familiar associate.

Now, he worked in this manner: he mixed a paste made from **silver** and **mercury** with **sulfur** and **olive oil**, cooking them together over a slow fire in a most strongly fortified **pelican** <note>original: *pellicano* — a circulatory distillation vessel with two "arms" that feed condensed liquid back into the body, resembling the mythical bird that feeds its young with its own blood.</note>, incorporating the mixture with a wooden spatula. But the matter could never be mixed into a single body, even over the span of two months. 

At last, we placed this matter in another vial, similarly fortified with **lute** <note>original: *luto* — a protective clay or paste used to seal vessels and protect them from breaking under high heat.</note>, and we kept it sealed and entirely buried in hot ashes, and we warmed it with fire all around. We believed that within fifteen or twenty-one days, the mercury would be converted into the finest silver by the power of the sulfurous body. 

When the cooking of the vial was finished, he added lead as he saw fit, and with a most violent fire he melted everything together into a single mass. When this was **refined** <note>original: *fulminata* — literally "struck by lightning," referring to the sudden brightening or "flash" of a metal during cupellation when impurities are finally removed.</note> through a cupel of ash <term>Cupellation: a refining process where ores or alloys are heated in a shallow porous cup (cupel) to separate precious metals like silver from base metals like lead.</term>, we were supposed to find the silver multiplied by a third part. For my part, I had given ten **marks** <note>A "mark" was a traditional unit of weight for gold and silver, roughly equal to 8 ounces.</note> of silver, while others gave thirty-two, from which we expected to receive one hundred and thirty—

...thirty **marks** <note>original: *marcas* — a traditional European unit of weight for precious metals, roughly equivalent to 8 ounces.</note>. But it turned out otherwise, since out of the thirty-two marks, they only recovered twelve from the partner's examination, while I received fourteen marks from my ten. Thus it happened that, while I believed I held the **secret of the stone** <note>original: *lapidis arcanum* — the formula for the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the "Great Work."</note> from the father Confessor, I was made poorer by four hundred **crowns** <note>original: *coronatis* — a gold coin used throughout Europe.</note> once again. Grieving, I abstained from the work for two months, so that I utterly recoiled from it; especially because all my kinsmen did not cease to rebuke and torment me daily. Because of this sorrow, I could scarcely eat or drink, and I was reduced to such thinness that everyone thought I had been infected by some contagious poison.

Nevertheless, I was suddenly afterwards more inflamed with desire than before, a thousand times over, mostly because I was weary of the time that had uselessly slipped away: for I was then in my fifty-eighth year. The sole cause of all my errors (alas!) was this: that wandering astray, I was not working with the suitable material. For, as **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815 AD), the most influential figure in medieval alchemy; Bernard is likely quoting the "Pseudo-Geber," a 13th-century European author writing under that name.</note> himself says:

> Any imperfect bodies, by mixing with bodies simply perfected by nature, are in no way perfected. This is because they have obtained from nature a simple form for themselves alone, in the first degree of perfection; and being as if dead, they can grant nothing of their non-superfluous perfection to others. This is for two reasons: the first, because by the admixture of imperfection they are rendered imperfect, since they have no more perfection than they themselves require; the second, because their principles cannot be mixed in this way **through their smallest parts** <term>per minima — a concept in natural philosophy where substances are mixed at the level of their most basic particles rather than just being placed next to each other.</term>, as is held in the thirteenth book of the **Digests** in the **Pandects** <note>Bernard is citing Justinian's Code of Roman Law, specifically the *Digest* or *Pandects*. Alchemists often cited legal texts to support theories on how substances "marry" or lose their individual identity when combined.</note>, and in the book of **Calib** <note>Likely referring to Khalid ibn Yazid, a semi-legendary Umayyad prince credited with early alchemical writings.</note>; likewise in Geber, and...

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>Bernard describes his despair and physical wasting following a financial loss of 400 crowns. He concludes that his failure stemmed from using the wrong materials, citing Geber's theory that "perfect" metals (like gold) cannot transfer their perfection to "imperfect" ones.</summary>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>ALCHEMY: BOOK I</header>

...in the natural work, according to Master Daalin <note>Likely referring to John Dastin (c. 1293–1386), a famous English alchemist.</note>, and **Arnaldus de Villa Nova** <note>A celebrated 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> in the *Mirror of Alchemy*, in the *Path of the Lost* written by **Plato** <note>In the Middle Ages, many alchemical texts were pseudepigraphically attributed to ancient philosophers like Plato.</note>, in the letter of Euuralius, in the *Great Rosary*, and in **Euclid’s** brief treatise, and the rest of the non-sophistical works in this manner: Common bodies, completed by nature alone in the mines, are **dead**, so that they cannot perfect imperfect things. But if through the art <term>Ars: the "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy itself</term> they are brought to a sevenfold, tenfold, or twelvefold **multiplication** <note>A process in the Great Work where the power of the medicine is increased exponentially.</note> of their perfection, then they color into infinity. For then at last, they become penetrating, entering, and coloring; they are made more-than-perfect and **alive** in respect to common materials. Regarding these things, one also finds in **Rhazes**, **Aristotle the chemist**, Aulphanus in his *Pandects*, and **Daniel** in the fifth chapter of his *Retractions*:

> "Our completed gold," they say, "is more than alive. Likewise, our gold and our white silver—which are one and the same—are not common metals; for these are **alive**, while the others are **dead** and devoid of power,"

as is read in the golden book of the *Codex*, and many others. Certainly, we have seen countless people working with **amalgamations** and **multiplications** toward the **white** and the **red** <note>original: *album & rubeum* — the two main goals of alchemy: the "White Work" (creating silver) and the "Red Work" (creating gold).</note>, using every kind of material that can be imagined, with the greatest labor and such constant perseverance that it could not be greater. Yet we have never yet seen silver enriched by a third part, a half, or even the smallest amount. Nonetheless, we have seen infinite **whitenings** <note>original: *dealbationes* — the process of turning a substance white, associated with the lunar stage of the work.</note> and **reddenings** <note>original: *rubificationes* — the process of turning a substance red, associated with the solar or final stage.</note>, and many deceptive recipes <note>original: *sophistationum* — refers to "sophistications," or fraudulent chemical tricks that make a metal look like gold or silver without actually changing its nature.</note> in such various and diverse regions, such as Rome, in Navarre, Scotland, Turkey, Greece, Alexandria, Barbary, Persia, Messina, Rhodes, in France, in Spain...

<sig>B</sig>

<summary>Bernard lists numerous alchemical authorities to argue that "common" metals found in nature are spiritually dead and cannot effect transformation. He contrasts these with the "living" gold of the philosophers and reflects on the widespread but ultimately failed attempts at alchemy he has witnessed across Europe and the Middle East.</summary>

...[Britan]nia <note>The text likely completes the word "Britannia" (Britain) from the previous page.</note>, in the Holy Land, and in neighboring regions, in Italy, in Germany, in England, and through almost the whole world in circuit, nowhere in these places have we yet seen anyone, except those laboring in **sophistical matters** <note>In alchemy, "sophistry" refers to the use of false or deceptive materials—such as organic matter—rather than the "true" metallic root.</note>, herbal materials, animal products, vegetables, plants, mineral stones, salts, alums, **strong waters** <term>Aqua fortis: corrosive mineral acids, usually nitric acid, used to dissolve metals</term> through distillations, separations of the elements, sublimations, calcinations, and the congelations of **quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the common name for mercury</term> by means of herbs, stones, waters, oils, smokes, fires, and very strange vessels; but never have we found them working with the appropriate material substances.

Sometimes we found a few in those regions who held the method and reasoning for making the **stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, but we were least able to enter into their familiar acquaintance. For which reason, by running about here and there, investigating and experimenting, I had already spent **ten thousand and three hundred gold crowns**.

I had sold a certain possession which was worth eight thousand florins of German money, so that I fell into the greatest contempt among all my relatives, because I was reduced to poverty, such that little money now remained to me, being then sixty-two years of age and beyond.

Nevertheless, on account of such great adversities, I was quite broken in body, though not so much in spirit that I would desist from my purpose; rather, trusting in the mercy of God, who never fails men of good will and diligence, leaving my homeland—not without the greatest disgrace—I betook myself to **Rhodes** <note>The island of Rhodes was a major Mediterranean crossroads, held at the time by the Knights Hospitaller.</note>, so that as one known to no man, I might seek if by any means I could find anyone who [might console...]

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header># BOOK OF ALCHEMY</header>
<sig>## B 2</sig>

[...reduced to poverty, so that] it might solace the afflictions of my desires. One day I heard of a certain man, very religious and of great name; the rumor was that he possessed the **stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> so greatly sought after. At the greatest expense, I entered into a friendship with him. I had borrowed eight thousand florins from a certain person who knew my parents very well.

The formula of his labor was this: he took **gold and silver** <note>original: "aurum & argentum"</note>, most highly purified and in leaf form, and mixed them with four parts of **sublimated mercury** <term>Sublimated mercury: mercury that has been heated into vapor and then solidified again to "purify" it</term>. These he placed in **horse dung** <note>Horse dung was used in alchemy as a "natural furnace"; as it decomposed, it provided a gentle, steady heat for incubation</note>. When they had remained there for eleven months, he distilled a water from them using a most vehement fire.

The **earth** <note>The solid residue</note> remaining at the bottom of the vessel we **calcined** <term>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder or ash</term> by itself with a violent fire. The water, however, we distilled again six times; with each distillation, we joined the earth that settled at the bottom to the first portion of earth, repeating the distillation so many times until it no longer left any residue. We gradually poured [this water] onto the ground earth, which had been placed in a **urinal** <note>An "urinal" or "urinale" was a common name for a glass flask shaped like a medical vessel</note>. Yet, despite many labors, we tried in vain to imbue the earth with its own water. For we could never mix them, nor could we prevent the water from always floating on top of the earth, even though we kept them in a continuous heat for the space of seven months. 

No union or alteration took place, even when the fire was increased; because of this, we abandoned the **frivolous work** <note>He realized the process was a "sophistry" or a dead end</note>. Through this endeavor, I once again suffered the loss of three years of time and five hundred **crowns** <note>original: "coronatorum"</note>. 

That religious man possessed most excellent chemical books, such as the *Great Rosary* <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note>, *Arnaldus de Villanova* <note>A 13th-century physician and alchemist</note>, and the *Book of the Words of Maria the Prophetess* <note>Maria the Prophetess is a legendary female alchemist from antiquity</note>. In these books, I began to study anew, abstaining from physical work for a space of eight years. Then, for the first time, I recognized through philosophical and evident reasons whatever [was wrong with my previous efforts...]

Here, Bernard reflects on the futility of those efforts and describes his pivot from practical experimentation to a rigorous study of alchemical texts to find the "consensus" of the masters.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>BERNARD TREVISAN ON</header>

[...previously] I had worked [at], were nothing but foolish and empty labors; especially when I considered this saying from the **Book of All Truth** <note>original: "Codicis totius veritatis"</note>:
> Nature, it says, is not improved except in its own nature; nature rejoices in its own nature, nature conquers nature, and nature retains nature. <note>This is a famous alchemical axiom often attributed to the ancient philosopher Pseudo-Democritus; it suggests that alchemy must follow natural laws rather than forcing artificial changes.</note>

At last, having been taught by this book, I escaped free from all my **sophistications** <term>Sophistications: in an alchemical context, this refers to "sophistry" or fraudulent and unnatural chemical shortcuts that do not lead to the true Philosopher's Stone.</term> and erroneous labors. Therefore, I wished to study first, rather than work again at great expense and without fruit. I spent many nights sleepless, constantly arguing with myself and concluding with this thought: "What need is there," I said, "that I should seek this art from men, tormenting myself in vain in this way? If they know the art, they will never reveal it; if they do not, I am dealing with them in vain, and at great cost I attempt to gain their familiarity and friendship."

I considered instead in which places the books most closely agreed; I judged that the truth lay hidden precisely where they shared the same meaning, for truth cannot exist in many different ways, but only in one. In this way, truth itself met me: for in those points where I saw they most agreed as one, that was the very thing I had so anxiously sought. Although one may call it by this name and another by that, it is nonetheless one and the same **substance**; the error is committed only in the diversity of words, and not in the points of agreement.

Therefore, my son, I wished to write this little book for your sake, so that you do not lose heart, nor be deceived as I was most miserably led astray for a long time. Indeed, the safest precaution is to learn from the example of others' dangers. Truly I believe (so may God love me) that those men who write such things **figuratively** <note>The text ends mid-word: "figurative-"</note>

<summary>Bernard of Treviso realizes his previous experiments were "foolish labors" and turns to the study of alchemical texts, concluding that the secret of the Stone lies in the common consensus found among different authors rather than the instructions of living teachers.</summary>
<keywords>Bernard of Treviso, alchemical axiom, Nature, textual analysis, distillation, deception, consensus of masters</keywords>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header># THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY</header>
<sig>B 3</sig>

...ly, or metaphorically, hair, urine, blood, semen, herbs, vegetables, animals, plants, mineral stones—such as salts, alums, **copperas** <term>Copperas: an archaic term for ferrous sulfate, used in making dyes and inks, often mentioned in alchemical recipes.</term>, **metallic salts** <note>original: "atramenta"</note>, **vitriols** <term>Vitriols: a group of sulfate minerals (like green or blue vitriol) that were essential reagents in the alchemist's laboratory.</term>, boraxes, **magnesias** <note>In alchemy, "magnesia" often referred to various minerals like manganese or even a specific stage of the "Stone," rather than just modern magnesium.</note>, and whatever other stones and waters they propose to readers regarding this "water" <note>This refers to the "Permanent Water" or "Mercurial Water," the sought-after universal solvent of alchemy.</note>. I believe (I say) that they have spent very little or nothing on it themselves, nor have they sustained any labor, unless they were moved to do so by mere cruelty. 

For I pity all those who are entangled in this labyrinth, or who might fall into it hereafter, whenever the intolerable punishments of misery and calamity which I have suffered come to my mind. Whoever, therefore, wishes to put their faith in me will do so to his own great advantage; from this I can only gain the labor of instructing others. But he who refuses to believe will at last experience, to his own loss, how great a folly it is to be unmoved by the examples of others, and to refuse to learn. 

Flee the **sophistications** <term>Sophistications: in this context, these are fraudulent chemical "shortcuts" or adulterations that mimic true alchemical results but lack the underlying natural philosophy.</term> of wicked alchemists, and any others who put their trust in them. For if your reading of books has taught you anything good, they will try to take it away by false oaths and assertions, leading you away from the right path. They have nothing with which to excuse their errors at last, saying: "I have done it often," (they say) "but this time I did not have what was necessary for the task; if this and that were added," and so on. 

Unless you flee these impostors and rascals as you would the plague, you will never taste anything good in this art. Before I had completed this work by **experiment** <term>Experiment: derived from the Latin "experientia," this refers to the practical, physical testing of theories in the laboratory.</term>, I had studied the art exactly from books for two years. Nevertheless, when such detestable men and most wicked thieves came to me, they asserted to me with oaths—even perjuries—[that...]

<summary>Bernard of Treviso warns readers against using mundane or "filthy" ingredients like urine or hair, characterizing such recipes as the traps of fraudulent "sophists." He reflects on his own suffering and the two years he spent studying theory before practical success, urging students to avoid impostors.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard of Treviso’s warning against the deceptive practices of "sophistical" alchemists. Having previously mentioned his own past failures and the misery of pursuing false recipes, he now transitions into a stern lecture on why one must abandon common chemical substances in favor of the "wise" way of nature.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

<header>BERNARD OF TREVISO ON</header>

...most manifest errors for true experiments, which nevertheless I once, in my delirium, had experienced at the greatest expense. Nor could I be confirmed in my sound opinions until I had completely abandoned those errors, and turned my whole study toward this matter, following it most vigilantly. 

Whoever desires to learn the true art should spend time with the wise: that is, let him read their books and not those of imposters, even if the wise teach it in obscure words. Nowhere will you find such books written in clear and open language; therefore, one must consider what is possible from the works of nature, by comparing the former to the latter, and vice versa. 

All **sophistications** <term>Sophistications: fraudulent or misleading chemical processes used to mimic precious metals.</term> are to be fled: likewise avoid their sublimations, conjunctions, separations, congelations, preparations, disjunctions, connections, and all other deceptions. Let all those be silent who affirm any other **tincture** <term>Tincture: in alchemy, a substance capable of infusing or "coloring" base metal into gold or silver.</term> than ours, and any other sulfur than our own, which is hidden in **magnesia** <note>original: "magnesia"; in this context, not the modern element magnesium, but a "primitive" mineral or a stage of the alchemical matter.</note>. 

Likewise, [ignore] those who try to extract any other **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: mercury.</term> except from the **Red Servant** <note>original: "servo rubeo"; likely a coded term for cinnabar (mercury sulfide) or a specific stage in the transformation of matter.</note>, and any other water than ours, which is **permanent** <note>original: "permanens"; referring to the "Permanent Water," a universal solvent that does not evaporate.</note> and is joined to nothing except its own nature, and wets nothing else besides that which is of the unity of its own nature. For there is no other vinegar than ours, no other regimen, nor other colors; likewise no other sublimation, solution, congelation, putrefaction, and so on, than ours. 

I advise that you abandon all alums, vitriols, salts, and all **metallic salts** <note>original: "atramenta"</note>, boraxes, **strong waters** <term>Strong waters: "aquas fortes," usually referring to nitric acid or other corrosive mineral acids.</term>, animals, and everything that comes from them—hair, blood, semen, flesh, eggs, stones, and any minerals—as well as metals alone. For although the entrance and our material are from them, as all the **Philosophers** <note>The text breaks here at "Phi-", completed by the catchword below.</note>...

<meta>catchword: sophists,</meta>

<summary>Bernard explains that the true alchemical path is hidden in nature and must be distinguished from the "deceptions" of chemical reagents like acids and animal products. He insists that the true Sulfur and Mercury of the Sages are not common minerals but specialized substances derived from a "Red Servant."</summary>

...of the wise, must be composed of **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: mercury (argentum vivum).</term>, and this is found nowhere else but in metals (as appears from Geber, the *Great Rosary*, the *Codex of the Whole Truth*, Aristotle the Chemist, Plato, Morienus, Haly, Calib, Mary, Avicenna, Constantine, Alexander, Bendegid, Esid, Serapion, Arnold of Villanova, the *Lily* of Sarni, Daniel, the *Breviloquium* of St. Thomas, the *Path* of Albert, the *Abbreviation* of Scot, Seneca's *Letter to Aros* for the King of Arabia, and Hemus, Euclid's seventieth chapter of the *Retractions*, and the Philosopher in the third book of *Meteorology*, by all of whom it is affirmed that metals are nothing other than quicksilver congealed by degrees of **decoction** <term>Decoction: the process of boiling or heating a substance to extract its essence or change its state.</term>). Notwithstanding all these affirmative arguments, [metals] themselves are not **our Stone** <note>original: "lapis noster"; the Philosopher's Stone.</note> while they remain in metallic form: for it is impossible for one and the same matter to have two forms. By what reasoning could they be the Stone—which has a noble and intermediate form between metal and mercury—unless the metal is first "corrupted" <note>In alchemical philosophy, "corruption" is the necessary breakdown of a substance before it can be reborn in a higher form.</note> and that metallic form taken away? For this reason, the Philosopher <note>Aristotle.</note> and Democritus in the third book of the *Physics*, and chapter 3 of the *Meteorology*, say: "Let the alchemists boast as they will, they will never transmute the forms of metals except by the **reduction** <term>Reduction: returning a substance to its most basic, undifferentiated state.</term> of those metals into **prime matter** <term>Prime Matter: (materia prima) the fundamental, formless substance from which all things are made.</term>." All books that treat metallic form hold this same thing. Truly, so that it may be understood what "reduction into prime matter" means, it is especially important to know that prime matter is the very thing into which the specific form is immediately and most closely introduced, just as the prime matter of a human is the seed of both man and woman. Many ignorant people, thinking they understand this reduction, interpret it...

<meta>This page continues the argument of Bernard of Treviso regarding the "proximate" versus "remote" matter of metals. On the previous page, he was discussing why metals in their finished form cannot be the Philosopher's Stone and was beginning to address the misconception that the four elements are the direct starting point for alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

<header>BERNARD OF TREVISO ON</header>

...wrongly to the four elements: for (as they say) all things that have been created certainly consist of these. However, these are the **first matter of the first matter** <note>original: "materia prima primæ materiæ"; Treviso is distinguishing between the fundamental building blocks of the universe (the elements) and the specific starting materials for a particular kingdom of nature (like metals).</note>. That is to say, **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: mercury (argentum vivum).</term> and **sulfur** consist of the four elements, and these two are ultimately the "first matter" of metals. For indeed, the four elements are just as suited to the formation of a donkey or an ox as they are to metals. Nature must first create quicksilver and sulfur so that they can serve as the first matter of metals. For example, a human being is not composed of the four elements while they still exist in their simple, elementary state; rather, they are transmuted by nature into the specific "first matter" of a human <note>Likely referring to seed or biological precursors.</note>. In the same way, nature—by transmuting the four elements into mercury and sulfur—has only then produced the "first matter" of metals. Once nature has worked upon this specific matter, nothing else can result from it except a metallic form. But earlier, when they were still just elements and not yet quicksilver or sulfur, nature could have used those same elements to create an ox, a plant, a human, or anything else entirely different from a metal. From what has been said, it is most clearly evident that the four elements are not the "first matter" of metals, but rather quicksilver and sulfur are. Otherwise, according to the opinion of those others, it would follow that humans, metals, herbs, plants, and irrational animals—in short, everything—would be nothing but one single thing without any difference. Nothing more absurd than this could ever be said. Likewise, it would follow that humans could even be generated from metals, since both equally consist of the elements. It would mean that anything could be made from anything, and that "like" would no longer produce "its like." It would not

->be<-

<summary>Bernard of Treviso argues that while the four elements are the ultimate components of all things, the specific "first matter" of metals is a combination of sulfur and mercury. He asserts that if elements were the direct matter of metals, there would be no distinction between living beings and minerals.</summary>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard of Treviso’s explanation of "first matter." Having established on the previous page that the four elements are too "remote" to be the immediate starting point for metals, he now uses biological analogies to explain how nature focuses matter toward a specific final form.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

->**THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.**<-

...there would be no generation, nor female, and no distinction between things, if in actuality all things were a single substance.

To return to the subject of **first matter** <term>First matter: *materia prima*, the fundamental starting material for a specific process.</term>, we shall use this example: the seed of the man by itself, and the seed of the woman by itself, are not the **first matter of the fetus**. This is because nature can produce something else—a monstrosity, such as a worm-like substance—from them when they are separated in this way. But from the **union of both into one**, so that the power of one receives and embraces the power of the other within itself, then at last nature can produce nothing else from it except the form of an infant; for this is the "end" <note>original: *finis*; in medieval philosophy, this refers to the "final cause" or the intended goal of a process.</note> of that matter, and no other. 

Therefore, nature cannot introduce any other form to the matter except that one toward which it is finally inclined and disposed. Consequently, only the **spermatic union** is the first matter of the individual, or of those things from which the seeds <note>original: *femina*; likely a typographical error in the Latin for *semina* (seeds), as the context discusses the union of male and female reproductive principles.</note> were cut or separated; this matter can receive no other form than that of its own proper species, for which it is prepared. 

Let this be a sufficiently simple example of this "disposition toward an end": If someone hastens on a journey to a certain place, many paths branching off here and there appear before he arrives; but once he has set his step upon the right path and perseveres in it, he will finally reach the place he intended to go. 

In this same way, individual things have their own proper path and matter, through which and from which they are made; for not just anything can be perfected from any matter, nor in just any manner. It is certainly manifest that every thing has a preceding thing similar to itself from which it is naturally produced, so that nothing else can be made from it. For example, in the formation of a horse, a certain "equine nature" is required, which has been transmuted into a spermatic substance from two contrary matters <note>Likely referring to the Aristotelian idea of male and female contributions to generation.</note>...

<sig>B 5</sig>

<summary>Bernard of Treviso argues that nature requires specific "proximate" matter, rather than general elements, to create specific things, using the analogy of human and animal reproduction to show how matter becomes "locked" into a specific path toward a final form.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bernardo Trevisano’s argument that "like produces like." On the previous page, he used the example of human generation to show that matter must be specifically "disposed" toward a certain form. Here, he extends this logic to the divine order of creation and the specific origins of metals.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

<header>BERNARD OF TREVISO ON...</header>

(united, however, under that same equine species): likewise, for a man, nature itself does not fundamentally take up an equine substance. For every single substance has its own proper and principal seed from which it is made, and by its own power it is multiplied within that seed, and not through any other thing or in any other way. This is clear from the Word of God; when He had created man as male and female, He said: "increase and multiply," <note>original: *crescite & multiplicamini*; Genesis 1:28.</note> from your own substance—namely, into that which is similar to yourselves. For He had said earlier to His other creatures: "Let each one bring forth its own fruit and seed within itself, let it be multiplied," etc. <note>A paraphrase of Genesis 1:11-12 and 1:22.</note>

If God had willed for everything to be generated from a single thing, He would not have made so many and such great creatures, and so diverse; nor would there have been a need for the heavens, nor for light. Instead, the four elements would have always brought forth one and the same thing, which would be most absurd. Accordingly, from all things He made diverse things of every kind, so that each might generate its own likeness. Did not the Lord God say to Noah before the flood: "Take with you into the Ark from all animals united pairs, male and female, so that afterwards each might be multiplied in its own species"? <note>A reference to Genesis 7:2-3.</note> And do we not see that it happens in no other way?

It is clear, therefore, from the preceding points that for the generation of anything, its own likeness is necessary, so that by the command of God, the diverse "roots" of things created by Him might be multiplied into diverse forms, each under its own proper substance. This same point is proven by the authorities of the Philosophers: for **Scotus** <note>Likely Michael Scot (c. 1175–c. 1232), a medieval scholar and translator associated with alchemical works.</note> speaks plainly: from coagulated **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: *argentum vivum* (Mercury).</term> and from sulfurous quicksilver, the **first matter** of all metals is made. Likewise, in the ***Turba*** <term>*Turba Philosophorum*: "The Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the earliest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between various sages.</term>, a certain man named **Noscus**, **King of Albania**, speaks in this manner: "From a man, nothing else but a man is made; from a winged creature, nothing but a winged creature; similarly from any beast..."

<page-num>21</page-num>

<header>THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.</header>

...from any animal, nothing is produced except a brute beast: for nature is not improved in another, but only in its own nature. The same is stated by Jean de Meun <note>original: "Io. Mechungo"; Jean de Meun (c. 1240 – c. 1305) was a French author best known for the *Roman de la Rose*, though many alchemical works were later attributed to him.</note> in his *Testament*: 

> Every tree produces its own fruit, as the pear tree produces pears, and the apple tree produces apples: likewise, metal is generated and multiplied from metal, and from nothing else. 

Likewise, Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational authority of medieval alchemy. The "Summa" refers to the *Summa Perfectionis*, a key alchemical textbook.</note> wrote well on this opinion in several places in his *Summa*, (although in almost all his books he is entirely rough and deceptive <note>original: "scabrosus totus & sophisticus"; a common complaint among alchemists was that the authorities intentionally used "sophistry" or confusing language to hide their secrets.</note> regarding his study), saying: 

> We have tested each individual thing most exactly by its own properties, and that by proven reasons; we have never been able to find anything permanent in fire except for the **viscous humidity** <term>viscous humidity: a technical term for the "oily" or "mercurial" moisture that allows metals to be malleable and melt without evaporating like water.</term> alone, the root of all metals. All other humidities easily flee from the fire through evaporation, and through the separation of one element from another, just as water does through fire: one part departs as smoke, another as water, and another as earth remaining at the bottom of the vessel. So it is in all things, since those things which are not well united in their homogeneity are consumed by the slightest fire and are separated from their natural composition. But the **viscous humidity**—that is to say, **mercury**—is never consumed in the fire, nor is it separated from its earth, nor from its other element: for either they all remain together, or they depart together, so that absolutely nothing of their weight is lost. 

By these words, therefore, Geber concludes that for this precious stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> there is need of nothing else than the sole substance of mercury, which has been excellently cleansed by art. This substance is penetrating, tinting, standing firm in battle against the fire, and not permitting itself to be separated into different parts, but always persisting most constantly in the sole substance of its <term>mercuriality</term> <note>original: "mercuriositatis"</note>. Then at last (he says) it is joined to metal...

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 20 regarding the "law of species." Having established that biological life only reproduces within its own kind (humans from humans, horses from horses), the author now applies this principle to alchemy, arguing that metals can only be multiplied or perfected using metallic substances, specifically mercury.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>

<header>BOOK OF ALCHEMY.</header>

[from] any animal nothing but a brute beast: for nature is not improved in another thing, but only within its own nature. The same is said by Jean de Meung <note>original: "Io. Mechungo"; Jean de Meung was a 13th-century French author famous for the *Romance of the Rose* and several alchemical works attributed to him.</note> in his testament: "Any tree produces its own fruit, as the pear tree bears pears, and the apple tree apples: likewise, from metal, metal is generated and multiplied, and from nothing else." Likewise, Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, though the works cited here are likely by "Pseudo-Geber," a 14th-century European alchemist who used the name to gain authority.</note> in his *Summa* <note>The *Summa Perfectionis Magisterii*, one of the most important Western alchemical texts.</note> wrote well in several places regarding this opinion (although in nearly all his books he is, regarding the study of this art, entirely rough and deceptive), saying: "We have tested each thing most exactly by its own means, and by proven reasoning, we have never been able to find anything that remains permanent in fire, except for the **viscous humidity** <note>original: "viscosam humiditatem"; a technical term in alchemy describing the oily, glue-like moisture that was believed to bind the elements of metals together.</note> alone, which is the root of all metals." 

All other humidities easily flee from the fire through evaporation, and through the separation of one element from another, just as water is affected by fire: one part goes off into smoke, another into water, and another into earth remaining at the bottom of the vessel. So it is in all things, since those things that are not well united in their homogenization <note>The process of making a substance uniform in its composition.</note> are consumed by the smallest fire and separated from their natural composition. 

But the **viscous humidity**—namely, **mercury**—is never consumed in the fire, nor is it separated from its "earth," nor from its other elements: for either all parts remain together, or they depart together, so that absolutely nothing of the weight is lost. With these words, therefore, Geber concludes that for this **precious stone** <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the goal of the alchemical Great Work.</note> there is need of nothing else than the substance of mercury alone, which must be most excellently cleansed by the art. It must be penetrating, tinting, and "standing in battle" <note>An alchemical metaphor for a substance that remains stable and does not vaporize when subjected to the intense heat of a furnace.</note> against the fire, not permitting itself to be separated into diverse parts, but always persisting most constantly in the substance of its mercuriality alone; then at last (he says) it is joined to metal—

<meta>This page continues the argument that metals can only be perfected through their own nature. The previous page discussed how "viscous humidity" (mercury) is the root of all metals. This page provides further evidence from classical alchemical authorities—Aros, Morienus, and Arnaldus de Villa Nova—to prove that the "Great Work" depends entirely on the manipulation of mercury rather than the four basic elements.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>

<header>BERNARD TREVISAN ON</header>

<meta>The catchword "eius-" from the previous page combined with the opening "lis" suggests the completion of a word like "naturalis" or "radicalis," likely referring to the "radical humidity" mentioned on page 21.</meta>

[radica]l in its depths, by destroying their form and introducing another according to the power and color of the elixir, or the tinting medicine.

Likewise, that great and most learned King Aros <note>original: "Aros ille Rex"; Aros is a recurring figure in alchemical texts, often associated with ancient Egyptian or Arabic wisdom.</note> says: "Our medicine is made from two things of one essence, namely from the mercurial union of a fixed and a non-fixed nature, of a spiritual and a corporal nature, of a cold and moist, and a hot and dry nature; and it can in no way be made from anything else." For indeed, the industry of art brings in nothing new, nor does it add to nature at its root; rather, nature is assisted by art, as is fitting, by loosening its bonds. Likewise, art is assisted by nature by learning from its operations, and in the supply of matter, both together fulfill the desire according to the wish of the diligent worker.

To this Morienus <note>Morienus Romanus was a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist who allegedly taught the secret of the Stone to Prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> says: "Mix and project the medicine upon bodies diminished in perfection, or imperfect ones." And he says this is nothing else but **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: Latin *argentum vivum*, the liquid metal mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals.</term> exalted by art over imperfect quicksilver. From these things they openly teach that it is nothing other than quicksilver.

Arnaldus de Villa Nova <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311) was a renowned Catalan physician and scholar to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note> adds: "Let your whole study be directed toward digesting and cooking the mercurial substance, and according to its dignity it will return worthy bodies, which are nothing other than a decocted mercurial substance."

It could be proven by infinite reasons that double mercury <note>The "double mercury" refers to the union of the "fixed" (solid/body) and "volatile" (spirit/vapor) aspects of the substance.</note> is the sole primary and proximate matter of all metals, and not the **four elements** <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note>, which are most remote. Someone wishing to protect their own error might say: "From your own words," he says, "I bring a case against you: the four elements are afterwards reduced by art into mercury and sulfur, which are the primary matter of metals; in this way they are made more subtle..."

<summary>Trevisan cites various alchemical authorities to argue that mercury is the sole "proximate matter" of metals. He emphasizes that alchemy does not create new nature but assists existing nature by refining mercurial substances.</summary>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the limitations of "Art" (human skill) versus "Nature." Following the discussion of quicksilver on page 22, the author now addresses the misconception that alchemical substances can be created from scratch without a natural foundation. The text moves from a chemical argument to a philosophical and medical one, citing classical authorities like Hippocrates, Avicenna, and Rhazes.</meta>

# BOOK OF ALCHEMY.

with that kind of separation preceding, so that if they had been immediately brought into a mercurial substance and the proximate matter <term>proximate matter: the immediate material foundation from which a thing is generated, as opposed to "remote matter" like the four basic elements</term> without this... To refute this as erroneous and false: from that vain opinion it would easily follow that nature is superfluous if the seeds <note>original: "spermata"; here referring to the creative or reproductive principles of substances</note> of things could be made by art. Thus, a human could be generated without a human, solely from the four elements without any other mediating nature or alteration. In the same way, the principles of compositions would be made [by art alone], which fights against all natural reason. In this art, nature has indeed produced the matter, in which it is afterwards assisted by art. It would also follow from that falsehood that a physician, by his art or with herbs, could resuscitate the dead or heal a dying man against the saying of Avicenna and Rhazes <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina) and Rhazes (Al-Razi) were the two most influential Persian physicians of the medieval world; their works were the foundation of European medical study.</note>. Medicine only assists nature, and when nature is absent or failing, art can perform nothing. For instance, if a laxative is cast into a dead body, it does not purge, because the action is not received by nature. Hippocrates—who was more divine than human, and like a spirit lacking a body—testifies to this in his *Aphorisms* and natural principles, saying:

> Art presupposes some thing created by nature alone, in which it provides help to nature itself, and nature to art. Therefore, it is established that art requires matter that has been created by nature, and not by art; for if it were from art, what need would there be for nature in those things which nature did not work upon, since nothing of her own would be present? Art alone would be able to perfect it without any other external thing and beyond nature.

It is clear from many irrefragable and most evident testimonies that nature, out of herself, procreates and prepares the spermatic natures; finally, art operates within them through conjunction, tending toward the end, in...

<summary>This page argues that alchemy must assist nature rather than attempt to bypass it, using medical analogies to show that art is powerless without a natural foundation. It cites Hippocrates and famous Persian physicians to prove that "Art" cannot create the "seeds" of things from basic elements alone.</summary>

which nature had created. of the elements, in which nothing else appears to occur except for a circulation of naked matter without form, devoid of any innovation, exaltation, or the acquisition of any form or new matter. In confirmation of this matter, a certain man named William of Paris <note>original: "Guilhelmus Parisiensis"; referring to William of Auvergne (c. 1180–1249), a philosopher and Bishop of Paris who wrote on the intersection of theology and natural philosophy</note>, a man learned in this art, speaks most aptly:

It effects only this: it joins both together in the maternal womb. Although art can also assist in the mixing through the fostering of external heat, rest, and the selection of the best and most easily digestible foods—taken with temperance and moderation—in all these things, art does nothing else than merely assist things already created by nature.

> To create seeds is not the work of art; but after they have been created by nature, then at last art—through the natural power alone existing in the seed-like matters already created by nature—
