<meta>This page continues Bernard of Trevisan’s critique of "sophistical" alchemy. Having just quoted William of Paris regarding the natural generation of human life from two seeds, the text now applies this logic to the "reduction" of metals, arguing that simply breaking metals down into elements is a pointless exercise unless new material is introduced to "exalt" the substance.</meta>

->BERNARD OF TREVISAN ON<-

...which nature had created. Even if it were possible for metals to be reduced into the four elements <note>The four elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, which medieval science believed were the building blocks of all matter</note>, it would first be necessary to separate them into mercury and sulfur before they could be separated into the elements. Afterward, those elements would have to be turned back into sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note> so that they might become a proximate metallic nature. 

What foolishness, I ask, would this be? Since all these things are one and the same, and the same substance—which acquires no new nature or matter from such "reductions," but only remains what it was before. Why then do we require these futile labors, if just as much substance was already held when they were in the form of the seed <note>original: "spermatis"</note> of quicksilver and sulfur? After reduction into the four elements, they acquire nothing <unclear>new</unclear>, neither in virtue <note>virtue: in this context, the inherent power or medicinal/transformative potency of a substance</note>, nor in weight, nor in quantity or quality. The reason for this is clear: because no other matter was added to it which would have rendered it more worthy, or which would have been exalted alongside it. Instead, a single, solitary matter was simply moved back and forth without any addition. Therefore, it is just as valid in the form of its own seed as it is in the form of the elements. 

If the same objection is raised against our Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone: the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and acting as an elixir of life</note>—namely, that nothing is acquired for it during its preparation—we deny it. For we "reduce" our substance so that, within that reduction, a conjunction <term>conjunction: the chemical and spiritual joining of two opposite substances, often symbolized as a "marriage"</term> of new matter from one and the same root may occur. Without this reduction, the union cannot happen, and yet an addition of matter truly takes place. 

Of these two matters, one aids the other, so that the resulting substance is far more worthy than they were by themselves, separately, before their union. Therefore, this reduction of ours is necessary, because through it the matters acquire a new form and virtue due to the addition of new matter—which does not happen in those other "reductions" performed by others...

<meta>This page continues Bernard of Trevisan’s argument against alchemists who believe that reducing metals to their elemental components is the key to transmutation. He moves from theoretical critique to a biological analogy, citing the theologian William of Paris to show that human craft (Art) can only facilitate natural processes, not create the "seeds" of life or matter themselves.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY

...of the elements, in which nothing else appears to occur except a leading-around <note>original: "circunductio," referring to a repetitive process that cycles matter without improving it</note> of naked matter without form, apart from any innovation, exaltation, or the acquisition of any new form or matter. From this, it is clear that the "reductions" performed by these men are mere fantasies, vain and erroneous. 

To confirm this matter, a certain man named William of Paris <note>Guillaume d'Auvergne (c. 1180–1249), the Bishop of Paris, whose works on "Natural Magic" were frequently cited by alchemists to explain the boundaries between natural law and human skill</note>, a man learned in this art, speaks most aptly: "In the creation of man," he says, "first two seeds <note>original: "spermata"</note> come together in union, differing in their qualities. Of these qualities, in one seed there is one that is cold and moist, and in the other, there is another that is hot and dry <note>These refer to the four primary qualities of Aristotelian physics which determined the "temperament" of all living things</note>. These are digested in the mother's womb by the mixing of the power of both seeds, and by an increase through the moisture of the blood—which is of the same substance as the female seed. Through the intervening action of the power of the male seed, the material of this mixture is nourished until it becomes an intermediate substance sharing in the nature of both, without any loss or excess. 

Therefore, seeds are created by nature and not by art <note>Art: In this context, "Art" refers to human technology, craft, or the practice of alchemy, as opposed to "Nature," which is the work of God</note>. Art could in no way create them; it only brings both together in the mother's womb. Although art can also assist in this mixing through the application of external heat, rest, and the selection of the best and easily digestible foods—taken with temperance and moderation—in all these things, it does nothing more than assist things already created by nature." 

The same author adds: "To create seeds is not the work of art; but after they have been created by nature, then finally art, through the natural power alone existing in the seed-like materials already created by nature..."

<summary>Bernard of Trevisan uses an analogy from human embryology, citing William of Paris, to argue that alchemy cannot create the "seeds" of metals but can only assist natural forces already present in matter. He emphasizes that "Art" (human skill) is a helper to Nature, not its creator.</summary>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard of Trevisan’s philosophical argument regarding the relationship between Nature and human Art (Alchemical skill). Following his citation of William of Paris, he emphasizes that the alchemist cannot create the "seeds" of metals from scratch but must work with materials already provided by nature.</meta>

->BERNARD TREVISAN ON<-

By joining them together, acting as a **servant of nature** <note>original: "naturæ minister"; a core alchemical belief that the artist only assists or accelerates natural processes rather than overriding them</note>, the alchemist aids the thing that already exists, though it is not yet perfected. However, he adds nothing to its form, its matter, nor its power. Therefore, the first cause of generation is natural, and art <note>Art, in this context, refers to human craft, technology, and the alchemical process</note> is only the secondary cause, which accomplishes nothing without the first. For it is in vain that the earth is cultivated for a harvest unless there is natural matter created by nature—namely, the grain of wheat. It is clear, then, that each is helped by the other: nature by art, and art by nature; and in turn, both work together toward the completion of the work, whether it belongs to nature or to art.

Since **seeds** <note>original: "spermata," here meaning the generative "starting materials" or "essences" of metals</note> can in no way be created by art, nor can the primary matter of metals, it would be impossible for a human being by any craft to reduce the four elements into a generative form—whether reductive, alterative, or attractive—for the purpose of receiving a form, or toward a specific action or disposition. 

But if someone should argue that the Philosophers say the four elements must exist in our art, their meaning is this: that in their two seeds, the four primary qualities of the elements are contained. Specifically, **heat and dryness**—that is, fire and air—are found in **mature quicksilver** <note>original: "argento viuo maturo," referring to Mercury that has been "fixed" or purified so it can act as the "masculine" principle</note>, which is the masculine seed. **Coldness and moisture** are found in **raw quicksilver**, which is imperfect as far as the final goal is concerned; these qualities represent earth and water in the female seed. 

This does not mean that the four elemental parts are actually separated from one another in these seeds, as are the four elements we see around us; for if they were, they would no longer be the <term>First Matter</term> <note>The "Materia Prima," the formless, fundamental substance from which all things are made</note> of metals. Nor could they be transformed by any human wit or skill into the form of the two seeds of the first matter, as **Khalid the Philosopher** <note>original: "Calib Philosophus," referring to Khalid ibn Yazid (d. c. 704), an Umayyad prince and one of the earliest legendary figures in the history of alchemy</note> clearly teaches.

<meta>catchword: sopher,</meta>

<summary>Bernard explains that the alchemist is merely a servant to nature who cannot create the "seeds" of metals from scratch. He argues that the four elements exist within the masculine and feminine "seeds" of mercury (as qualities like heat and cold) rather than as separate, raw substances.</summary>

### ALCHEMY BOOK.

...[Alphi]diphus and the King of Albania <note>original: "Albaniæ Rex"</note> [speak] in this manner: Know that from the beginning of our work, we require nothing but two materials; only two are seen, neither are they touched, nor do more than two enter into the beginning, middle, and end of our mastery <term>magisterium: the alchemical process or "Great Work"</term>. In truth, within these two, the four qualities exist potentially: for in the greater seed <note>original: "spermate"; here referring to the generative essence or "starting materials" of the work</note>, as the more noble one, two more noble elements are contained through their qualities: namely fire and air. In the other seed, however—which is raw and imperfect by its nature—the remaining two qualities are found, along with the two less noble elements, which are earth and water. Through Calid <note>original: "Calib"; likely referring to the Umayyad prince and alchemist Khalid ibn Yazid</note> it is clearly enough established that in this art there are only two spermatic materials, sharing one and the same root, substance, and essence—namely, the mercurial. This consists of a single viscous and dry substance, which is joined to nothing in this world except for bodies. Morienus <note>a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist</note> asserts this same thing clearly in his book, saying:

> Make the hard watery, and the soft hard, so that the water may be joined to it; hide, or rather conceal, the fire under or within the cold water. This means: join the masculine seed—which is nothing other than cooked and ripened mercury, containing within itself the element of fire through digestion—with the feminine seed: that is, with the living water.

Likewise, Isudrius speaks to the same effect in the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum", one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts</note>:

> Mingle the water with the fire; this is the spermatic union potentially closest to the noble perfection of the stone, ready to receive it.

And in the *Codex of All Truth*, a certain Philosopher named Artefimalef <note>likely a variant of Artephius, a famous alchemical author</note> says:

> Join the red man to his white woman in a round bed <note>a metaphorical reference to the alchemical flask or vessel</note> surrounded by a fire of husks <note>original: "corticum igne"; a gentle, steady heat produced by burning chaff or bark</note>, and cherish them with continuous heat—

That is to say: in this container, everything is present which is required for the perfection of the work. Then at last that conjunction is called the **primary matter** of the stone, and not otherwise. For it contains within itself the nature of the fixed (because it is made fixed) as well as the spiritual nature and the worthy substance of the most noble stone. In summary, whoever properly understands all the Philosophers sees that they agree perfectly in all things; but those who are not **sons of the art** <term>sons of the art: a traditional term for initiated or true alchemists</term>, but are instead ignorant, wrongly judge that they disagree. After I have spoken more than enough regarding the primary matter of metals, and have proven through the strongest arguments and worthy authorities that they are mercury and sulfur, I shall proceed further for the benefit and utility of my listeners. In this following third part of my book, I shall say what mercury and sulfur are, how metals are generated in the earth, and their differences, citing the authorities of the Philosophers from whom I learned what I know about the stone, God granting grace and understanding.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the creation and categorization of natural things, building on the introduction to the "Third Part" of the treatise. # ALCHEMY BOOK

[He] drew forth the four elements, and from their mixture He created living things and diverse creatures. He willed for some to be **intellectual**, others **sensitive**, others **vegetative**, and others **mineral**. 

**Intellectual** creatures <note>This refers to humans, who possess "intellectus" or the capacity for rational thought.</note> were created from the four elements, but fire and air dominate in them more than the other two. In **sensitive** creatures <note>This refers to animals, who possess "sensus" or the ability to perceive and feel, but not human reason.</note>, however, the fire is diminished so that air dominates as much as fire, as seen in brute animals—horses, donkeys, dogs, birds, and so on. 

**Vegetative** creatures <note>This refers to plants, which possess the "vegetative soul" enabling growth and reproduction.</note> likewise consist of the four elements; in these, air and water dominate. However, the dignity <term>dignity: the prevailing influence or strength of a specific element within a mixture</term> of the air is diminished by the water, and the water is diminished by a certain terrestrial and vaporous substance alone. 

**Mineral** creatures, truly, although they also consist of the four elements, are nevertheless dominated by earth and water; yet the dignity of the water is more earthy than it is aquatic. They exist in diverse forms, but they can **never be multiplied** except through reduction to their "primary matter." This is because the other creatures mentioned previously possess their own **seed** within themselves, in which lies hidden all the power of their multiplication and final composition. 

Metallic matter consists of **mercury alone**—cold, moist, and crude—in which there are also the four elements: namely, the hot, the moist, the cold, and the dry. Of these, two dominate: the cold and the moist. The hot and the dry are subject to these. 

The heat of celestial motion, penetrating around the earth and into its veins, is so mild and tempered that it is scarcely perceptible, yet it is continuous: equal always by night as well as by day. This heat does not come from the Sun (as some foolish people claim), but from the reflection of the **sphere of fire** <note>In medieval cosmology, the "sphere of fire" was the highest layer of the sublunary world, located just below the moon.</note> surrounding the air, and also from the continuous motion of the celestial bodies, [generating] a continuous and slow heat...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of mineral formation. On the previous page, the author began discussing metallic matter and the elements. This page picks up with a critique of traditional theories regarding the source of heat in the earth’s veins.</meta>

### BERNARD TREVISAN ON
### [THE CHEMICAL MIRACLE]

...exciting a power that is barely imaginable. Although the heat of minerals might even be from the Sun (according to the opinion of Raymond Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher whose name was often attributed to alchemical works.</note> and Aristotle), nevertheless that heat would be continuous, because the Sun revolves around the earth incessantly day and night. But the opinion of those men is false, since the Sun is neither hot nor cold, but its motion is naturally hot. Therefore, heat coming from the motion of celestial bodies is constantly present in the veins of the earth: yet it does not heat the minerals (as some speaking foolishly think); for if it heated them even in the slightest, with this tiny amount of active heat, in a space of time less than ten years, mercury would be boiled down <term>decoqueretur: the process of "ripening" or "cooking" a metal through internal heat until it reaches maturity</term> into the perfection of the Sun <note>original: "Solis" — in alchemical texts, the Sun is the standard symbol for Gold.</note>, which is hardly accustomed to happen even in a hundred years. Since the earth is cold and dry, and minerals are also in the center of the earth: before they would have perceived even a tiny bit of heat from the Sun in actuality, we, who are closer to it, would be utterly burned up; for the heat would have to be most intense if, penetrating the earth and water—which are very cold bodies—it still retained its heat when it reached the center, rather than being completely extinguished. But truly, these things ought to be understood more naturally in this way: Since mercury itself is composed of the four elements, once these are heated by common and general causes, a natural heat is excited from its own proper motion through that kind of movement: likewise, the fire and air existing in the mercury are moved and gradually elevated, since they are more noble <term>digniora: "worthier" or more refined; fire and air were considered "higher" elements than the "lower" earth and water</term> elements than the water and earth of the mercury; nevertheless, moisture and coldness dominate. But because heat and dryness are more noble elements, they strive to overcome the other two, namely coldness and moisture...

<summary>The author argues that the heat forming minerals does not come directly from the Sun's rays, which would be too intense for life on the surface, but rather from the internal motion and elemental friction within mercury itself.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Raymond Lully, celestial motion, internal heat, mercury, gold, elemental hierarchy, mineralogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of mineral formation. On the previous page, the author began discussing how celestial motions influence the natural heats within the earth. This page explains the specific process of how mercury transforms into various metals based on the balance of the four qualities: heat, cold, dryness, and moisture.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY
->C 3<-

...[quali]ty in mercury: for celestial motions excite other motions of natural heats, through which the motions of mercury—that is, its qualities—are moved. Afterward, with the long passage of time, the dryness of mercury overcomes one degree of its humidity, and it becomes lead; then it also overcomes another degree, and it becomes tin. Finally, the heat of mercury again overcomes a little bit of the humidity arising from coldness, and it becomes the Moon <note>original: "luna" — the alchemical name for Silver.</note>. With heat dominating even more, it becomes bronze <note>original: "æs"</note> or Venus <note>original: "venus" — the alchemical name for Copper.</note>, and afterward iron and the perfect Sun <note>original: "sol" — the alchemical name for Gold.</note>. In this way, the two qualities which previously succumbed, being overcome by cold and humidity, now overcome the others; heat and dryness dominate, which in their state of excitation are called "sulphur," while the coldness and humidity of the mercury are the mercury itself. 

It is not that this sulphur is something divided or separated from the mercury: rather, it is simply the heat and dryness that [initially] do not dominate the coldness and humidity of the mercury. This sulphur, once it has been digested <term>digestum: a state of being "cooked" or matured by internal heat over a long period</term>, has dominion over the other two mentioned qualities—coldness and humidity—and impresses its own virtues upon them. Through such varieties of degrees of decoctions <term>decoctionum: the process of thickening or ripening a substance through heating, similar to a reduction in cooking</term>, the diversities of metals are created. 

Lead continually flees from fire and flies away <note>A reference to lead's low melting point and its tendency to oxidize or vaporize when heated.</note> for no other reason than because the coldness and humidity of its mercury have not yet been altered by the heat and dryness of its sulphur, which indeed do not dominate in it at all. Otherwise, lead would not be consumed by fire, however violent; rather, its mercury would rejoice in that which is like itself. Likewise, all other metals flee from the fire except for the Sun <note>Gold, which does not tarnish or diminish in the furnace.</note>, because they are still cold and humid, one more than another, according to their greater share of the said qua- <meta>catchword: qua- [qualities]</meta>

<summary>This page describes the alchemical theory of "metallic ripening," where mercury evolves into "higher" metals like silver and gold as internal heat (sulphur) gradually overcomes moisture and cold. The author explains that differences between metals, such as lead's tendency to melt or burn away, are due to the varying degrees of this internal "cooking" process.</summary>

<keywords>mercury, sulphur, lead, tin, silver, copper, gold, decoction, qualities, alchemy, metallic transformation</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 31 regarding the internal qualities of metals. It concludes the explanation of how metals react to fire based on their composition and begins a detailed argument that "Sulphur" is not a separate substance, but rather an internal quality of Mercury itself, citing the authority of Geber.</meta>

# BERNARD OF TREVISO ON [ALCHEMY]

...[sharing in the] qualities, or a lesser participation, they flee (I say) from what is contrary to them, and they cannot endure it. For everything naturally flees from its adversary, but delights in its like. From this it follows that the Sun <note>original: "solem" — the alchemical name for Gold.</note> is nothing other than pure fire in mercury, since it does not withdraw from fire, however intense it may be, while all other metals cannot endure it—some more, some less—according to how much they approach or withdraw from the temperament of fire.

For Sulphur is nothing other than pure fire hidden in mercury, which over a long passage of time is excited in the mines and moved by the motions of the celestial bodies (as I said above); it digests <term>digerit: the process of internal maturation or "cooking" by natural heat</term> the coldness and humidity in the mercury, according to the variety of degrees of decoction <term>decoctionis: the thickening or refining of a substance through heat</term> and alteration into different metallic forms. The first of these is lead (being less hot and humid); the second is tin; the third is silver; the fourth is copper; the fifth is iron; and the sixth is the Sun [Gold], which indeed, in the perfection of its metallic nature, is pure fire digested by the sulphur existing within the mercury. 

From these points it is again clearly evident that sulphur is not something separate or distinct outside the substance of mercury, nor is it common sulphur <note>original: "vulgare sulphur" — the yellow mineral found in nature, as opposed to the philosophical "Sulphur of the Wise."</note>; otherwise, the matter of metals would not be homogeneous (which contradicts the assertions of all the Philosophers). They called these dominant qualities "sulphur" by analogy, because they possess an inflammable substance—being hot and dry—just like the sulphur in which they reside. 

Hence it is most clearly evident that metallic forms are created by nature solely from the pure substance of mercury, and not from anything foreign, as Geber <note>original: "Gebro" — Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) was the most cited authority in medieval alchemy, credited with the Mercury-Sulphur theory.</note> thus attests. "In the depths," he says, "of the nature of mercury, there is sulphur, which is made..."

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.

->G 4<-

...through a long passage of time in the veins of the earth's minerals. Morienus <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist known for his dialogue with King Khalid.</note> and Aros speak more clearly on this matter: "Our sulphur," they say, "is not common <note>original: "vulgare" — referring to the brittle, yellow mineral found in nature.</note> sulphur, but a fixed and non-volatile <term>non volatile: a substance that does not evaporate or transform into gas when heated</term> sulphur, belonging to the nature of mercury and not from any other thing whatsoever." We imitate nature most exactly, which in its mines has no other matter upon which to work except the pure mercurial form, as is also evident through the best reasoning, authorities, and experience. Within this Mercury is a fixed and incombustible sulphur, which perfects our work, without any substance other than the pure mercurial substance. 

Calib, Bendegid, Iesid, and Maria <note>Maria Prophetissima, or Mary the Jewess, one of the most famous early alchemical authorities.</note> speak no differently, but clearly in this manner: Nature procreates its metals from the heat and dryness of mercury overcoming the coldness and humidity through alteration; and they are perfected by no other essence, as is affirmed in all the books of the Philosophers, all of which would be tedious to cite. 

Some wrongly think that some external sulphurous matter intervenes in the procreation of metals; but on the contrary, it is clearly evident that sulphur is included within the mercury when nature works. However, it does not dominate within it except through the motion of heat, by which the aforementioned sulphur is altered, along with the other two qualities of mercury. Thus, in this way, nature—through this sulphur in the veins of the earth—generates the diverse forms of metals according to the diversity of the degrees of alteration. In this, we likewise imitate nature, since we take nothing foreign into our matter; yet in our quicksilver <note>original: "argento viuo"</note> there is a mercurial, incombustible sulphur which, nevertheless, does not yet dominate, but rather the coldness and humidi—

<summary>The author argues that all metals are formed solely from Mercury, which contains an internal "fixed sulphur." He cites various alchemical authorities to dismiss the idea that common mineral sulphur plays a role in the creation of metals.</summary>

# BERNARD OF TREVISAN ON 

...[volatili]ty of the volatile mercury, until by the action of continuous heat persevering upon this our quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum" — literally "living silver," the common name for mercury.</note>, the fixed element, mixed throughout the entire volatile substance, gains dominion. It overcomes the coldness and humidity of the mercury, and finally the heat and dryness of the fixed element—which are its true qualities—begin to dominate the others. According to the stages of this alteration of mercury, various colors arise through its own internal sulphur, in the same way, and no differently, than nature itself does within the mines. 

The first of these colors is the blackness of Saturn <note>Saturn is traditionally associated with lead and the "Nigredo" or blackening stage, the first step in the Great Work.</note>; the second is the whiteness of Jupiter; the third is of the Moon; the fourth of Venus; the fifth of Mars; the sixth of the Sun; and the seventh is brought by us one degree higher than nature. For we complete the process, and we render it "more-than-perfect" <term>more-than-perfect: a state where the substance is so pure it can "tinge" or transform other metals into gold.</term> with a most exalted red or blood-like color. 

Therefore, since it is more-than-perfect, it is able to perfect other things. For if it were not perfected beyond its own simple nature, what need would there be to spend so much time—namely nine and a half months—when we can already obtain such bodies created by nature without such great labors? But it is necessary, as I said before, that the "masculine body" <note>In alchemical gender theory, the "masculine" usually refers to the fixed, active sulphur, while the "feminine" refers to the volatile, passive mercury.</note> be more-than-perfected. This is done through Art imitating Nature, so that by its "more-than-perfection" and abundant radiation, it can perfect other imperfect metals in weight, color, and substance, within their mineral roots and principles. 

Who, then, is so presumptuous as to believe he can perfect it and make it as we desire by using some foreign or external thing <note>original: "extraneo" — Bernard is arguing against adding outside chemicals, insisting the secret is hidden within the mercury itself.</note> in which there is no radical mixture? Indeed, in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba" refers to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of ancient sages.</note>, one named Aristeus, who governed the entire world...

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY

### C 2

...the sixteen <note>The text likely refers to the "Sixteen Sages," a group of legendary authorities often cited in medieval alchemical compendiums.</note> by his wisdom, most excellent after Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure considered the father of alchemy.</note>, of whom it is read in the Chronicles of Solomon that he gathered the disciples of Pythagoras <note>A famous Greek philosopher and mathematician whose secret society and theories on harmony influenced alchemical thought.</note>, and was never accustomed to lie. Wherefore, called "The Truth-Teller" by the Astrologers, he says: **Nature is not improved except within its own nature.** Therefore, our matter cannot be corrected in any other way than in its own proper nature. 

Parmenides <note>An ancient Greek philosopher; here cited as an alchemical authority on the unity of nature.</note> says the same thing, he who first withdrew me from errors and directed me onto the right path. It is certainly evident that the metallic nature is not improved except within its own metallic nature, and not in any other thing whatsoever. Thus, by this way through our art, we may perfect in a few months that which nature herself could scarcely do in a thousand years. For in the mines there is almost no heat; if even a small amount were present, the work of nature would be very swift. 

But in our art, we have a double heat—namely, that of the sulphur and that of the fire—so that one may assist the other. The fire is not (as Constantine <note>Likely Constantine the African, a 11th-century physician and translator.</note> and Empedocles <note>A Greek philosopher who proposed the four elements; his theories were foundational to alchemy.</note> would have it) of the substance of the matter, though it nonetheless increases the work; otherwise it would follow that heat of this kind would penetrate more and more daily, which is erroneous. Truly, fire alone rules the whole art which nature uses; we can bring nothing else to it. 

By a violent fire, however, they are not altered among themselves in turn, nor are they moved. Let there be, therefore, a **digesting fire** <term>digesting fire: a steady, gentle heat, similar to the warmth of an incubator or a stomach, used to slowly mature the alchemical matter.</term>, continuous, not violent, subtle, enclosed, airy, surrounded, altering, and not burning. So may God love me, I have now told you the whole method of constructing the fire; consider, therefore, and ruminate on my words piece by piece. For indeed, fire encompasses the whole art, as is evident from all passages of the *Codex of Truth*. Consider likewise what the *Great Rosary* <note>original: "Rosarius magnus" — a reference to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, one of the most famous illustrated alchemical texts.</note> says in this sen-

<summary>Bernard argues that metals can only be perfected through their own metallic nature and describes the specific, gentle qualities of the "alchemical fire" needed to accelerate nature's slow processes.</summary>

->### OF BERNARD TREVISAN<-

...brings forth this opinion: "Beware," he says, "that you do not hasten your solution <note>original: "solutionem" — the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state in the alchemical vessel</note>, wishing to complete it before the required time: for this haste is a sign of the deprivation of conjunctions <note>Haste prevents the chemical and spiritual marriage of the substances.</note>. Therefore, let your fire be persistent and gentle, in the manner of the degree of nature, mild toward the bodies, digesting, and excluding the cold."

Likewise, <term>Maria Prophetissa</term> <note>Also known as Mary the Jewess, a legendary female alchemist from the 1st–3rd century CE, credited with inventing the bain-marie (water bath).</note> brings this forward: "A vehement fire hinders conjunction, and stains the white into a red the color of wild poppy flowers; which indeed you will be able to imagine for yourself and learn from the work, just as I did. In the heat of dung, I held it in vain, and in a fire of coals without a medium, my matter was sublimated <note>original: "sublimata est" — converted from a solid to a vapor, bypassing the liquid state</note>, not dissolved. But in the fire I have described—vaporous, digesting, continuous, not violent, subtle, circulating, airy, clear, enclosed, non-burning, altering, penetrating, and living—it succeeded well. Now, if you are such a true seeker as you ought to be, you will easily understand by these words what kind of fire it should be." 

In the <term>Turba</term> <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), a widely influential Latin alchemical text written as a debate between ancient Greek sages.</note> it is held that artificial experience will teach you what kind of fire it is. In the <term>Light of Aristotle the Alchemist</term> <note>A medieval work attributed to Aristotle, focusing on the chemical properties of "The Work."</note>: "Mercury," he says, "must be cooked in a triple vessel to evaporate and convert the activity of the fire's dryness into the vaporous humidity of the air surrounding the matter." 

<term>Geber</term> <note>The Latin name for Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath; his works were the bedrock of medieval chemistry.</note> and <term>Seneca</term> <note>Though a Roman Stoic philosopher, Seneca was frequently cited in alchemy for his observations on the natural world and through pseudo-epigraphical texts.</note> affirm this very thing: "Fire," they say, "does not digest our matter, but its altering heat—considered dry through the air, which is the medium by which fire moves—moistens it." Up to now, I have deferred speaking of this: for fire perfects or destroys all things. <term>Aros</term> and <term>Calib</term> <note>Aros and Calib (the latter likely Khalid ibn Yazid) are legendary figures representing the transmission of alchemical secrets from the Arabic to the Latin world.</note> say: "In all our work, mercury and fire shall suffice for you, in the middle and at the end."

It highlights the consensus among diverse figures like Maria Prophetissa and Geber on the delicate balance of the alchemical furnace.</summary>

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.

But in the beginning, the matter does not stand quite so: for it is not our mercury <note>original: "mercurius noster" — a term for the "philosophical mercury," the purified spirit of the matter, rather than common quicksilver</note>; which is very easy to understand. Likewise, <term>Morienus</term> <note>Morienus the Roman, a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist who allegedly taught the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> says: "Know that our <term>Laton</term> <note>original: "Latonem" — an alchemical term for an impure metal or "base" state of the matter, often described as a copper-like alloy that must be cleansed.</note> is red; but it remains useless to us until it becomes white. Know also that warm water penetrates and whitens, just as it is itself, and that a humid and vaporous fire performs all the work."

Again, <term>Bendegid</term>, <term>Jean de Meun</term> <note>original: "Io. Mehungus" — the 13th-century French author of the *Roman de la Rose*, to whom several alchemical treatises were attributed.</note>, and <term>Haly</term> <note>Likely Haly Abenragel or Khalid ibn Yazid, major figures in the transmission of Arabic alchemy to the Latin West.</note> say: "You who spend days and nights searching," they say, "squandering your money, wasting your resources and wealth, and twisting your minds uselessly over the subtleties of books, I wish to warn you out of charity (through compassion), like a father moved toward his son: that you should whiten the red <term>Laton</term>, quenched by white and warm water. For the rest, break so many deceptive books; leave behind those endless regimens and great subtleties. Believe me, so that it may be well with you: for indeed all these works are vain, except for that which I have already told you: and all things that have been written tend toward this single goal." What could ever be said better or more clearly?

The <term>Codex of All Truth</term> also adds to this: "Whiten the red," it says, "and afterward make the red white; in that consists the whole art, its beginning and its end." But I say to you, unless you have made the matter black, you will by no means be able to whiten it; for indeed blackness is the beginning of whiteness, and the end of blackness is the sign of <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: "putrefactionis" — the stage of "rot" where the old form of the matter dies to allow for a new, purified form to emerge.</note>, and of the body now penetrated and of its <term>mortification</term> <note>original: "mortificationis" — the alchemical "death" or chemical breakdown of the substance.</note>. And this, says that wise Roman philosopher Morienus: "Unless it putrefies and turns black, it will not be dissolved; and unless it has been dissolved, from its water..."

### OF BERNARDUS TREVISANUS ON...

...it cannot be penetrated, nor can it be whitened: <note>The text suggests that without the proper "warm water" mentioned on the previous page, the base matter remains impenetrable to transformation.</note> whence no conjunction, no mixing, and consequently no union at all can occur, for this last stage cannot happen without mixing. Likewise, composition must precede alteration. In this way, through these specific degrees, our matter is treated in imitation of nature’s own work, in all things without the subtraction or addition of anything else.

Regarding weights, let no one be overly anxious as to what they are or of what kind nature uses; it should be known that they are certainly not found in the places where minerals are mined. For where there is only one thing, what need is there for weights? Do they exist anywhere except between two or more things? Our **weight**, however, is in respect to the <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle, while mercury represents the passive, feminine, and fluid principle.</note> existing within the **mercury**. Indeed (as I said before), the element of **fire**, which does not dominate in crude mercury, is this very thing which <term>digests</term> <note>original: "digerit" — in alchemy, "digestion" refers to the application of gentle, consistent heat to mature or ripen the substance, similar to biological digestion.</note> the matter. Therefore, everyone who is a true **Philosopher** <note>In this context, a "Philosopher" specifically means an alchemist or a seeker of the "Philosopher's Stone."</note> will know best how much subtler the element of fire is than the other elements, and by what proportion it is able to overcome them.

The weight, therefore, is in the first elemental composition of mercury, and not in any other work. Hence, **composition** must occur in the first place, or conjunction; afterward **alteration**; then **mixing**; and finally, at last, it brings about **union**. Whoever wishes to imitate nature in these things should observe only its order and weights, just as the *Book of All Truth* maintains. If the preparation is made without weight, the work will be delayed, which will cause you to easily lose heart. Regarding this opinion, <term>Albugazal</term> <note>Likely a reference to Al-Ghazali (1058–1111), the Persian theologian. Alchemical texts often pseudepigraphally attributed works to him and claimed he was a "teacher of Plato" to lend ancient authority to the text.</note>, the teacher of Plato, speaks most excellently:

> Earthly power acting upon that which resists it, delayed in proportion to that resistance, is the action of the agent in this matter.

These are truly golden words, concerning the weights...

<summary>Bernardus explains that alchemical "weight" refers to the internal balance of sulfur (fire) and mercury, and outlines the four necessary stages of transformation: composition, alteration, mixing, and union.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, philosophical mercury, four stages of alchemy, Al-Ghazali, alchemical weight, digestion, element of fire</keywords>

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY. PAGE 39

...opening the foundations to us, which you will not easily understand unless you are learned, or they have been explained by someone remarkably learned. I would have willingly explained these things to you myself, had I not made a vow to God, to justice, and to the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical literature, "Philosophers" refers specifically to the "Sons of Art" or true alchemists who have mastered the secret of the Stone.</note> that I would never explain in naked words <note>original: "nudis verbis" — meaning plain, literal language without the protection of allegory.</note>, or in common speech, the weight, the matter, nor the colors; but only through true and perfect parables (as you will soon hear below), without omission or excess, in imitation of the wise.

I spoke before about the inventors of this art, and those who possessed it; I have also confessed that I myself possessed and perfected the **stone** <term>stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone (lapis philosophorum), the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting longevity.</note> from its beginning even to the end. Likewise, I spoke of the deceptions I endured, and because of them, the vast expenses and intolerable labors I suffered from the eighteenth year of my age until my sixty-fourth year, before I finally attained the art. 

Truly, if I had possessed from the beginning all the books which I later obtained, I would undoubtedly have reached it much sooner. But I read nothing but the falsest recipes and erroneous books; furthermore, it was my lot to communicate only with the most perverse thieves, worthless men, and impostors. 

But after I gained true knowledge, I had an intimate acquaintance with fifteen good men who likewise knew the craftsmanship of the stone, and especially with a certain **Barbarinus**. (I had nonetheless known the art for two years before I had fully worked it out or perfected it). This man, during a dispute—when I had unexpectedly mentioned that I had not yet tested the process—tried to turn me away from my true purpose; for this reason, I left his company. We were disputing primarily about the method of concealing and covering the art from unworthy men. But regarding the structure of the fire, I wri... <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("scri..."), likely leading into a description of the furnace or "athanor" on the following page.</note>

...[ha]bentum <note>The OCR begins with a fragment of a word continued from the previous page, likely "habentum" meaning "having" or "possessing."</note> while others disagreed with one another, even though they all aimed for the same goal: namely, to proceed in such a way that the "fleeing one" <note>original: "fugiens" — a common alchemical term for a volatile substance, often symbolized by a bird or Mercury.</note> does not fly away before its "pursuer" <note>original: "persequens" — the fixing substance that prevents the volatile element from evaporating.</note> catches it, regardless of how the fire is prepared. 

Therefore, so that you may understand this conclusion of mine, listen most attentively. Our work is created from a single root and from two mercurial substances—raw, taken and extracted from the mine, pure and clean—joined together by the "fire of friendship" as the material itself requires. These are cooked steadily until one is made from the two; in this single thing, the body has become spirit and the spirit has become body through their mingling.

Afterward, the fire should be increased until the fixed body tints <note>original: "tingat" — the process of "tincture" where the Stone imparts its quality to a base metal.</note> the non-fixed body with its own color and nature. For when it is rightly mixed, it overcomes all things and leads them to its own nature and virtue. Finally, it surpasses and "tints" a thousand times a thousand, and ten thousand times ten thousand <note>This refers to the "projection" ratio, where a tiny amount of the Stone can transform a massive amount of base metal into gold.</note>. He who has seen this likewise believes it. In this manner, it is multiplied both in power and in quantity, as the venerable <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>The famous Greek philosopher, often claimed by alchemists as a foundational authority in their lineage.</note>, <term>Isudrius</term>, and the <term>Codex of All Truth</term> testify. 

In no other books have I ever found the secret of "multiplication" <note>The final stage of the alchemy where the potency of the Stone is increased through repeated dissolution and coagulation.</note>, except in the *Great Rosary*, in the *Pandects of Maria* <note>Referring to Maria the Jewess, an early alchemical authority and purported inventor of the bain-marie.</note>, in the *Truth-Teller*, in the *Testament of Pythagoras*, in the *Turba* <note>The "Turba Philosophorum" or Assembly of the Philosophers, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note>, in <term>Morienus</term>, in <term>Avicenna</term>, in <term>Bolzain</term>, in <term>Albugazal</term> brother of <term>Bendagid</term>, and in <term>Yesid of Constantinople</term>. As for other books, if the secret was contained in them, I did not understand it, nor could I learn it from them. 

I knew a certain man from the March of Ancona who knew the Stone perfectly well, yet he was ignorant of its multiplication. This man followed me anxiously for sixteen whole years so that he might learn it; however, he never learned it from me, for he possessed the same books I have just mentioned. Therefore, the entire theoretical study and the mineral principles...

->BOOK OF ALCHEMY.<-

...[I have] revealed [the work] with its reasons, by which the true can be distinguished from the false. Now, indeed, it has seemed right to weave in the practice, though in obscure words, as I myself have labored and four times composed the said Stone <note>original: "lapidem" — the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Whoever, therefore, possesses and reads this little book of mine should deservedly be freed from all anxiety, and moreover know the truth without fail; for I could not have written more clearly unless I were to demonstrate the matter to the eye, which indeed all reason advises against. You yourself, when you know the secret, will veil it far more cautiously than I, and you will take it ill that I have written so openly. The will of God has forbidden that which is good for very few to know to be made public to everyone, as is read everywhere in the *Assembly* <note>original: "Turba" — referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts.</note>.

# FOURTH PART OF THE BOOK OF BERNARD,
## *Count of the Trevisan March, on the*
## *practice of the Philosophical Stone.*

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring elaborate floral scrolls surrounding a central face.</image-desc>
When I perceived that I had made the greatest progress in this art, I began especially to seek out and frequent those learned in it; for it is fitting for proven men to be joined with those like themselves, and not others. When, therefore, I was passing through a certain famous city, Appulea of India <note>While "Appulea" sounds like Apulia in Italy, the author specifies India; in alchemical literature, exotic or distant locations often serve as allegories for specific chemical environments or stages of the work.</note>, I heard that a most learned man resided there in every kind of science, who had no equal in this world. This man had established a prize for debates for all those skilled in the art: a small book with leaves and a cover crafted of pure and unadulterated gold. Therefore, eager for honor, I did not hesitate to take heart and prepare myself for the prescribed debates, [the challenge] adding to my resolve for this undertaken province...

<header>BERNARD TREVISAN ON</header>

...truly a spur to the most learned. It also came into my mind that while the daring are carried to the heights, the timid are cast down to live forever on the ground; thus, I manfully entered the arena <note>original: "palestra" — literally a wrestling school, used here to mean a place of intellectual competition or debate.</note> and, by fate, I obtained the palm of victory in the debate before the bystanders. A little book was honorably offered to me as a prize by the faculty of philosophy, so that I might be respected by all.

Next, for the sake of refreshing my mind, weary from study, I sought out pleasant fields and meadows. I happened upon a most limpid little fountain, enclosed and fortified with very beautiful stone, set upon an oak trunk <note>The "hollow oak" is a common alchemical symbol for the furnace or the vessel that protects the inner transformation.</note>, and otherwise surrounded by a wall, lest brute animals enter it or birds make a bath for themselves in it. Sitting above this fountain, I contemplated its beauty and saw that it was closed at the top. While I was doing this, an old and very venerable man, like a Priest, passed by. I greeted him honorably and asked why this little fountain was closed and fortified in this manner—above, below, and on all sides?

Granting me a friendly response, he said: "You should know, my friend, that this fountain is of a very wonderful and awesome <note>original: "terribilis" — in this context, it means "inspiring awe" or "powerful" rather than "scary."</note> power beyond all other fountains in the whole world. It belongs only to the King of this country, whom the fountain knows very well, and he knows the fountain. It always draws the King toward itself as he passes by, but it is not drawn by the King. In that bath <note>original: "balneo" — a technical alchemical term for the dissolution of a solid into a liquid state.</note> he remains for two hundred eighty-two days, by which he is brought to such youthful strength that he cannot be conquered by anyone, however robust. He therefore took care to have his little fountain enclosed with a round white stone, as you see, in which the fountain shines clear like silver and of a celestial color, so that it might be even stronger, lest it be destroyed <note>original: "destrueretur" — completed from the catchword "strueretur."</note> by horses and other animals."

...was constructed, he brought in an old oak split through the middle, which protects it from the rays of the Sun, making a shadow. Next, as you see, it is surrounded by a very thick wall: first it is closed in with hard and clear stone <note>Likely referring to the glass of the alchemical vessel.</note>, then finally with the hollow oak, especially because it is of an awesome <note>original: "terribilis" — in this context, it implies a powerful or fearsome natural force.</note> nature, so much so that it penetrates all things once ignited and provoked; likewise, if it were to vanish, it would be all over for us.

I asked him afterwards whether he himself had seen the King in the said fountain, who replied that he had indeed seen him entering, but from that time in which he is closed in it by the guards, he does not appear any longer until the hundred and thirtieth day; at last he emerges exceedingly bright and shining. 

The gatekeeper <note>original: "portenarius"</note> diligently heats the bath <note>original: "balneum"</note>, so that it might persist in its heat, which is hidden in the water of the fountain, and he heats it day and night without intermission. 

I asked again what color the King was, who said he was dressed in a golden cloth first from the beginning, then in a silk doublet <note>original: "holosericea diploide" — a "diploid" is a double-layered cloak or tunic.</note>, or a black breastplate, with a shirt indeed whiter than snow, yet his flesh or blood was most red <note>original: "rubicundissimam" — This sequence describes the alchemical stages: Yellowing (Citrininitas), Blackness (Nigredo), Whiteness (Albedo), and Redness (Rubedo).</note>. 

I asked next whether the King, coming there, brought with him a great cohort of strangers and common people? He answered kindly and smiling: "When the King proposed to come here, having left behind all his courtiers and strangers, he enters alone, with no one approaching the fountain except himself and his sole guard; whose care is held by a very simple man—indeed the simplest of men—who could best fulfill his duties, since he does nothing else than heat the fountain." 

I asked again whether the King was a friend to the fountain, and the fountain to him? He said, "They love each other in a marvelous way; the fountain attracts the King, and..."

<header>### BERNARD TREVISAN ON THE CHEMICAL MIRACLE</header>

...[the fountain made] the King, not the King the fountain: for to the King, it is like a mother. I asked finally what nature the King was of? He replied that he was from the fountain, which had made him such as he is, without any other thing whatsoever. I asked whether he had many courtiers; he said he has only six <note>The "six subjects" likely represent the six other metals known to alchemy: Silver, Mercury, Copper, Iron, Tin, and Lead, which seek to become Gold (the King).</note>, who await the succession, so that if by chance the King should die at some time, they might also gain the kingdom just as he did. From this it follows that they serve him, because they hope for great dominion and possession from him. 

Finally, I asked whether he was an old man; he replied that he was older than the fountain and more mature than any of his subjects whatsoever. How is it then (I asked) that they do not kill the King, from whose death they expect such a great inheritance, especially since he is so old? 

He said: Although he is of an advanced age, nonetheless none of his subjects is so enduring of cold and sweat, rain, wind, and labors as he is. Likewise, none of them by themselves, nor all together, could kill him, nor could any other force. How then will they possess the kingdom if he can neither die nor even be killed by anyone? 

He replied: His six subjects are from the fountain, from which they possess everything they have, just as he does; thus it happens that they are drawn from the fountain most of all. The King is killed by the fountain itself just as they are, and he is resurrected through it. Afterward, from the substance of his kingdom—which is divided <note>original: "posita est" — in this context, laid out or distributed.</note> into the smallest parts—each of his subjects receives a portion. And no matter how minute a particle each one has, he is made equal to the King in wealth, strength, and power, and they become equal among themselves <note>This describes the process of transmutation, where the "King" (the Philosopher's Stone or perfected Gold) is dissolved in the "Fountain" (the solvent) to multiply its power and transform base metals into its own substance.</note>. 

I asked again how long they would have to wait? Smiling again, he said: Know that the King enters alone without his subjects; although the fountain loves them as well, they do not enter because...

<summary>The old man explains that the King (Gold) is produced by the fountain (the alchemical solvent) and that through his symbolic death and resurrection within it, his power is distributed to his six subjects (base metals), making them all equal to him.</summary>

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.
## D 2

They have not yet earned this dignity. But when the King enters, he is stripped of his garment woven from leaves of pure gold, which he hands to his first chamberlain, named **Saturn** <note>Saturn represents Lead, traditionally associated with the first, dark stage of the alchemical process.</note>. Saturn keeps it for forty days—or sometimes two days more at most—once he has received it. Next, the King takes off his **black silk breastplate**, which he hands to his second chamberlain named **Jupiter** <note>Jupiter represents Tin; the black garment signifies the *nigredo* or "blackness" stage of decay and transformation.</note>. Jupiter guards it for twenty good days. Then finally, at the King's command, Jupiter hands it to the **Moon** <note>The Moon represents Silver and the *albedo* or "whitening" stage.</note>, who is the third person, beautiful and resplendent; she keeps it for twenty days.

Then at last the King stands in a **pure white shirt**, like snow or the flower of salt. He also takes this off and hands it to **Mars** <note>Mars represents Iron, signaling a transition toward the heat of the final stages.</note>, who keeps it for forty days, sometimes with two additional days approaching. Afterward Mars, by God's command, hands it to the **yellow Sun** <note>The Sun represents Gold and the *rubedo* or "reddening" stage.</note>—not yet clear—who keeps it for forty days. Then finally comes the Sun most beautiful and blood-red, who immediately seizes the shirt.

I asked at last: what becomes of all these things? 

He said: Next the fountain is opened, and just as he gave them the shirt, the garment, and the breastplate, in an instant he now gives his own **bloody and red flesh** to all of them to be eaten <note>This "eating" refers to "projection," where the perfected Philosopher's Stone is added to base metals to transform them into gold.</note>; now at last they have what they desired. 

I asked again whether they always waited for such a long time, and whether they could have any reward for their services before the very end, unless they persevered until then?

He said: As soon as they have the **white shirt**, four of those courtiers could rejoice if they wished, and enjoy very many riches <note>The "white shirt" symbolizes the White Stone, which can transmute metals into Silver, providing "riches" but not the ultimate goal.</note>; however, they would only possess half of the kingdom. For this reason, they prefer to wait just a little longer for the end and the final result.

# BERNARD OF TREVISO ON...
### D 3

...so that they may also be crowned with the royal diadem of their lord.

I asked whether any physician ever approached, or anything else at that time? He replied, none, nor anything else except a single guardian <note>In alchemical allegory, the "guardian" or "custodian" usually refers to the alchemist or the controlled heat of the furnace (the Athanor).</note>, who stirs up a continuous, circulating, and vaporous heat from below, and nothing more. I asked if this guardian suffered much labor? He said, more at the beginning than toward the end, because the fountain is set on fire. I asked whether many had seen this; he replied: "It is turned before the eyes of the whole world, yet no one of those who are in the world has recognized it" <note>This is a common alchemical paradox: the "First Matter" is said to be a common, lowly thing that everyone sees but few value.</note>.

I asked more broadly what they were doing? He said: "If those six <note>The six base metals: Lead, Tin, Silver, Iron, Copper, and Mercury.</note> wish, they can purge the King again in the fountain in three days, by circling the place according to the contained containment <note>original: "iuxta contentam continentiam" – a phrase likely referring to the relationship between the alchemical vessel and the substance within.</note>, giving on the first day the breastplate, on the second the shirt, and on the third his own bloody flesh." To this I said, "To what purpose are these things?" But he replied, "I have answered you enough thus far; I am tired." 

When I realized this, I did not wish to be a nuisance, but I accompanied him a little way as he departed; giving him eternal thanks, I bid him farewell. He was a truly venerable and most wise old man, so much so that the heavens themselves obeyed him, and all things trembled in his presence. 

Then a deep sleep <note>A "philosophical sleep" in these texts often signifies a state of inner revelation or a transition between the stages of the Great Work.</note> came over me, so that returning to the fountain, I intended to rest for a short while; but sitting upon it, I could not refrain from secretly unlocking all the barriers. Meanwhile, as I gazed upon my little book <note>original: "libellum"</note>—the prize of my debate—its splendor and beauty so increased my drowsiness that, with a certain yawn, it slipped from my hands into the fountain. This troubled me greatly, for I wished to keep it in memory of the honor I had won. And when I looked for it, it vanished from my sight; thinking therefore it had sunk to the bottom...

# THE BOOK OF ALCHEMY.
### D 3

...having fallen away from the fountain, I began to draw out the water, yet with diligence, so that there did not remain in it except a tenth part of it with ten parts <note>This likely refers to a specific ratio of "water" (mercury or solvent) to "earth" (solid matter) required for the next stage of the work.</note>. And when I tried to draw it all out, they <note>Possibly referring to the remaining parts or the substances within the water.</note> were very tenacious; while I was being exhausted in this work, some people arrived to prevent me from drawing further. Before I left, however, I closed everything up again, so that no one would perceive that I had drained the fountain, nor have seen it, nor perhaps even steal my little book from me.

Then finally heat began to be stirred up all around in the bath for washing the King. But I, on account of the crime committed <note>The "crime" or "sin" is a common alchemical metaphor for the stage where the material is "punished" or confined in the vessel to undergo transformation.</note>, was led into prison, and detained there for forty days <note>The "forty days" represents the traditional period of *putrefaction* or the "Black Stage" (Nigredo), where the matter must rot in a sealed container.</note>. When these were completed, I was released and returned to the fountain to see it: dark clouds appeared, which lasted for a long time. In short, I saw near the end everything that my soul desired, without much labor; which will not torment you either, provided that having entered the right path, you do not deviate from it to erroneous ones, but have imitated nature in all things.

Concluding, I say to you: whoever reading this little book of mine does not understand the Stone <term>Stone: The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work, capable of transmuting base metals into gold and grant long life.</term> through it, will never understand it, whatever he may perform. For in this parable of mine, the whole work is contained in the practice—the days, the colors, the regimens, the ways, the dispositions, and the continuations. I have narrated each detail as well as could be done, moved by piety, charity, and compassion toward the desolate workers <note>original: "desolatos operatores" – refers to alchemists who have spent their fortunes and lives failing to find the secret.</note> in this most precious art. Making an end, therefore, to my little book, I pray to God the Best and Greatest, by whose grace it was finished, that He may open the minds of men of good will, for whom, as they are ingenious, there will be little difficulty, provided they abstai-

### D

LITTLE TREATISE <note>original: "Opusculum"</note>
D 4

# THE NATURAL PHILOSOPHY
## OF METALS BY MASTER DENIS ZACHAIRE
### a noble French Philosopher,

->To the kind Reader.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'V' featuring ornate floral and scrollwork patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>HOWEVER MANY <note>original: "Votquot," a variant of "quotquot"</note> learned and wise men, and natural philosophers <term>natural philosophers|Early scientists who studied the laws of the physical world, often blending observation with spiritual and alchemical theory</term>, once wrote on this divine art, they forbade the profanation or the public divulging of its secret <term>arcani|the "arcanum," or the hidden core mystery of alchemical transmutation</term>. Nevertheless, friend Reader, when I had sufficiently considered the interpretations of the contradictions, figures, comparisons, double-meanings, and the countless various riddles occurring everywhere in their writings, I did not wish to keep hidden whatever I have resolved. This resolution came after many useless and extremely lengthy labors concerning the recipes of "sophistications" <note>In this context, "sophistications" refers to the work of "sophists"—fraudulent alchemists who used chemical tricks to create false gold or misleading imitations.</note>—or rather, I should say, deceptive and utterly foolish works. Instead, through true insights gained from the books of honest men, I resolved these mysteries by the continuous reading of the most highly approved authors, such as Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational 8th-century figure in alchemy whose works were central to the medieval European tradition.</note> in his *Summa* <note>Referring to the *Summa Perfectionis Magisterii* (The Sum of Perfection), a landmark text of medieval alchemical theory.</note>, and others. Therefore, withdrawing my foot from those erroneous paths in which I was entangled...

<summary>This page serves as the preface to Denis Zachaire’s treatise, where he warns of the confusing nature of alchemical literature and claims to have found the truth through the study of authoritative texts like Geber's Summa.</summary>
<keywords>Denis Zachaire, Natural Philosophy of Metals, Geber, Summa, arcanum, alchemical riddles, sophistications, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the "To the kind Reader" preface begun by Denis Zachaire. After mentioning his studies of authorities like Geber, the author now describes his transition from confusion to the discovery of the "true matter" of alchemy and outlines the three-part structure of the following treatise.</meta>

*...I had wandered* <note>The text continues from the previous page's final word, likely "erraveram" (I had wandered).</note> *before more than **Daedalus** <note>In Greek mythology, Daedalus was the master craftsman who designed the Labyrinth; the author uses this to illustrate his earlier state of alchemical confusion.</note> in his labyrinth, I wished to set myself upon the right path. Walking along it, I finally recognized the true and perfect **matter** <term>matter|In alchemy, the "Prima Materia" or starting substance required to begin the transmutation process.</term>, prepared for us by nature in the caverns of the earth, in order to perfect above the ground those metals which she herself generates naturally beneath it. This is exactly what experience (by the mercy of God) has taught me, as I shall declare in this little book more clearly than has ever before been possible.*

*In the first part of this work, I will narrate the manner and the progress by which I finally arrived at the true knowledge of this divine work. In the second, I will state which authors I used in seeking out the explanations of authorities, of technical terms, and of other difficulties. In the third and last part, indeed, I will declare the practice—but in such a way, surely, that it remains hidden from the ignorant, yet is most manifest to the **sons of art** <term>sons of art|A traditional alchemical term for initiates or those dedicated to the study and practice of the "Great Work."</term>. For their sake, I have endeavored to reduce everything into the best possible order as diligently as I could.*

*In this matter, I have not imitated certain men who are envious of the **public good** <note>original: "R.P.", likely an abbreviation for "Res Publica" (the Common Wealth or Public Good).</note> and far too greedy for their own private advantage. These men refused to reveal their "matter" except under various and diverse allegories and fables; indeed, they were scarcely willing to show their books to others. I once knew a certain man who had received some writings from a **Venetian** <note>original: "Veneto"</note>; he hid these away, keeping them locked up as if he were worshipping them, so that he himself hardly dared to look inside—*

<summary>Denis Zachaire describes his transition from confusion to alchemical clarity, comparing his past errors to Daedalus's labyrinth, and outlines his three-part plan to reveal the secrets of the "true matter" to worthy students.</summary>
<keywords>Daedalus, Prima Materia, sons of art, alchemical secrecy, Denis Zachaire, common wealth, Venice</keywords>

<page-num>51</page-num>
-># TO THE READER.<-
<page-type>preface</page-type>
<sig>D 5</sig>

*...to conceal, let alone show or communicate it to others, perhaps thinking that the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> would emerge from that paper alone without any labor, provided that the **philosophical matter** <term>philosophical matter|The starting substance for the Great Work, often called the Prima Materia.</term> were kept most tightly shut in a chest, as if by a **Hermetic seal** <term>Hermetic seal|An airtight closure; here used metaphorically to describe absolute secrecy or a vacuum-sealed vessel used in alchemy.</term>.*

*But those men are ignorant that such a divine science is not granted to humans by chance, as the Philosophers say when they rebuke those who work at the expense of others rather than their own. By these men, I will no doubt be most harshly criticized because I have published this little work—especially in the **vernacular** <note>original: "idiomate vulgari." While this specific text is in Latin, Zachaire's original work was famously published in French to reach a wider audience of seekers.</note>—since in these times no science is more hated by the common people.*

*To them, I wish to give a brief answer which they have hitherto ignored: namely, that a portion of this divine philosophy is not within human power such that it can be understood by books alone, but is placed in the will of God, who reveals it to whom He wishes through His Holy Spirit, or through the medium of some man, as is told more broadly in the second part of this book. So far is it from being the case that I am making it public to everyone.*

*What is new if I write the art in the common tongue, which has hitherto been described by all its professors in their own language just as much? For instance, by **Hamech** <note>Likely referring to a medieval authority of Hebrew origin.</note> the Hebrew in Hebrew; by **Thebit** <note>Thābit ibn Qurra, a 9th-century mathematician and astronomer.</note> and **Haly** <note>Likely Haly Abenragel or Al-Razi, influential Arabic-speaking scholars.</note>, the **Chaldean** <note>original: "Chaldæis." In this period, "Chaldean" was often used to refer to Syriac or Arabic sources.</note> Philosophers, in Chaldean; by **Homer**, **Democritus**, and **Theophrastus**, the Greeks, in the Greek language; by **Aboholy** <note>Likely Abu Ali ibn Sina, known in the West as Avicenna.</note>, **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most famous figure in Islamic alchemy.</note>, and*

<meta>catchword: &</meta>

<summary>Denis Zachaire defends his decision to publish alchemical secrets in the common tongue, arguing that true understanding is a gift from God and noting that ancient masters always wrote in their own native languages.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic seal, divine revelation, vernacular, Hamech, Thebit, Haly, Homer, Democritus, Theophrastus, Aboholy, Geber, secrecy</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the preface "To the Reader" by Denis Zachaire. Following the previous page where he discussed the necessity of divine grace in understanding alchemy, he now justifies his decision to write his treatise while warning against the common frauds that plague the art.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>->TO THE READER.<-</header>

*Avicenna <note>original: "Avicenna." A Persian polymath and physician (c. 980–1037) whose works on medicine and chemistry were foundational in the Middle Ages.</note> wrote for the Arabs in Arabic. Morienus, Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher to whom many alchemical texts were attributed.</note>, and many other Latin authors handed down the art in Latin, so that it might be known by their successors that this **divine science** <term>divine science|Alchemy was often called a "divine science" because it was believed to require both physical labor and spiritual purity or divine revelation.</term> was at one time granted even to men of their own nation. Regarding popular hatred, it should be known that the truth of the art is not despised, but only the false **sophistications** <term>sophistications|In alchemical literature, this refers to fraudulent practices, "get-rich-quick" schemes, or the use of cheap materials to mimic the appearance of gold.</term> within it, as I will describe more extensively in the first part of this little book.*

*But if they should say, why do I not write each thing clearly and manifestly, so that from this little work anyone might be able to work safely, since by doing otherwise I bring nothing of use to the readers? To answer this: who is ignorant of the innumerable and useless expenses which are made daily regarding this art in France alone, because of deceptions? If readers of my book can be drawn back from these onto the right path, and then pious and honest readers be kept on the path of truth through those things which I teach in the second part, then for all these people the reading of the third part will not be useless, in which it is told how an altogether easy access lies open to the practice of the **divine work**. I call it "divine" for this reason: that no one can comprehend or understand it by themselves without divine inspiration, even if they are otherwise a most learned philosopher, as Geber <note>original: "Geber." Often identified with Jabir ibn Hayyan, the 8th-century "father of chemistry," though this likely refers to "Pseudo-Geber," a 13th-century European author writing under his name.</note> says against all those who strive to work solely by the consideration of causes and natural works, saying: "In this they err greatly, thinking that they [can understand] **nature**..."*

<meta>catchword: nature (original: "turam," likely from "naturam")</meta>

<header>->TO THE READER.<-</header>

...to be able to imitate **nature** <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing the word *naturam* (nature) from the previous page.</note>, since that is impossible for art in all respects. Therefore, let such slanderers abstain from reading our **little work** <term>little work|Original: *opusculum*. A humble term used by authors to describe their treatises.</term>; for I have not labored for them, but for the kind, teachable, and studious **sons of doctrine** <note>A traditional term for initiates or serious students of alchemy who were considered "spiritual descendants" of the great masters.</note> and lovers of this **art** <term>art|In this context, *Ars* specifically refers to Alchemy, the "Art" of transformation.</term>. 

I ask them in a friendly way, and wish them to be advised, that before they begin to work, they should compare all the doubts that arise concerning each necessary operation in the **composition** <term>composition|The specific process of combining and preparing alchemical substances.</term>—including those things which will seem to contradict each other, the ambiguities of riddles, and others of that kind. They should keep their minds so resolved and free that they see and understand that these are nothing but pure agreements: they are not the words of different men, but of things expressed in various ways toward one and the same meaning. 

This is the only way of knowing the truth, especially in this art, as **Rasis** <note>original: "Rasis." Refers to Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 865–925 AD), a Persian physician and alchemist who was one of the most cited authorities in Western alchemy.</note> says. Whoever is lazy or yawning while reading our books will also be unfit for preparing the **materials** <term>materials|The raw substances or "subject" used at the beginning of the alchemical work.</term>, since one passage is clarified by another; what is perhaps required in this one is supplied in another. 

For it would be impossible (by the arrangement of divine counsel) to find everything necessary for this art written out in order. Therefore, I ask all those who, through this little work of mine, shall arrive at such great happiness, that they do not abuse...
