### THE FIRST PART OF THE TREATISE OF DENIS ZACHAIRE
### WHICH TELLS BY WHAT PATH THE AUTHOR CAME TO THE TRUE KNOWLEDGE OF THIS ART AND DIVINE WORK.

<image-desc>A decorative typographical ornament featuring symmetrical leafy scrollwork.</image-desc>

That famous **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the "father" of alchemy. The name means "Hermes the Thrice-Great."</note>, not undeservedly called the first prophet and father of all natural philosophers, the author of the **divine stone** <term>divine stone|The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work, believed to transmute base metals into gold and grant longevity.</term>, and the Thrice-Great Interpreter—after he had experienced the truth of this divine philosophy, he left us in writing that, had he not feared the final universal judgment of God, he would never have written anything about this divine science, so great was the reverence in which he held it. Of this same opinion were all those who imitated him in this **art** <term>art|Alchemy, often called the "Sacred Art" or "Spiritual Art."</term>: which resulted in them all writing books on the subject with various arguments leading toward contradictory conclusions (as **Geber** <note>Refers to "Pseudo-Geber," the anonymous author of the *Summa Perfectionis*, a highly influential 13th-century Latin alchemical text attributed to the Arabic chemist Jabir ibn Hayyan.</note> says in his *Summa*), so that the ignorant might be forced to wander in error in this manner, while on the contrary, the **sons of doctrine** <note>True initiates or serious students of alchemy who possess the spiritual insight to see past the metaphors.</note> might perceive their true intent beneath such a variety of opinions. It was nonetheless necessary for even these students to wander in error from the beginning, so that after they have attained [their goal...]

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>A LITTLE WORK <note>original: "OPVSCVLVM"</note></header>

...[those who have learned] the art through the greatest labors, both of the mind and of the body, value it more highly and keep it more hidden. This is surely the greatest reason for concealment: for in addition to the unbearable and painful labors, there are enormous expenses—especially for those who learn through the reading of books alone, waiting for inspiration from the divine spirit. I did this myself for the space of ten years, as I am about to tell, and I shall describe the means by which I came into the knowledge of this **secret** <term>secret|original: *arcani*. Refers to the "arcanum," the hidden or mysterious knowledge of alchemy.</term>. 

When I was about twenty years old, after I had been instructed at home through the care of my parents in the principles of **Grammar** <note>The foundational study of Latin and classical literature.</note>, I was sent away to study the liberal arts and **philosophy** <note>In this context, natural philosophy, which included the study of the physical world.</note>. In the space of three years, through the particular diligence of my private teacher, I made such progress that it seemed right to my guardians (for in the meantime both of my parents had died) to advance me further, namely to the study of **law** <note>original: *legisprudentiam*</note> under that same teacher. 

But before I departed from the college of arts, I had entered into familiarity and friendship with many other students. These men had various books of **chemical recipes** <note>original: *receptorum chimicorum*. In the 16th century, "chemical" and "alchemical" were often used interchangeably.</note>, which I had transcribed with the greatest diligence when they were lent to me, with the consent of my private teacher, who for some time had also begun to labor in this art. Thus, before I left, I had compiled a very large book filled with such recipes. 

As soon as I arrived with my teacher at the place where I was to devote my efforts to the law, I began to turn over my writings. Some of these contained **projections** <term>projections|The final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the Philosopher’s Stone is "projected" into molten metal to transform it.</term> of one part upon ten, others upon twenty, thirty, a third, or a half part, for the **red stone** <note>original: *rubeum*. The final stage of the alchemical work, the Red Elixir or *Rubedo*, used to create gold.</note> of eighteen carats, twenty, and so on, into gold of the **crowns** <note>original: *coronatorum*. Referencing the *écu au soleil*, a French gold coin.</note>, **ducats** <note>original: *ducatorum*. A gold coin used widely in Europe.</note>, and of a higher co- <note>The word is cut off; likely *coloris* (color or quality).</note>

The Arsenal of Venice was the massive shipyard and armory famous for its intense heat and industrial scale.</note> by the casters of cannons) because, having been already inflamed and burning inwardly, he had drunk cold [liquids]; I grieved most greatly for his death, both for himself and because my parents refused to send any more money than what was required for me alone. I immediately leased these out <note>original: *in arendâ*</note> for a period of four years for the price of four hundred crowns <term>crown|original: *coronatorum*. The *écu au soleil*, a gold coin used in France during the Renaissance.</term>, with which I might finally test the recipe given to me by the Italian.

I supported this man with me until I could see the outcome of the matter, for he affirmed that he had seen its effect through his own experience. For this undertaking, I bought two ounces of gold and one mark of silver; having melted them together, we dissolved them in *aqua fortis* <term>aqua fortis|Literally "strong water"; nitric acid, used by alchemists to separate gold from silver.</term>. Afterward, we calcined the mixture through evaporation, and we also attempted to dissolve it with various other "waters" and various distillations. Three months passed before this powder was ready for projection <term>projection|The final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the "Stone" or powder is cast upon a base metal to achieve transmutation.</term>, which we indeed performed according to the instructions of the recipe, but in vain.

Indeed, our "increase" was transformed into a loss, so much so that from the eight ounces of gold and silver combined, only three ounces remained—not to mention the many other expenses, by which my four hundred crowns were reduced to two hundred and thirty. Of these, another twenty were spent; I gave these to my Italian so that he might go to the author of the recipe, who he claimed was in Milan <note>original: *Mediolani*</note>, from whom he might be better instructed. Consequently, I waited for his return throughout the entire winter; truly, I could have stayed there until my final day, for he was never seen by me again. Summer...

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>DENIS ZACHAIRE</header>

At length, as the plague returned, I was forced to leave that place along with my other companions for half a year. During this time, I happened upon a certain old man commonly called "The Philosopher." I struck up a friendship with him and shared the recipes I had collected, asking him to mark those he judged better by his own estimation. He pointed out ten or twelve which seemed to him the most promising. 

When the plague subsided, I returned to my former place and began to test these right after my return. I worked through the whole subsequent winter until the feast of Saint John <note>original: *feſtum uſque Ioan*. The Feast of St. John the Baptist, celebrated on June 24th.</note>. I gained just as much profit from this as I had from the previous attempts; for indeed, my four hundred crowns had decreased to such an extent that I could count only seventy of them remaining. Yet, I did not lose heart because of this. 

A certain Abbot had a recipe sent from a friend in Rome, from the court of Cardinal Armignac <note>Georges d'Armagnac (1501–1585) was a powerful French prelate and diplomat who lived in Rome for many years; he was a known patron of the sciences and alchemy.</note>. I put forward a hundred crowns, and he contributed the same amount, so that we might experiment at our shared expense. 

Therefore, having built new furnaces of different shapes than the others—since we needed the most excellent *aqua vitae* <term>aqua vitae|Literally "water of life." In this context, it refers to highly concentrated ethanol or "spirit of wine," distilled to a high purity to serve as a solvent.</term> for the dissolution of a mark <term>mark|A traditional unit of weight for gold and silver, roughly equal to 8 ounces or 244 grams.</term> or a half-pound of gold—we bought a large vessel of the finest wine. From this, using a pelican <term>pelican|A circulatory distillation vessel with two side-arms that return the condensed vapors to the body of the flask, named for its resemblance to a pelican pecking its breast.</term>, we prepared a great quantity of this water, frequently rectified <note>To "rectify" a liquid in alchemy is to purify it through repeated distillation.</note>, using many glass vessels bought for this purpose. 

Afterward, we took one mark of our gold—which had first been calcined <term>calcination|The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in a crucible until it is reduced to a fine, crumbly powder.</term> for a full month—and four marks of the said "water." We placed them into two very thick glass retorts, one fitted inside the other and sealed. We placed both of these within two large round furnaces. We even bought charcoal at the price of thirty crowns at a single time, for the purpose of continuing the fire beneath...

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>THE LITTLE WORK.</header>

...to be continued for an entire year, during which time we nevertheless attempted something with other accepted recipes. From these we gained no more utility than we did from the "Great Work" <note>original: *opere magno*. In alchemy, the "Great Work" or *Magnum Opus* is the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>—which is to say, absolutely nothing from either. We would have kept the fire burning for infinite years, and sooner—just as the recipe promised—a congelation would have formed at the bottom of the vessels, but without any dissolution having preceded it. However, we were not working with the proper material, nor was that the true "water of solution" <note>The solvent, likely intended to be *aqua regia* (a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids), which is the only substance that can dissolve gold.</note> which ought to dissolve our gold, as was made clear by experience. For we found the gold powder still intact, only a little finer, which we threw upon quicksilver <term>viuum argentum|Literally "living silver," the standard Latin term for mercury.</term> as was directed in the recipe, but in vain. 

We took this misfortune poorly, and especially the Abbot; for he, like a grand public official, had boasted before his monks that he expected nothing less than the completion of the work, at which time he would finally melt down the leaden fountain standing in the monastery and convert it into gold. But he was forced to reserve this for another time, so that later he might provide for the expenses which he meanwhile incurred through a passing German operator <note>A traveling alchemist or laboratory assistant hired for specific tasks.</note>. He attempted this in vain while I was away in Paris. 

The Abbot, however, having regained his courage, persuaded me that I should gather on my part three hundred or four hundred crowns, and he would add as many to mine. With these in hand, I was to go to Paris, where an infinite number of people were laboring at this art, and I was to mingle among them and various others until I found some perfected work to be shared between us like brothers. Once this agreement was entered into, I again drew up the accounts of my property and possessions, arriving in Paris in the [year] eight- <note>The text cuts off mid-word at *octingentesimo*, likely referring to a date in the 1500s or the number of crowns.</note>

# DENIS ZACHAIRE #

...eight hundred crowns <note>original: *coronatos*. Based on the context of Zachaire's life, he likely started the sentence mentioning the 800 crowns he secured for his journey.</note> I brought, with the intention that I would not depart from there until I had either spent them all or found what I desired. I departed not without the greatest offense to my parents and all my friends, who would have gladly promoted me to the rank of a senator <note>The *ordo senatorius* refers to high judicial or administrative office in the French legal system.</note> in my homeland, thinking me to be a most ready legal expert. 

But I, resisting them and pretending that I would buy some office with this money, said farewell and prepared myself for the journey on the day after Christmas <note>original: *natalis festi*</note>. I arrived in Paris three days after Epiphany <note>original: *Reges* (Kings), referring to the Feast of the Three Kings on January 6th.</note>. There, for a whole month at first, I wanted to stay hidden and unknown. Soon after, I began to associate with artists, goldsmiths, founders <term>fusoribus|Craftsmen who melt and cast metal.</term>, glassmakers, furnace builders, and others; I established such an acquaintance that before the second month had ended, more than a hundred practitioners became my close associates. 

Some worked on the tinctures <note>Substances used to "dye" or transform base metals into gold or silver.</note> of metals through projections <term>proiectiones|The final step of alchemy: casting a small amount of the Philosopher's Stone into molten base metal to trigger transmutation.</term>, others through cementations <term>cimentationes|A method of purifying gold or silver by heating it with powdered substances.</term>, some through dissolutions, and many through the joining of the essence of the stone commonly called emery <note>original: *smirilli*</note>. Some worked through lengthy decoctions <note>Gentle, sustained heating of a substance in a liquid.</note>, others on the extraction of mercuries from metals, and still others on the fixation <term>fixationibus|The alchemical process of making a volatile substance, such as mercury, stable and resistant to fire.</term> of these same things. 

So frequent was our gathering—now in houses, at other times in the great cathedral <note>original: *summo templo*, likely referring to Notre-Dame de Paris, a famous meeting place for 16th-century alchemists.</note>—that even feast days and Sundays were not spared. Some said among themselves: "If only we had the means to repeat our work, we would reach perfection!" Others said: "If our vessel had remained intact, the matter would have succeeded." Still others: "If we had a copper vessel perfectly rounded, we would have rendered mercury fixed with silver <note>original: *luna*. In alchemical symbolism, "Luna" (the Moon) always represents silver, while "Sol" (the Sun) represents gold.</note>." 

In short, not one of them all had performed anything that lacked its own excuses—to which, for a little while longer, I turned a deaf ear, [understanding them to be] useless...

<header>->A LITTLE WORK<-</header>

...for the expenses I had incurred refreshed my memory of such persuasions. Meanwhile, a certain Greek man, reputed for his learning, approached a treasurer known to me, promising him mountains of gold from the fixation <term>fixatio|The alchemical process of making a volatile or "spirituous" substance, like mercury, solid and resistant to fire.</term> of mercury from cinnabar <note>original: *mercurij cinabaris*. Cinnabar is the bright red ore from which mercury is extracted.</note>. This acquaintance provided the opportunity for me, along with a friend, to pay out money for my share to carry out this work.

Since there was a need for pure silver broken into filings, we bought three marks <note>A "mark" was a common unit of weight for precious metals, roughly equivalent to 8 ounces or 244 grams.</note> weight of it; from this, with a certain artificial paste added, our worker formed nails, mixing in powdered cinnabar, which he cooked in a closed earthenware pot at the proper time. Finally, once they were dried, he refined them through a cupel <term>cupellam|A shallow, porous ceramic cup used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead or copper.</term> until they "flashed" <note>The Latin *fulminabat* refers to the "blick" or sudden brightening of the metal bead when the last impurities are absorbed by the cupel, leaving pure silver behind.</note>, so that we found three marks and a little more of pure silver. He indeed claimed that this extra silver had been produced from the cinnabar, and that our original silver had vanished into smoke. God alone could know if there was any profit here, not we, since for my part I had lost, or rather spent in vain, 30 crowns and more.

This rumor of the nails spread throughout the whole city of Paris, so that there was no one who had not heard of them, especially among those accustomed to works of sophistication <term>sophisticationum|The creation of fraudulent alloys or "stretched" metals that mimic the appearance of gold or silver.</term>, just as previously a rumor had been spread about "copper apples" used for fixing mercury with "luna" <note>In the symbolic language of alchemy, *Luna* (the Moon) refers to silver.</note>. At last there arrived a certain nobleman remarkably skilled in these deceptions, even to the point that he made a profit from it by selling his works to goldsmiths. I entered into a friendship with him, though not without spending money, lest he think me a beggar.

I spent a whole year with him before he would reveal even the slightest thing; at last he finally shared his secret <note>original: *arcanum*</note> with me, which, although he valued it highly, was nothing perfect. Nevertheless, toward...

<header>### OF DENIS ZACHAIRE</header>

I wrote to my Abbot of everything that had occurred, and sent him a duplicate of this final work along with the "practice" <note>The practical method or recipe.</note> of the aforementioned nobleman. He wrote back to me, urging me to remain in Paris for another year and telling me not to fear the costs, especially since I had stumbled upon a secret <term>arcanum|A hidden or mystical secret, often referring to an alchemical formula.</term> as a starting point, which he considered to be of no small value. 

However, I had already resolved in my mind not to use any matter <note>original: *materia*</note> that did not remain in the same state in which it had first appeared. My principle was that no one should work or labor merely to become a rogue and an impostor, enriching themselves at the expense of others. 

And so I stayed for another year as he had requested—making three years in total—visiting first one person and then another, specifically those who were rumored to possess something "good and perfect" <note>An alchemical term for successful transmutation or a completed medicine.</note>. I had nearly exhausted all my funds when the Abbot wrote to me once again, ordering me to come to him without delay as soon as I had read his letter. I obeyed, for I did not wish to break the "fraternal faith" <note>The bond of trust or oath between the patron (Abbot) and the alchemist.</note> I had promised him. 

Upon my arrival, he showed me letters from the King of Navarre <note>Likely Henry d'Albret (1503–1555), who was known for his interest in the occult and alchemy.</note>, who seemed to have a great curiosity for such matters. The King had written asking the Abbot to persuade me—if he wished to do the King a favor—to come to the court immediately. I was to teach him the secret of that nobleman, as well as other things the King had heard I knew. In return, he promised a reward of three or four thousand crowns <term>coronatorum|The "écu au soleil" or gold crown, a significant sum of money.</term>. 

This sum so excited the Abbot that he could not have appeared happier even if the money were already tucked away in his own purse. He could not rest until I had set off for the Royal Court <term>Aula|The court or palace of a sovereign.</term>. Once I arrived there, I was unable to begin work for six weeks because the necessary materials had to be sourced from elsewhere. Finally, when the work was completed...

...nothing less than what I expected happened: nothing. For although the King had conceived in his mind to remunerate <note>To pay or reward for services rendered.</note> my labors (to say nothing for now of the favor and friendship shown to me by the inhabitants and courtiers—though by very few indeed), nevertheless, his advisors and the highest nobles—especially those who had been the chief cause of my coming—dissuaded the King from his good opinion of me. I was dismissed by the King empty-handed, with only a word of thanks being given; he promised that if there were any confiscations <note>original: *confiscationum*; the seizing of private property by the state, which the King was offering to Zachaire as a form of debt-collection or payment.</note> in his regions over which he had power, I should seek them out and he would give them to me. 

Troubled by this tedious response, I did not wish to trust such vain promises, which had been very well known to me in the past. I returned to the Abbot, not quite satisfied, but not before I visited in passing a certain religious **most excellent Doctor in philosophy**, of whose singular learning there was no small rumor. This man drew me back greatly from **sophistications** <term>sophistications|In alchemy, this refers to "sophistry"—the use of deceptive, fraudulent, or overly complex chemical methods that mimic true transmutation without achieving it.</term>. 

After he learned that I had devoted effort to **philosophy** to such an extent that I had received the **Master’s degree** (as they call it) <note>original: *magistratus gradum*</note>, as I had told him myself, he said he grieved deeply that I had not possessed any of the best books before I had so uselessly consumed both my time and wealth in such **diabolical sophistications**. I told him of this last work of mine, and he soon judged that it could not withstand many tests. Therefore, he kindly persuaded me that from then on I should entirely abstain from those errors, and read the books of the ancient **Philosophers** more attentively, toward the knowledge of their true matter, in which the most powerful perfection of this science seemed to consist. Accordingly, I hasten...

->DIONYSIUS ZACHAIRE<-

<meta>catchword: lutionem (resolution)</meta>

I took my journey back to the Abbot to give an account of the expenses I had incurred in Paris, and to give him his portion of the reward I had received from the King of Navarre. When I had told him everything, he was saddened, and especially so because I did not wish to persevere in the work <note>That is, the practical chemical experiments they had been funding.</note>; for he believed that I was an industrious operator. Even the kind words he used were not enough to stop me from embracing the Doctor’s advice, because of the clear reasons the Doctor had provided. After settling the accounts <note>original: *Calculo dato*; literally "the pebble being given," referring to the ancient method of counting or calculating a balance.</note> with the Abbot, of our eight hundred shared crowns, only ninety remained. Having said our farewells, we were parted. I returned to my home to collect more money from my tenants <note>original: *arrendatoribus*; those who leased or rented land from him.</note>. Having received these funds, I made my way back to Paris again. I had made a firm resolution that, once I arrived, I would not leave my lodgings until I had achieved an excellent and perfect resolution <note>A clear understanding or "solution" to the alchemical mystery.</note> by reading the various authors of our **natural philosophy**, before I would again set my hand to our **Great Work** <term>Great Work|Also known as the *Magnum Opus*, this is the ultimate goal of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, leaving all **sophistications** <note>Deceptive or fraudulent chemical methods.</note> to their own wicked author.

I arrived in Paris on the day after All Saints' Day <note>November 2nd.</note>, in the year 1546. After purchasing several books for the price of ten crowns—some of them printed and some in manuscript, by both ancient and modern writers, such as the ***Assembly of the Philosophers*** <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*; one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts.</note>, ***Trevisan*** <note>Bernard Trevisan, a famous 15th-century alchemist whom Zachaire deeply admired.</note>, the ***Lament of Nature***, and many other treatises not yet printed—and having rented a room in the **Saint-Marceau suburb** <note>The Faubourg Saint-Marceau, an area of Paris then known for its tanneries and student housing.</note>, I remained there with only my servant boy. I socialized with no one during this time, but studied assiduously day and night for an entire year with the greatest diligence. Scarcely had the first month passed when I began to find one reso-

### OPVSCVLVM.
<note>original: *Opusculum*; literally "A Little Work" or "Small Treatise."</note>

[reso]lution to myself, then soon another, expanding on this one or changing it entirely. I had proposed to do this for as long as it took until I finally stumbled upon one single path in which no variety or contradiction of philosophical opinions could find a place. In these efforts, a year and a good part of another passed by before I could extract any stable knowledge from them. Being placed amid these perplexities, it seemed useful to seek out good practitioners in this divine work, leaving behind the sophists <note>Deceptive or false alchemists who used trickery rather than true natural philosophy.</note>, whom I had realized were "as far as the whole heaven" <note>original: *toto caelo*; a Latin idiom meaning they were completely and utterly in error</note> and leading others astray.

However, the ambiguity that kept me from my studies was now increasing, and even more so while I considered the various and differing methods of working among these men. For if one worked with gold alone, another worked with gold and mercury; another mixed in lead, which he called "sounding" <note>original: *sonans*; likely referring to the sound of the metal or a specific stage of the chemical reaction</note>, because it had passed through a retort <term>retort|A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</term> with quicksilver <term>quicksilver|The common name for liquid mercury.</term>. Another likewise converted metals into quicksilver using various and diverse simple substances through sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance to turn it into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid form.</note>. Yet another worked with "black ink," <note>original: *atramento nigro*</note> which he claimed was the true matter used by Raymond Lull <note>Raymundus Lullius; a 13th-century philosopher and mystic whose name was frequently attached to alchemical texts he did not actually write.</note> in the composition of his Great Stone <term>Great Stone|Another name for the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical quest.</term>.

If one person worked with an alembic <term>alembic|A specialized distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</term>, another would be found using many other different vessels made of glass, some of bronze, copper, lead, or silver, and even gold. Likewise, some performed their decoctions <note>The process of boiling or heating substances to extract their essence.</note> over a fire of large coals, others with wood, some with the refuse of grape clusters, others by the rays of the Sun, and others in a Bain-Marie <term>Bain-Marie|original: *balneo Mariae*; a water bath used to provide gentle, uniform heat.</term>. So great was the variety of such operations, combined with the contradictions in the books, that they reduced me to such a state that I nearly despaired—

# THE OPUSCULUM OF DENIS ZACHAIRE
<note>original: *Dionysii Zacharii*; Denis Zachaire (1510–1556) was a French alchemist who claimed to have successfully created the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

...[lest I should] lose heart, I was lifted up again by the divine Spirit and turned myself once more to the serious reading of books. I re-read more diligently and with greater attention the writings of **Raymond Lull**, <note>A 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and mystic to whom many alchemical works were later attributed.</note> especially in his *Testament* <note>original: *Testamentum*</note> and *Codicil*, <note>original: *Codicillo*</note> which I compared with a certain letter he wrote to **King Robert** <note>original: *Roberto Regi*; referring to Robert of Anjou, King of Naples.</note> when he was near death. I also compared these with a certain writing given to me by a Doctor—which had been useless to him—so that, by concluding against all those operations I had seen before, I might finally resolve upon a single path. I eventually found one that agreed with all the books, as well as with the resolution made by **Arnold of Villanova**, <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> who was Lull’s teacher in this art, near the end of his great *Rosary*. <note>original: *Rosarium*; a title for a collection of alchemical "flowers" or key teachings.</note>

Remaining fixed in this opinion, I did nothing else but assiduely read and meditate upon my resolution day and night, until the term of my allowance <note>original: *census*; his financial support or the time allotted for his studies.</note> arrived for me to return home to begin the work. Once I was home, I proposed to put what I had resolved into effect, having first provided everything necessary. I first constructed a furnace, <term>furnace|furnum; the central tool of the alchemist, designed to provide constant, controlled heat over long periods.</term> and on the day after Easter, I began to work. This was not without many hindrances, of which I shall make no mention here, except for the frequent rebukes from my parents and friends, as well as my neighbors.

Some would say to me, "Hey! What are you turning over in your mind? Have you not already made enough useless expenditures on these trifles, I ask you?" Another would warn me that unless I abstained from handling so much coal, the common people would suspect me of forging false coinage, <note>original: *monetæ falsæ*; a frequent accusation against alchemists, as their laboratories and smoke looked similar to illegal mints.</note> since they had already begun to murmur about it. Yet another would come by and assert that all the citizens, even the most prominent ones, wondered why I did not take up the profession of **Law**... <note>original: *Iurisprudentia*</note>

of prudence, especially since I was a **Licentiate**, <note>A university degree holder, specifically one licensed to teach or practice law, indicating Zachaire was an educated man of some status.</note> until I might reach some higher office of administration in the **State**. <note>original: *Republica*</note> Those who were most closely joined to me, and my familiars, rebuked me daily without ceasing, saying: "When at last will you put an end to these follies, and to the wasting of your father’s estate?" "If you have no respect for us," they said, "nor for your other friends, at least have some regard for yourself; consider that although you are thirty years old, or thereabouts, you seem to be acting fifty; your beard is thus turning almost white from excessive labors, all for the sake of some vain opinion you have endured since your youth."

Consider whether these words stung me most bitterly: especially when I saw my work succeeding more prosperously every day, upon which I persisted most tenaciously and constantly without weariness, despite these and other intervening impediments—and especially the **plague**, which raged throughout the entire summer, so that all business dealings ceased. Indeed, every single day I beheld the appearances of the **three colors**, <note>In alchemy, these are typically *Nigredo* (black), *Albedo* (white), and *Rubedo* (red), signaling the stages of the "Great Work."</note> which the **Philosophers** <note>Meaning the ancient and medieval alchemical authorities.</note> write must appear before the perfection of the **divine work**. <note>original: *operis diuini*; the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> By the grace of God, I saw them all successively, one after another; and finally, on **Easter Day** itself of the following year, I saw the perfection. I made a trial upon **quicksilver** <note>original: *viuum argentum*; mercury.</note> heated in a **crucible**, <note>original: *tigillo*; a ceramic or metal container used for high-temperature melting.</note> and it was converted into...

# DIONYSIUS ZACHAIRE

...pure gold before my eyes, in a shorter space of time than one hour, with very little of this **divine powder**. <note>The "powder of projection," a substance alchemists believed could transmute base metals into gold.</note> God knows with how much joy I was filled. Yet I was not boastful because of it, nor did I exalt myself before the world, but secretly I gave immortal thanks to God, who had deigned to hear my prayers through **JESUS CHRIST** his son our Lord; I prayed further that He might illuminate my heart and understanding by His grace, so that I might use this gift for the honor and praise of His glorious name. On the next day, I quickly prepared myself for a journey toward my **Abbot** <note>Zachaire's former patron and partner in alchemical research, mentioned earlier in his narrative.</note> to fulfill my promised word, but he had died six months prior. For this reason, I was struck with the greatest grief, as well as by the death of the other **Doctor**, namely, which was reported to me on my journey while passing not far from his monastery. 

Therefore, I betook myself to a certain place assigned to a close relative of mine before I departed from my country, so that I might wait for him to arrive. I had left him at my home with **power of attorney** <note>original: *potestate procuratoria*</note> to sell all my paternal goods so that he might satisfy my creditors and distribute the remainder secretly to the poor and needy, and so that my parents and others might become sharers of this gift with which God had willed to honor me. Most people, and almost everyone else, thought I was acting out of despair, and that I had sold everything out of shame for such great and useless expenses, so that I might flee to foreign regions and remain hidden and unknown; so my relative reported to me when he arrived on the first of July. 

Afterward, we went to **Lausanne**, situated in **Savoy**, <note>A city in modern-day Switzerland; at the time, its political alignment was shifting between the Duchy of Savoy and the Swiss Confederacy.</note> so that from there we might travel to a certain famous trade center of **Germany**, with a very small company...

<header>LITTLE WORK.</header>

state, lest it become known even to those who might read this little work of mine while I am still living. I have composed this solely so that I might turn away good and pious men from **sophistications** <term>sophistications: in alchemy, this refers to fraudulent or "deceptive" processes that mimic true transmutation through alloys or chemical tricks</term> toward the right path of perfection in this divine work. As a conclusion to this first part, I bring to the memory of all what the Poet sang:

->*Happy is he whom the dangers of others make cautious.*<-
<note>original: "Fælix quem faciunt aliena pericula cautum." A common Latin proverb derived from the works of various medieval and classical authors.</note>

By this they may learn from my errors to flee useless expenses, which occur in **sophistications**. On the contrary, they should patiently and constantly first read and understand the good authors before they apply their hands to the work, always praying to God for grace and sincere understanding. Indeed, one does not arrive here by fate or by fortune, but by a **divine calling** alone, rather than by study. To those to whom it has been granted, there will likewise be given a way of veiling it from the unworthy, and of revealing it to other honest men, lest all understand equally; and likewise given is the grace of using it well, in honor of the Most High, the giver of lights. So be it. Amen.

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>A LITTLE WORK</header>

...[sin]gular <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of those working at "singula" (individual or particular tasks).</note> things through the authorities of the most ancient and learned philosophers, so that they might not think they are being exposed with their deceptions by any modern person. In order that we might better declare the first inventors of our science <term>science: here, 'scientia' refers to the structured knowledge of alchemy</term>, it is the teaching of the blessed Apostle James that we should recall to memory, which holds thus: "Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights," etc. <note>original: "Omnis donatio bona, & omne donum perfectum è supernis est..." — A quote from James 1:17, used here to argue that alchemy is a gift from God.</note> This sentiment is a general one, wherefore it also suits our intention, especially since our science is so divine and so supernatural (certainly in its second operation <term>second operation: likely referring to the higher stage of the alchemical process, such as multiplication or projection, as opposed to basic chemical preparation</term>) that it has always been, and still is, impossible for this to be made known to men through any study or industry whatsoever, even if they be the wisest and most learned of all philosophers, unless it be first inspired by God.

For in this part, all natural reason and experience fail us. Wherefore it has been deservedly written by some that the secret is reserved by God for His own, for those who fear and honor Him, as that great Prophet of ours, Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical and hermetic texts were attributed.</note>, says. "From no other," (he says) "nor through any other, than from God through His divine inspiration, do I possess [this]." Alphidius <note>An Arabic alchemist often cited in medieval Latin translations; his identity is somewhat obscure but his authority was widely recognized.</note> confirms the very same with these words: "Know, my son, that God has reserved this art for the descendants of Adam, and especially for the truly poor." Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the most famous figure in Islamic alchemy, known in the West as Geber. His 'Summa Perfectionis' was a cornerstone of the field.</note> affirms the same in his *Summa*, saying: "Our science is in the power of God, who out of His mercy gives it to whom He wills." It is not, therefore, in the power of men, nor was it devised by them. Truly, insofar as it is natural, or in its first operations imitates nature, to that extent there are various opinions in demonstrating the first inventor of that imitation. Not—

<meta>catchword: Non- (Not-)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that alchemy is a divine gift rather than a human invention, citing biblical and legendary authorities like St. James, Hermes, and Geber to prove that its "supernatural" secrets can only be attained through God's inspiration.</summary>
<keywords>divine inspiration, St. James, Hermes Trismegistus, Alphidius, Geber, supernatural science, alchemical secrets, imitation of nature</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of the origins of alchemy, moving from the divine inspiration mentioned on page 71 to the historical lineage of its first practitioners and the difficulty of learning from their writings alone.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIAS</header>

Some <note>original: "nulli"; likely a transcription error for "multi" (many), as the context lists various opinions.</note> assert that it was Adam, others Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of healing, often associated in alchemy with the restoration of health through the Elixir.</note>; some would have it that Enoch had the first knowledge of it—whom they say was actually Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure who was often identified with the biblical Enoch or the Egyptian god Thoth.</note>, to whom the Greeks attributed so much honor that they called him the inventor of all the occult arts. For my part, I would gladly agree with this last opinion, since it is most certain that he was a preeminent Philosopher, as is apparent from his writings. He investigated the causes of things most diligently through experiences in natural science; once these were known, he likewise became aware of the matter which nature uses in the hollows of the earth to generate metals. 

For as many as have imitated nature, they have all arrived at the true knowledge of this art, such as Pythagoras, Plato, Socrates, Zeno, Haly <note>Haly Abenragel, an 11th-century Arab astrologer and alchemist.</note>, Senior Rhazes <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and one of the most important figures in the history of medicine and alchemy.</note>, Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Islamic alchemy.</note>, Morienus, Bonus <note>Petrus Bonus, author of "The New Pearl of Great Price."</note>, Arnold of Villanova, Raymond <note>Raymond Lull, to whom many later alchemical works were attributed.</note>, and many others whom it would be tedious to list. From all these, as if from the most principal authorities, we have composed this present little work of ours, not without the greatest labor, as their own books testify. 

For they wrote in such a manner (as they set the fear of God before their eyes) that it is impossible to reach a knowledge of this art by the mere reading of their books; as Geber says in his *Summa*: "There is no reason," he says, "for the son of the art <term>son of the art: a traditional term for a dedicated student or initiate of alchemy</term> to lose heart in this divine work; for by constantly meditating on the causes of natural compositions, he will finally reach that goal toward which his mind tends." <note>original: "Non est quòd filius artis animum despondea... tandem eo perueniet, quò tendit animus eius."</note> Conversely, whoever expects to find the secret solely by means of our books will achieve the matter only slowly; for the Philosophers wrote in such a way (he says in another place) that they seem to congratulate only themselves on the practice <note>That is, the practical laboratory application of the theory.</note>

<summary>Zacharias traces the lineage of alchemy from figures like Hermes (Enoch) through the great Greek and Arabic masters, concluding that while they provide the foundation, the art cannot be learned through reading alone without divine meditation.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Enoch, Geber, Rhazes, lineage of alchemy, philosophy vs practice, son of the art, natural composition</keywords>

<header>THE LITTLE WORK.</header>

...the true <note>original: "veram"; likely referring to the "true knowledge" or "true science" mentioned on the previous page.</note>, mixing into this the mode of inquiry; likewise placing the principal operations in various ways and in different chapters, he provides the reason: Because if they had observed order in their treatises, this **divine science** could have been understood by everyone on the first day, or in a single hour, so noble and wonderful is it.

**Alphidius** <note>An Arabic alchemist often cited in medieval Latin texts, possibly a corruption of the name Al-Kindi or another early philosopher.</note> says: It must be believed that the **Philosophers**, our predecessors, concealed their primary intention under enigmatic and ambiguous speech, and countless figures <note>In alchemical literature, "figures" refers to allegorical illustrations, symbols, or metaphors meant to hide the truth from the uninitiated.</note>, lest through the manifestation of their doctrine, the world should suffer ruin thereby. For all agriculture, commerce, and whatever is necessary for the preservation of human life would perish: since no one would wish to labor when everyone saw themselves filled with such great wealth, they would despise necessity.

Whence it happens that **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>, excusing himself at the beginning of his book, says: Little sons, it is not that you should think the Philosophers concealed this <term>arcanum|A deep secret or mystery, specifically the secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</term> out of envy toward the learned and studious, but only lest it become known to the wicked and the ignorant. **Rosinus** <note>The Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, a Greco-Egyptian alchemist from the 3rd century.</note> says: By this means, the ignorant would be made equal to the wise, and a malicious, worthless man would abuse it, to the detriment, ruin, and destruction of all the people.

**Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Islamic alchemy whose works were central to Latin alchemy.</note> uses similar excuses in his *Summa*, in the chapter on the **Administration of the Solar Medicine** <term>Solar Medicine|The "Gold Medicine" or Red Elixir, capable of transmuting metals into gold (associated with the Sun) and healing the human body.</term>, saying: It is not that the sons of doctrine should wonder why the Philosophers wrote their books obscurely; for it was not done for them, but they wished this meaning to be veiled from the ignorant and unworthy under such great variety and confusion of diverse operations: meanwhile, however, the path is paved for the **sons of wisdom**, by which

<page-num>76</page-num>

...those who wrote in this art in the past, whether in Hebrew, Greek, 
or Latin, or in any other tongue, have agreed among themselves; and

that is performed rightly is perfected by a single means." 
Therefore, it is the opinion of all that our divine work is completed

science," he says, "is not perfected through diverse things, but 
only through a single thing, to which we add nothing, nor do we 
diminish it, but we only remove the superfluous, separating them

is perfected by only one thing, by one regimen, and by a single 
means." All the **Philosophers** who have written in this art 
until now assert the same, although they may seem to be 
pulled in different directions. The most powerful certainty 
of our science, by which we are confirmed that it is most true,

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>->A WORK OF ALCHEMY<-</header>
<sig>F 2</sig>

...Senior and **Rhazes** <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi, the Persian physician mentioned on the previous page.</note> follow. But in order to prove this to those who—with good reason—happen to doubt, it must be confessed, along with the other **Philosophers**, that this art is contained under that part of philosophy which they call **operative** <note>Philosophy was traditionally divided into "speculative" (theory) and "operative" (practical application/experiment).</note>, and under medicine, which can prove its truth from nowhere else than from experience. Just as when medicine teaches that **Rhubarb** <note>original: "Reubarbarum"</note> purges bile <note>original: "coleram," referring to the yellow bile humor.</note>, this cannot be better understood than by the eye perceiving it, and by experiment through the application of said simple medicine.

By a similar reasoning, we conclude in our art: if it is seen that **mercury** <note>original: "mercurium"</note> is coagulated by the smoke of lead alone, and that from **vitriols** <note>original: "atramentorum" - literally "black inks," but in alchemy, this refers to various metallic sulfates like green or blue vitriol.</note>, a most perfect medicine for the nature of metals can be made and composed, consistent with their qualities, by which other imperfect metals can be perfected solely through **projection** <note>term: "projection" (proiectio) - the act of casting a small amount of the Philosopher’s Stone or "medicine" onto a base metal to transform it.</note> performed upon them; especially since even compound minerals themselves congeal mercury and reduce it into its own nature. 

How much more will those perfect things, rightly prepared through our art, congeal it and reduce it with other imperfect metals, by their great and exuberant **decoction** <note>term: "decoction" (decoctio) - the process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or mature its qualities.</note> which they possess through the administration of our art. In order to more firmly prove our intention to curious men, so that they may better assent to its truth and certainty, let us bring forward the opinion of **Aristotle** in the fourth book of the *Meteorology* <note>original: "Meteor." - Aristotle's treatise on the elements and their transformations.</note>: 

"Every thing," he says, "which performs the same operation as some composite, is similar to it in all respects; just as everything that has the operation of an eye, is an eye." 

In no different way, since our gold composed by our art is similar in all respects to the mineral gold (concerning which revolves the whole controversy of the debate: whether the gold composed by us is true gold), we seem to have taught enough from...

<summary>This page argues that alchemy is an "operative" science validated by empirical experience, comparing its practical proofs to the way medicine uses rhubarb to treat bile. The author cites Aristotle's Meteorology to argue that if alchemical gold functions exactly like natural mineral gold, it must, by definition, be true gold.</summary>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>## THE WORKS OF DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS</header>
<meta>catchword: yet</meta>

According to the opinion of the **Philosopher** <note>In this context, "The Philosopher" almost always refers to Aristotle, who provided the logical and physical framework for medieval science.</note>, our art is true and most certain. This will be confessed as sufficiently proven by those who have seen its practical demonstration <note>original: "experientiam"</note>, but not by others; to these others, we shall bring forward more probable authorities of the **Philosophers** regarding this matter. In the same book of *Meteorology* <note>original: "Meteororum"; referring to Aristotle's *Meteorologica*, specifically Book IV, which was used by alchemists to explain the formation of minerals.</note>, in the fourth chapter on **Digestion** <note>term: "digestion" (digestio) - in alchemy, this refers to the process of maturing or ripening a substance through gentle, continuous heat, mimicking the way gold was thought to "grow" in the earth.</note>, he says something like this:

Whatever is destined for perfection, and yet remained as it is because of a defect of digestion, can be perfected by constant digestion. Such are all imperfect metals, since they were ordered by nature toward **gold** from the very beginning of their composition, so that they might ultimately be brought to that end—namely gold—by that same nature; nor are they in such a state [imperfect] except because of incomplete digestion. Therefore, we shall hear what follows in that place where we discuss the fourth part of our division.

What nature, therefore, intended to perform beneath the earth, we achieve above the ground by imitation through our art and by **projection** <note>term: "projection" (proiectio) - the act of casting a small amount of the Philosopher’s Stone or transformative "medicine" onto a base metal to complete its transmutation into gold.</note>, as will be heard more extensively later in the penultimate part of our division.

Again, since the elements, which are mutually contrary to one another in their qualities—as the Philosopher says in the second book of *On Generation* <note>original: "Generationum"; referring to Aristotle's *De Generatione et Corruptione*, which discusses how matter changes form.</note>—are convertible into one another, how much more can the metals themselves be converted (this one into that) most easily? For metals are of one and the same substance and matter, and are not contrary in their qualities. For this reason, **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage and purported founder of the alchemical tradition.</note> said that their procreation is circular, yet in a certain way improperly so, as he himself confesses; for they were not created by nature for the purpose of perfect things becoming imperfect, but the opposite. Wherefore, their generation is not entirely circular, except in part.

Although these and similar reasons are sufficient in themselves for the confirmation of the art: yet—

<summary>Dionysius Zacharius uses Aristotelian physics to argue that because all metals are composed of the same underlying matter and "strive" toward the perfection of gold, the alchemist can complete nature's unfinished work through "digestion" and "projection."</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Hermes Trismegistus, digestion, projection, metallurgy, alchemy, elements, transmutation, Meteorologica</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, the author cited Aristotle’s "Meteorology" to argue that imperfect metals can be perfected through "digestion." He now addresses the common skeptical counter-argument that metals must first be reduced to their "first matter" to be transformed.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>A LITTLE WORK</header>
<sig>F 3</sig>

...but because the **sophists** <note>original: "sophistæ"; here referring to skeptics or critics who used logical fallacies to argue against the possibility of alchemy.</note> always assert the contrary—which resists the truth—we shall bring forward those arguments that are more difficult. If we do not address them (though it would be more long-winded than suits this little work of ours), they might disturb the hearts and minds of students and keep them hanging in a middle state between doubt and resolution. By seeing the solutions to these, students may learn to refute other claims.

Based on the authority of the **Philosopher** <note>Aristotle</note> in the fourth book of *Meteorology* <note>original: "Meteororum"</note>, an authority shared by **Avicenna** <note>A Persian polymath (c. 980–1037) whose works on medicine and philosophy were foundational in the Middle Ages.</note> and **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century German Dominican friar and philosopher who wrote extensively on the natural sciences.</note>, they argue as follows: **Alchemists** work in vain when trying to perfect metals unless they first reduce them to their **first matter** <note>term: "first matter" (prima materia) — the fundamental, formless substance that alchemists believed could be stripped of its old qualities and impressed with new ones, such as the form of gold.</note>. Since they do not do this, they conclude that the works of all alchemists are deceptive. As Albert himself says in this manner:

"Whosoever tints metals into different colors using various simple materials are impostors, unless they reduce them to their first matter."

Many very wise men have attempted to resolve this argument, as it is among the most apparent challenges. Some of them say that although the imperfect metals are not reduced during the process of **projection** <note>term: "projection" (proiectio) — the act of casting the finished Philosopher's Stone onto a molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note>, nevertheless, a similar reduction occurs during the composition of the **medicine** <note>In alchemy, "the medicine" is a common synonym for the Philosopher's Stone or Elixir, used to "cure" imperfect metals.</note>. Specifically, it is reduced into **sulfur** and **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury.</note>, which are the true matter of metals, as will be discussed more broadly in the fourth part of our division.

Therefore, the reduction of the active agent alone is sufficient, combined with the highest perfection acquired through **decoction** <note>term: "decoction" (decoctio) — the process of refined heating used to mature or "ripen" a substance.</note>, even if no reduction of the passive subject [the base metal] occurs. This was the opinion of **Arnaldus de Villanova** <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> in his *Great Rosary*, and he was followed by **Raymond Lull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher to whom many influential alchemical texts were attributed.</note> in his *Testament*. With all due peace and reverence to these most serious authors, however, this opinion seems to contradict too much...

<summary>This page addresses a major philosophical objection to alchemy: the claim by Aristotle and Albertus Magnus that metals must be reduced to their "first matter" to be changed. The author presents the counter-argument that reducing the "medicine" (the Stone) into its constituent sulfur and mercury is sufficient for the transformation to occur.</summary>
<keywords>first matter, sophists, Aristotle, Albertus Magnus, sulfur and mercury, Arnaldus de Villanova, Raymond Lull, projection, decoction</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>Continuity: Having addressed the skeptics' claim that metals must be reduced to their "first matter" before they can be transformed, Dionysius Zacharius now offers his rebuttal, distinguishing between creating something entirely new and simply perfecting what is already there.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
->DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS<-

This is the view held by all philosophers. For since they concede that metals must be reduced to their **first matter** <note>term: "first matter" (prima materia) — the most basic, formless substance from which all things are made; critics argued that to change lead to gold, one must first strip it back to this state.</note>, which, according to **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose theories of "generation and corruption" formed the basis of medieval science.</note>, occurs through the motion of corruption, they seem to suggest that by the mere **fusion and projection** <note>term: "projection" — the final stage of the alchemical process where the Philosopher's Stone is cast upon molten metal to transform it.</note> of our divine stone upon metals, these metals are corrupted in such a way and stripped of their **first form** <note>original: "prima sua forma"; the essential quality that makes a substance what it is (e.g., the "lead-ness" of lead).</note>. To concede this would be quite unworthy of any philosopher.

Many others offer different solutions, as may be seen in their own books. However, I shall put forward my own solution. If we were indeed attempting to manufacture entirely new metals, or stones out of them, or anything else foreign to the nature of metals, then they would necessarily have to be reduced to their first matter by the means already mentioned. 

But since our intention is only to perfect imperfect metals into gold—without any transformation into new materials that differ from their own nature—we instead purge and cleanse them through the **projection** of our divine work, so that they may arrive at an overflowing perfection. There is no need at all to reduce them to their first matter; for it is one thing to **perfect the imperfect**, and quite another to produce or generate something entirely new from it. Otherwise, one would have to conclude that all **half-cooked** <note>original: "semicocta"; alchemists often compared base metals to "unripe" or "undercooked" fruit or food that only needed further heat and time to become "perfect" gold.</note> things must be reduced to their first form in order to be completely cooked—an idea that is truly unworthy of any philosopher.

I will set aside other objections commonly raised against the **chemical arts** <note>original: "chemicas artes"; here referring to alchemy.</note> for the time being, because any remaining arguments can easily be resolved by the principles we have established. However, there is a point of authority from **Avicenna** <note>A Persian philosopher and physician whose works were central to medieval science; though he was often skeptical of alchemy, alchemists frequently cited him.</note> that I recall, concerning the contradictions Aristotle raised against alchemy in his youth. "I have known," he says, "the intention of those who deny our art, and of others who affirm it to be true."

<summary>Zacharius argues that alchemy does not require metals to be destroyed down to their "first matter" because the process is one of perfecting "half-cooked" metals rather than creating a new substance from scratch. He uses the analogy of cooking to show that base metals are simply immature versions of gold.</summary>

<keywords>first matter, Aristotle, Avicenna, perfection of metals, projection, Philosopher's Stone, chemical arts, maturation</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>Continuity: Dionysius Zacharius has just argued that alchemical transformation does not require reducing metals to their "first matter" in a way that destroys them, but rather purges and perfects them. He now moves to explain why his method is both natural and hidden from the senses.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
# A LITTLE TREATISE #
F 4

The former authorities, such as **Aristotle** and many others, use certain arguments that appear plausible, but are by no means true. The latter writers, however, say that this science cannot or should not be proven by certain demonstrations as all others are, since it proceeds in a different way, contrary to all others. For it hides and veils the properties of its terms, while other sciences labor mostly in their explanations. Therefore, following the order of our division, I will take up its third part, explaining its necessary operations for the execution of our **divine work** <note>term: "divine work" (opus divinum) — a common name for the alchemical process, suggesting it mimics God’s creative power and produces the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. First, I will state the reason why it is called "natural" and "divine," so that the enormous errors of those practicing in this time may be recognized.

**Aristotle** teaches that nature operates beneath the earth in the **procreation of metals** <note>term: "procreation of metals" — the Aristotelian and medieval belief that metals grow like living things inside the "womb" of the earth over centuries.</note> through four qualities—or elements, to be better understood: fire, air, water, and earth. Two of these encompass the remaining two: earth contains fire, and water contains air. 

Since, therefore, our matter consists of water and earth (as will be said further in the penultimate part of our division), it is rightly called **natural**, because four elements enter into its composition: two are manifest, beneath which the other two lie hidden, veiled from bodily eyes—namely, fire and air—which must be perceived by the mind alone, as **Raymundus Lullius** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher. Many alchemical texts were later attributed to him, forming a body of work called "Pseudo-Lullian" alchemy.</note> says in his *Codicillus* <note>original: "Codicillus"; a famous alchemical manual attributed to Llull, often used as a primary textbook for the "Great Work."</note>, in this manner:

> **Consider (he says) the nature and property of oil (which the sophisters** <note>term: "sophisters" — here meaning fraudulent or misguided alchemists who use deceptive or "sophistical" logic rather than true natural principles.</note> **call air, because they say it abounds more in its qualities); your eye shall not judge its differences and properties.**

Through these words, he sufficiently implies that the four elements in our divine work are not evident, which many falsely...

<summary>Zacharius argues that alchemy is a "natural" science because it uses the four elements, though fire and air remain hidden within the earth and water. He cites the authority of Aristotle and the alchemist Raymond Lull to explain that the true properties of the work are perceived by the mind rather than the eyes.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Raymundus Lullius, Codicillus, four elements, divine work, procreation of metals, alchemy, natural philosophy</keywords>

# DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS #

...as they thought, as will be said below, where we will declare the terms of the art. This science is also called "natural" because in its first operation it imitates nature as diligently as it can—for it could not do so in all things, as **Geber** says in his **Summa** <note>original: *Summa* — referring to the *Summa perfectionis magisterii*, a highly influential alchemical text attributed to the 8th-century figure Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan).</note>. We find the best confirmation in the operations of the ancient natural Philosophers who preceded us. After the most diligent investigations, these men realized—as **Raymond Lully** says in his letter to **King Albert**, and **Albertus Magnus** in his treatise **On Simple Minerals** <note>original: *Simplicium mineralium*</note>—that nature works toward the procreation of metals only beneath the earth through continuous and persistent **decoctions** <note>term: "decoctions" — the process of heating a substance to refine it, much like "cooking" materials in the womb of the earth.</note> of true matter. Through these, nature separates the clean from the unclean, the pure from the impure, and the perfect from the imperfect by means of constant evaporations. This is achieved partly by the internal heat of the mineral-bearing earth and partly by the heat of the Sun—though the Sun alone cannot complete this total and perfect decoction.

This is excellently explained by the good **Trevisan** <note>historical reference: Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century alchemist known for his travels and his belief that the Great Work should be simple and natural.</note>, and experience teaches us the same. For we see in the mines a diversity of metals and materials: some are thicker and coarser, while others are more subtle and pure, often being raised or "exalted" to the heights. Therefore, our science, in imitating nature, proceeds in its first operation through **sublimation** <note>term: "sublimation" — an alchemical process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then re-solidifies, used here to represent the purification and "exaltation" of the matter.</note>, by which it may best purify our matter. Indeed, it would be impossible for us to prepare it in any other way, as **Geber** says in the **Summa** and **Rhazes** in the **Book of Lights** <note>original: *Luminum*</note>: "The beginning of our work is to sublimate." This is why it is rightly called "natural." For this reason, those who came before us wrote that this work of ours is not artificial, but whatever...

# DIONYSIUS ZACHARIAS

...which is the opinion of the Philosophers, that all things congealed by cold abound greatly in aqueous humidity in their **prime matter** <term>materia prima — the fundamental, formless substance from which all physical things are created</term>, as *Aristotle* <note>The Greek philosopher whose "Meteorology" provided the scientific framework for medieval alchemy.</note> says in the fourth book of the *Meteorology*. Since, therefore, molten metals are congealed by cold, their **prime matter** must necessarily possess much aquatic humidity. But *Albert the Great* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican scholar who was a primary authority on the "nature of things" and minerals.</note> (who examined minerals more precisely than others) says that this aqueous humidity is not like the humidity of water which we commonly see in other natural compounds. For the latter passes into smoke through the violence of fire. But molten metals are not so reduced into vapor; therefore, it must be concluded that their humidity is mixed with some matter by which they are retained in the fire and are safe from its vehemence. Nothing, however, resists fire more than a **viscous humidity** <note>original: *humiditas viscosa* — a thick, oily moisture that acts as a binding agent, preventing the substance from drying out or burning up.</note> mixed with the finer parts of earth, as *Bonus of Ferrara* <note>Petrus Bonus, author of the influential 14th-century alchemical text "The New Precious Pearl."</note> says, and as experience teaches; for which reason it is firmly to be believed that the humidity of metals is of such a kind. Yet since experience teaches that, during purification, some humidities pass away into smoke, while the metals are nevertheless not consumed in the fire, it must be confessed, with the principal authors of our science, that a twofold **viscous humidity**—extrinsic and intrinsic—enters into the composition of metals. The former is thick, and not well mixed with its subtle earthy matter; for this reason, it is easily burnt by fire until it is consumed. The latter, however, because it is most subtle and best mixed with its subtle earth—so that both parts constitute a single and simple matter—can neither be separated by fire nor consumed, but rather they pass away together, or remain to-

<meta>catchword: -gether</meta>

<summary>This page explains the internal composition of metals, arguing that their durability comes from a "viscous humidity" that binds the elements together. It distinguishes between impure, extrinsic moisture that burns away and the pure, intrinsic moisture that defines the metal's essence.</summary>

# A SHORT TREATISE

...remain in the fire. From such a humidity is created the **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum — literally "living silver," known today as mercury</term> that we see, as is also evident through experience. *Arnaldus de Villanova* <note>Arnau de Vilanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist who sought the "universal medicine."</note> bears witness to this: "This," he says, "makes us more certain that both materials previously mentioned <note>Referring to moisture and earthiness.</note> are most perfectly joined within it: for either the earthiness retains the humidity with itself, or the humidity carries the earthiness into the air."

This was best understood by *Albert the Great* <note>Albertus Magnus, the medieval scholar who attempted to reconcile Aristotelian science with Christian thought.</note> as he searched most diligently for the causes of the composition of metals. He recognized that quicksilver moves and flows constantly because each part in turn holds dominion over the other. Thus, the cause of its flow and motion is the rule of humidity, while the action of earthy dryness upon that humidity ensures that it does not stick to what it touches, nor does it make things wet.

From this, *Albert's* opinion is confirmed in his book *On Simple Minerals*: "The **prime matter**," he says, "of metals is an incombustible **viscous humidity** <note>original: *humiditas viscosa* — a thick, oily moisture that acts as a binding agent.</note>, joined through a strong mixture with a subtle earthiness in the mineral caverns of the earth."

This agrees perfectly with *Geber* <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Islamic alchemy; these quotes likely come from the "Summa Perfectionis," a core alchemical textbook.</note>, who asserts in his *Summa* that the true matter of metals is quicksilver, because, he says, "nature, never idle, created quicksilver from the aforementioned mixed matter," etc. *Bonus of Ferrara* <note>Petrus Bonus, author of the 14th-century "The New Precious Pearl."</note> says that quicksilver is the proximate matter of metals, but specifically a viscous humidity mixed with its own subtle earthiness. *Geber* also provides this definition of quicksilver in his *Summa*: "It is," he says, "a viscous humidity condensed by the help of its earthy part, which enters into its composition."

Let us therefore consider diligently the progress of nature in the creation of all things, to which she mixes a proper matter, which the Philosophers called

# THE WORKS OF DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS
### ON THE GENERATION OF METALS

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing an argument from the previous page about the role of an "agent" in creation.</note> acting, because it does not produce itself, as Aristotle says: that is, it does not demonstrate its own effects. For this reason, nature, in the procreation of metals, after it has created the matter—that is, **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum — mercury</term>—as something most adaptable, joins to it its own proper agent: namely, a certain kind of mineral earth, like its **colostrum** <note>original: *collostrum* — the first, nutrient-rich milk produced after birth, used here as a metaphor for a "primordial" nourishing substance.</note> and fatness in the mineral caverns of the earth, thickened by a long **decoction** <note>A process of heating or "cooking" within the earth's crust over long periods of time.</note>. This we commonly call **sulphur** <note>In alchemy, "sulphur" is not just the yellow chemical element, but a philosophical principle representing heat, masculinity, and the "form-giving" power.</note>. In comparison to quicksilver, it is no different than rennet to milk, the male to the female, or the agent to the matter subjected to it.

The Philosophers have proposed a twofold sulphur: one of very easy melting, and the other indeed only solidified and not **fusible** <term>non-fusible — incapable of being melted into a liquid state</term>. Accordingly, so that nature might demonstrate the power and efficacy of the agent—that is, sulphur—upon the matter to which it is joined, she brought about by a certain marvelous composition that metals should be solidified by the action of **fusible sulphur**, so that they might be capable of melting. 

But she composed other minerals by the simple action of **non-fusible sulphur**, so that they would not be meltable; such are **magnesia**, **marcasites** <note>Iron pyrites or "fool's gold," which does not melt like true gold but decomposes when heated.</note>, and others of that kind. 

Since, however, the agent cannot be a material part of the compound, as Aristotle says, nature, procreating metals beneath the earth, after she has mixed the said sulphur with quicksilver in an indescribable composition, then generates from it the most precious metal, namely **gold**: separating from it its agent—that is, sulphur—by a perfect decoction. 

And this is the reason why it is thus more perfect than all other metals, and also because we see that nature does not transmute it any further into anything better. Likewise, for this reason, gold is more easily and better joined to quicksilver, because it is nothing other than quicksilver [joined with its] own...

<meta>catchword: suo (its own)</meta>

<summary>Dionysius Zacharius explains the "Sulphur-Mercury theory," arguing that gold is the most perfect metal because nature has "cooked" it perfectly, using a specific type of fusible sulphur to solidify quicksilver into a stable, meltable form.</summary>
<keywords>Dionysius Zacharius, alchemical theory, sulphur, mercury, quicksilver, Aristotle, gold, magnesia, marcasite, decoction, generation of metals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the metallic "agent" known as sulphur. The previous page ended by discussing how nature uses sulphur to act upon matter; this page explains that the "perfection" of gold is achieved when this sulphur is finally separated or "cooked out" of the metal through natural heat.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>A LITTLE TREATISE.</header>

...cooked by its own **sulphur**, and immediately thereafter separated from it by the aforementioned **decoction** <note>original: *decoctio* — a process of "cooking" or refining a substance using heat to remove impurities.</note>. Just as the separation of sulphur represents the perfection of **gold** <note>original: *aurum*</note>, the fact that it remains behind in other metals is the primary cause of their imperfection. From this it follows that **silver** <term>argentum</term> is less perfect than gold, and **copper** <term>cuprum</term> less perfect than silver: namely, because of a lack of complete **digestion** <note>original: *digestio* — in alchemy, this refers to a slow, steady heat that matures a substance, much like the stomach digests food.</note>, which is the only way the agent—that is, the sulphur—is separated from them.

In this is declared the most powerful and chief secret of our entire **Art** <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, specifically the Great Work of transmutation.</note>. For since Art must imitate **Nature** in its operations, it is necessary that before this divine work of ours can be perfected, its agent or sulphur must be separated. 

All the Philosophers have indeed hidden this fact, referring us in their books to "natural operations," which I have already explained sufficiently and abundantly. However, so that it may be more perfectly understood in what way our science imitates the operations of nature, it would be worthwhile to explain the chief and more common method she uses in the perfection of metals. 

Previously, we stated that the perfection or imperfection of metals occurs because of the removal or the mixing of the agent—that is, the sulphur. We have already shown the first way which Nature is accustomed to use in the composition of the prince of metals, the most perfect one: gold. Now, it must be known that she also uses another progression. Although this second way may seem contrary to the first, it is nonetheless very similar if one properly considers the end toward which the intention of Nature tends. For it is likewise nothing other than to refine the metals and purge them thoroughly from their sulphur. For that which she achieves in the former way through perfect decoction, she achieves in the latter through continuous and long digestions...

**sulphur** <gloss>the active principle/agent</gloss>, **gold** <gloss>the perfect metal</gloss>, **silver** <gloss>the second metal</gloss>, **copper** <gloss>the base metal</gloss>, **digestion** <gloss>maturation by heat</gloss>, **metal** <gloss>mineral body</gloss>, **art** <gloss>alchemy</gloss>, **nature** <gloss>the guiding force</gloss>
