<meta>This page continues the argument that Art (Alchemy) imitates Nature. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing how "per..." (perfection) is achieved; this page opens by explaining how Nature gradually purifies metals into gold over long periods within the earth.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIAS</header>

...it carries out <note>This sentence continues from the previous page: "perfection is carried out."</note>, by gradually purifying the imperfect metals for such a time until they are reduced into **gold** <term>aurum</term>. Since experience teaches us that in **silver** mines, **lead** <term>plumbum</term> is commonly found, and in some, both are mixed together, so that the silver appears almost imperfect due to a lack of **digestion** <note>original: *digestio* — the alchemical process of maturing a substance through steady, natural heat.</note>: therefore, those skilled in metallic matters advise that once the caverns are closed up—so that the subtler part of the matter cannot evaporate—they should be left for thirty or forty years longer, until they are perfected. 

Which indeed **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century philosopher and bishop renowned for his writings on natural science and alchemy.</note> recounts happened in his own time in the kingdom of Liburnia, or Slavonia; likewise, it was reported to me by an experienced mineral miner that this happens quite often. Therefore, according to this method which nature uses in perfecting metals, **Art** <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy.</note> imitates nature through its own operations to perfect the imperfect. This is done, namely, by the removal of its **sulphur**, performed by the "projection" of the divine work upon the metals after they have been melted. By this, they are purified from their aforementioned sulphur and perfected into pure gold, through an abundant and perfected **decoction** <note>original: *decoctio* — a "cooking" or boiling down of a substance to refine it.</note> which the metal acquired through the administration of our Art. 

It is no different than the various ways nature uses in the purification of metals; these various ways do not cause the forms of gold to be different (I mean in its perfection). Likewise, the different methods we use to perfect it above the ground do not make our gold different from natural and mineral gold, since we use the same matter above the ground that nature uses beneath the earth and in its caverns. 

**Aristotle** <note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose theories on the composition of matter formed the foundation of medieval alchemy.</note> himself testifies to this in the ninth book of his *Metaphysics* <term>Metaphysica</term>. "From similar agents and matter," he says, "the operations are always similar, even if the means are different: for these and the matter are two..."

<summary>Zacharias argues that alchemy is a natural process that simply accelerates what the earth does over decades. He cites Albertus Magnus and Aristotle to claim that alchemical gold is identical to mined gold because they share the same material origins.</summary>
<keywords>maturation of metals, Albertus Magnus, Aristotle, Metaphysics, sulphur, gold, silver, lead, decoction, projection</keywords>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>A SMALL WORK</header>

...are. <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page.</note> But if the matter be one and similar in all respects to the agent, all operations—which at the beginning seem dissimilar or contrary—achieve the same effect towards the end. To prove that our matter, which we use to perfect metals above the earth, is in all things very similar to that which nature uses in the caverns of the earth for their procreation, we cite **Geber** <note>original: *Gebrum* — referring to Pseudo-Geber, the influential 13th-century author of the *Summa Perfectionis*.</note> as a witness in his *Summa*: "Our **Art**," he says, "imitates nature as closely and as possibly as can be done." **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note> says the same, as do **Pythagoras**, **Senior** <note>Zadith ben Hamuel, an 10th-century Arabic alchemist whose name was Latinized as "Senior."</note>, and many others. Accordingly, if Art imitates nature, it must necessarily be admitted that it uses a similar matter as nature, which in both cases can only be one alone; this we have called **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term>, not only by itself, but mixed together with its own proper agent, namely true **sulphur**. Therefore, this same matter, which the Philosophers called **animated quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum animatum</term>, will be the true matter of our divine science for completing our divine work. Since nature uses this very thing and no other in the hollows of the earth for the procreation of metals as its true matter, as we have sufficiently demonstrated above.
   They called it "animated quicksilver" to show the difference between it and **common quicksilver** <note>The liquid mercury found in nature or trade, which alchemists believed lacked the "life force" or "agent" required for the Great Work.</note>, because the latter remained as it is because nature did not join to it its own proper agent. Therefore, many have falsely believed that common quicksilver and common sulphur are the matter of metals. For it has never happened at any time that those two were found mixed together in mines; by what reasoning, therefore, can they be the true...

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 93. On the previous page, the author began to demonstrate that the matter used in the "Art" (alchemy) is identical to the matter found in the earth's depths. The text here picks up mid-sentence, concluding that demonstration.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
# OF DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS #

...matter of metals in the hollows of the earth, or of our art above the earth: as **Geber** speaks in his *Summa on the Principles of Art*. And the same author says in another place: "Our **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term> is nothing other than a viscous water, joined through the action of its own metallic **sulphur**." This is our true matter, which nature has prepared for our art (as **Valerandus Syluensis** <note>A 16th-century alchemist often cited in collections like the *Theatrum Chemicum*.</note> says) and reduced into a specific form known to true Philosophers, without further transformation of itself. **Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and alchemy were foundational in Europe.</note> similarly says: "Nature has prepared for us one single matter, which our art is quite unable to compose by itself." It would therefore be foolish to believe that out of all the materials we might mix, any—whether metallic or otherwise—could be the true matter of our science, since nature has prepared it for us so that nothing is lacking except these two things: namely, to purify and perfect it, and to join it together through its own proper and suitable **cooking** <term>decoctionem</term>. 

Regarding this matter, **Rasis** <note>The Latinized name of Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and chemist.</note> speaks in his *Book of Precepts*: "Our **mercury**," he says, "is the true foundation of our science, from which the true **tinctures** <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is a substance capable of transforming or "coloring" base metals into gold or silver.</note> of metals are extracted." **Alphidius** <note>An author of Arabic origin whose alchemical dialogues were popular in the Middle Ages.</note> says of the same: "Take note, my son, that the entire work of the wise Philosophers consists in quicksilver alone: wherefore **Hermes** commands us to observe this mercury, which he calls 'coagulated' and 'hidden in gilded hiding-places'." **Geber** says of the same mercury: "Praised be the Most High," he says, "who created this quicksilver, and gave it incomparable power for perfecting the true **mastery** <term>magisterium</term> <note>The *magisterium* or "mastery" refers to the successful completion of the Great Work—the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of our science." In short, there was not one among the wise writers who was not of this same opinion.

<page-num>95</page-num>

### TREATISE ###

Modern practitioners will object, judging me rash for daring to rebuke so many great predecessors whose theories and practices teach how to sublimate **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term> (which they call **mercury**) with **vitriol** <note>A historical name for various sulfate minerals, such as copper or iron sulfate, used as corrosive agents in alchemy.</note> and **common salt**. They then teach how to revivify it through hot water so that it may finally be mixed with **gold** (which they called **Sol** <note>original: "Solem"; the sun, used as the alchemical name and symbol for gold.</note>), and in this manner to dissolve and fix it, so that our **divine work** <term>opus divinum</term> may be perfected. This is what **Arnald of Villanova** <note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311) was a famous physician and alchemist whose works were highly influential in the Middle Ages.</note> writes in his *Great Rosary*; likewise **Ramon Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher. Many alchemical texts were attributed to him after his death.</note> in his *Testament*.

I shall answer these critics in a way that should satisfy them, using the very same authors they cite. Their own writings testify that all those operations—distillations, the separation of elements, reductions, and other things of that kind—were set down for no other reason than to veil the practice of our science under such disguises <note>original: "laruis," literally "masks" or "ghosts."</note>. We shall bring forward the same Arnald of Villanova who, after teaching these various operations in his *Rosary*, breaks out into these words (or something similar) near the end of his summary:

> We have shown (he says) the true practice and the true method of perfecting our divine work, but in very brief words, which will nevertheless seem long enough to those who understand. Therefore, through so many and various long-winded discussions of operations, he did not mean the true preparation of this divine work, nor the practice which he had previously said he had treated briefly.

Ramon [Llull] says the very same at the end of his *Codicil*, where he responds to those who might perhaps ask why he described the art, since he had previously said it was impossible for anyone to reach the art and its true knowledge through books...

G 3

<summary>The author argues that famous alchemical texts by Arnald of Villanova and Ramon Llull use complex descriptions of chemical processes as "masks" to hide the true, simpler secret of the work. He claims these masters admitted that their long explanations were not the actual practice.</summary>

<keywords>Arnald of Villanova, Ramon Llull, quicksilver, mercury, vitriol, gold, Sol, divine work, alchemical allegory, Rosarium, Codicillus</keywords>

<page-num>96</page-num>

<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS</header>

...to prevent? He answers: So that the faithful reader may be introduced and acquire for himself the disposition by which he may arrive at the true knowledge of our **divine work** <term>opus divinum</term>, the preparation of which we have never sincerely set down, nor openly declared. It is therefore clear that those lengthy and diverse preparations, which he <note>Referring back to the authorities mentioned on the previous page, such as Arnald of Villanova.</note> teaches in his books, are not the sole and unique practice of our **divine work**. 

Those who are a bit more learned will object to me, asking why I have written that our divine work is made from one single matter—namely, from one and only **animated quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum animatum</term> <note>Mercury that has been "awakened" or "vitalized" through alchemical processes to become the living base of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>—when **Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815). The *Summa Perfectionis* mentioned here was the most influential alchemical textbook in medieval Europe.</note> in his *Summa*, in the chapter on the *Coagulation of Mercury*, says it is extracted from metallic bodies prepared with their **arsenic** <note>In an alchemical context, "arsenic" often refers to a volatile "spirit" or a specific reagent rather than the modern chemical element alone.</note>. **Rosinus** <note>A Latinized name usually referring to the 3rd-century alchemist Zosimos of Panopolis.</note>, on the other hand, says it is true **incombustible sulfur** of which our divine work consists. **King Solomon**, son of David, attests to this very thing, saying:

> God preferred our true **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur"</note> above all things existing under heaven.

**Pythagoras** in the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," a famous 13th-century Latin text where ancient Greek philosophers are depicted debating alchemical principles.</note> wrote:

> Our divine work is perfected when sulfurs are joined in turn, one with the other;

therefore, it consists of sulfurs, and not of animated quicksilver alone. To provide an answer that satisfies those minds wandering from the path, we must recall what we said above concerning the matter of metals: namely, that nature, in the cavities of the earth, joins a proper **agent** to the quicksilver. Furthermore, because our divine work does not have a name of its own, some have given it one name and others another; as **Lilius** <note>Likely referring to the author of the *Lilium Intelligentiae* (Lily of Intelligence), another common alchemical authority.</note> wrote, it is called by as many names among the Philosophers as there are created things in this world, hinting by these words—

<meta>catchword: names</meta>

<summary>Zacharius addresses the apparent contradictions between his "one matter" theory and the writings of authorities like Geber and Pythagoras, who mention sulfur and arsenic. He argues that these different terms actually describe the natural process of mercury being acted upon by a specific agent in the earth.</summary>

### TREATISE.
<sig>G 4</sig>

...[names] <note>original: "nominibus"; the word begins on the previous page</note> to be called by infinite names: although one and the same thing always exists, consisting of a single matter alone. Nevertheless, the philosophers who preceded us imposed upon it so many and such various and diverse names at their own discretion, according to the variety of colors appearing during the **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>The process of "cooking" or heating the alchemical matter over a long period.</note>. Those who, like us, called it **animated quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum animatum</term> <note>Living mercury; the fundamental substance of the work after it has been "vitalized."</note>, considered our **primary matter** <term>materia prima</term> (which the ancient philosophers called **Chaos** <term>Chaos</term> <note>The primordial, unformed state of the universe; here used to describe the raw, disorganized state of the alchemical starting material.</note>, the beginning of everything taken from its very start) to be truly similar in all things to the nature and matter of quicksilver, out of which nature composes and perfects metals in the cavities of the earth, as we have shown above. Likewise, those who called our divine work the **Philosophers' Stone** <term>lapis philosophorum</term> (which is its more common name), did so with respect to the end of the decoction of our matter, because it remains fixed in the fire: for they are accustomed to call "stone" whatever does not flee the fire and is not sublimated <note>Turned into vapor</note> by it. Many others devised many other names, according to their own reasons, which would be long to review individually; as **Malvescindus** <note>An authority often cited in early Latin alchemical texts like the *Turba Philosophorum*.</note> says: "If we say our matter is spiritual, it is true; if corporeal, we do not lie; if truly celestial, that is its true name; if terrestrial, we do not speak improperly." From these words it is clearly evident that the variety of names imposed on our divine work by our ancient predecessors was based on various reasons, founded in the diversity of colors and operations appearing during its decoction.

Likewise, those who called it **sulfur** <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle, often associated with the "soul" of the matter.</note>, as attested by the authorities that could be brought against me, did so with respect to the final decoction, in which the matter...

...appears fixed; thus they have spoken. Therefore, just as from the beginning it demonstrated the true appearance of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term> <note>Mercury; in alchemy, it represents the principle of fluidity and volatility.</note> because it was volatile, in the end it is fixed. Then, at last, that which was hidden and existing within was unknown: namely the fixed part, which we call **sulfur** <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and "fixing" principle that gives form to the "quicksilver."</note>. It is manifested through the continuous and final **decoction** <term>decoctio</term>, in which it dominates over the volatile part. For this reason, our matter is no longer called volatile (I am speaking of those who consider the final decoction), but finally **fixed sulfur**.

As **Arnaldus de Villanova** <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist to whom many influential texts were attributed.</note> says in his *Great Rosary* <note>original: "Rosario magno"; a seminal medieval alchemical text.</note>, when he speaks of the final decoction of our divine work: "It is," he says, "true **red sulfur** <term>sulphur rubeum</term>, through which quicksilver can be perfected into gold." Therefore, we can truly conclude and resolve every doubt: the matter from which we compose our divine work is nothing but one single thing in all aspects, and in all ways similar to that matter which nature uses in the cavities of the earth for the procreation of metals—notwithstanding all the authorities cited above to the contrary, or any other similar ones. For a diversity of names (as **Aristotle** says) does not make a thing diverse.

To gradually bring our division to an end, it remains for us to declare the terms of our science; that is, to compare the opinions of the best and most distinguished authors who preceded us. Among other things, they use four terms in particular when discussing the composition of our divine work, which are: the **four elements**, **perfect ferment**, **true poison**, and **perfect coagulum** <note>A substance that causes a liquid to curdle or solidify; here, the agent that "fixes" the liquid mercury into solid gold.</note>, which they elsewhere called the **male**, [compared] to the fe-

<header>LITTLE WORK</header>
<note>original: "OPVSCVLVM"</note>

...feminine <note>Completing the word "feminam" from the previous page's catchword.</note>, making a comparison, like rennet to simple milk. So that we might better declare what he understands by the **elements** <term>elementa</term>, we must know what the natural philosophers said regarding **prime matter** <term>materia prima</term> <note>The formless, original substance from which all things were thought to be created.</note>, which they called **Chaos**. In this Chaos, those four elements were contained in a confused state; because of their contrary natures, they became manifest to us by each demonstrating its own actions. For this reason, **Alexander** <note>Likely referring to Pseudo-Alexander, a name often attached to alchemical "Epistles" or "Books of Secrets" in the Middle Ages.</note> says in his *Epistle*:

> Whatever (he says) was demonstrated to the ancients to be of a hot quality, they judged to be **fire**; that which was dry and coagulated, they judged to be **earth**; that which appeared to them moist and fluid, they called **water**; and that which seemed cold, subtle, and windy, they called **air**.

Two of these are hidden and enclosed within the other two, as **Rasis** <note>Abū Bakr al-Rāzī (c. 865–925 AD), a Persian polymath and one of the most influential figures in the history of alchemy and medicine.</note> says in his *Book of Precepts*:

> Every composite (he says) consists of four elements, with two hidden in the other two that are apparent: namely, **air** hidden in the secret part of **water**, and **fire** in the interior parts of **earth**, as we said before.

And because the two enclosed elements cannot show their actions except through the other two, they called them "weak," while the others they called "strong." For this reason, they say that compounds are perfect when the moist and the dry—that is, water and earth—are joined equally by the help of nature with the cold and the hot—which are air and fire—through the conversion of one into the other. Wherefore Alexander says in his *Book of Secrets*:

> If you convert the elements one into another, you will find what you seek.

This sentiment, once well and perfectly understood, demonstrates to us the true matter and indicates the perfect practice of our science; therefore, so that it may be better understood, [we shall speak] more closely of the four elements, and...

This page completes the word "elements" (elementorum) and proceeds to discuss the specific roles of earth and water in the alchemical "Great Work."</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS</header>
<note>original: "DIONYSII ZACHARII"</note>

[the elements] <note>Completing the word "elementorum" from the previous page.</note>, regarding whose nature we must speak, since they are necessary in the composition of our **divine work** <term>opus diuinum</term> <note>The "Great Work" or *Magnum Opus*, the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy.</note> says: "In our earth, all other elements were created." **Alphidius** <note>A common authority cited in medieval alchemical texts, often thought to be an Arab philosopher.</note>, on the other hand, puts forward that **water** is the principal element, from which the other elements required for the composition of our divine work were created. In these views, there is no apparent contradiction. For from the beginning of the procreation of our divine work, nothing else appears but water alone, which the Philosophers have called **mercurial water** <term>mercurialis aqua</term> <note>Not common water, but a specialized solvent or "living" mercury used to dissolve metals.</note>. From this water, earth was procreated when it was thickened by a supernatural conjunction and **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>A process of heating a substance in a fluid to extract its essence or refine it.</note>, aside from that which is useless to us. Therefore, Hermes spoke the truth when he said that the remaining elements proceeded from the earth, since the earth alone demonstrates its qualities in the second operation, just as water demonstrated its own at the start of the work. Wherefore Alphidius wrote to **Valerandus** <note>An otherwise obscure figure addressed in alchemical epistles.</note> and others that water is the principal element in the composition of our divine work.

These are those two elements which the Philosophers say must be known before we approach the work. As **Rhazes** <note>Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, the influential Persian physician and alchemist.</note> says in his *Book of Lights*: "Before (he says) the work is begun, one must know the nature and quality of water and earth, because in these two the four elements are comprised." Otherwise, the **volatile** <term>volatile</term> <note>Substances that easily vaporize or escape when heated.</note> will snatch the **fixed** <term>fixum</term> <note>Substances that remain stable and do not change when exposed to heat.</note> away with it, and our science will be useless to us. For this reason, the conversion of the four elements is commanded, so that our divine work, properly qualified, may be fixed in the end, and so that it may be able to resist all the violence of fire, the corruption of air, the rust of earth, and the putrefaction of water, no differently than **mineral gold** <term>aurum minerale</term>, because...

<meta>catchword: magnam</meta>

<summary>Dionysius Zacharius reconciles the views of Hermes and Alphidius, arguing that while water is the primary source of the work, it eventually produces the "earth" from which all other qualities emerge. He stresses the importance of mastering the "fixed" and "volatile" aspects of these elements to create a substance as permanent as natural gold.</summary>

<keywords>Dionysius Zacharius, Hermes Trismegistus, Alphidius, Rhazes, four elements, mercurial water, decoction, fixation, mineral gold, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 100, which discussed how the element of earth demonstrates the origin of the other elements. This page completes that thought, noting that this demonstration leads to their "great perfection." It then moves into specific authorities (Llull, Rhazes, and Arnold of Villanova) to argue that the alchemical process is a self-contained transformation of elements within a sealed vessel.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>

<header>LITTLE WORK</header>
<note>original: "OPVSCVLVM"</note>

[...demonstrates] <note>Completing the thought from the previous page: "the earth alone demonstrates [the elements']..."</note> their great perfection. This conversion of elements (as **Ramon Llull** <note>Raymundus Lullius, a 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many later alchemical works were attributed.</note> says) is nothing other than making the earth (which is **fixed** <term>fixa</term> <note>A substance that remains solid and does not evaporate when heated.</note>) into something **volatile** <term>volatilem</term> <note>A substance that easily turns into vapor or gas.</note>, and making the water (which is moist and volatile) dry and fixed. This is correctly achieved by our continuous **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>A process of heating a substance over a long period to refine it.</note> in our vessel, without any removal or opening of the container, lest our elements perhaps perish and fly away into the air as smoke.

The writings of **Rhazes** <note>Rasis; Abu Bakr al-Razi, a Persian physician and alchemist who emphasized laboratory observation.</note> and various other Philosophers testify to this same opinion when they say that the true separation and conjunction of the four elements takes place in our vessel without being touched by hands or feet in any way. For (as they say) our **stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the work.</note> dissolves, coagulates, washes, purifies, whitens, and reddens itself, without the addition or mixture of any external thing.

**Arnold of Villanova** <note>Arnaldus de Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> is of the same opinion in his *Great Rosary* <note>original: "Rosario magno"; likely referring to his *Rosarius Philosophorum*.</note>, when he says in very few words: "There is no need for any other labor than in the slaying of the water, that is, in its **fixation** <term>fixanda</term> <note>The process of making a liquid or volatile substance solid and resistant to fire.</note>. For as soon as this water is dead, the remaining elements are killed—that is, they are fixed." 

Therefore, the **sophistic** <note>In alchemy, "sophistic" refers to false, deceptive, or purely chemical operations that do not lead to the true Philosopher's Stone.</note> separations of elements performed by practitioners of this time are false. These methods are most poorly founded upon the writings of those Philosophers who expressly forbid the destruction of **simples** <term>simplicium</term> <note>Basic, unmixed medicinal or chemical ingredients.</note> in their preparations, because (as they say) it is impossible for Art to provide primary forms. Yet it is most certain that the four elements cannot be combined at all unless they were first destroyed. For this reason, there is no need for this sophistic separation of elements, which is a false [path] to the work... <meta>catchword: rum</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the alchemical Great Work is a self-contained process of "killing" or fixing water and volatilizing earth within a sealed vessel. It cites Llull, Rhazes, and Arnold of Villanova to criticize contemporary alchemists who use "sophistic" or false methods of separating elements.</summary>

<keywords>conversion of elements, fixation, volatile, Ramon Llull, Rhazes, Arnold of Villanova, decoction, sealed vessel, slaying the water, sophistic alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 101, which discussed the self-contained transformation of elements within a sealed vessel, emphasizing that the "stone" performs its own purification and transmutation. This page transitions into a critique of false alchemists and introduces the concept of "ferment."</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>

<header>OF DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS</header>
<meta>catchword: For indeed</meta>

...the composition of our divine [work]. That such a separation <note>Referring to the physical separation of elements by hand, which the author argues against.</note> is false is proven by this very fact which was stated before: namely, that two elements are contained within the other two. Whereby it happens that we are least able to know their true separation, much less their perfect conjunction. 

It is proven by experience that the elements which they <note>False practitioners or "sophists."</note> claim to have separated have nothing in common with the nature of true elements (as **Valerandus** <note>Likely Valerandus de Aula, an alchemical authority often cited in the 16th century.</note> says). Take for example their **oil** <note>original: "oleum"</note>, which they call "air"; it moistens and wets everything it touches, which is contrary to the true nature of air. Therefore, it shall be enough for me to have shown the nature and quality of the elements and their necessary conversion in this science of ours, so that the ignorance of modern practitioners may be made plain to the **true sons of science** <note>A traditional term for dedicated students of alchemy who seek spiritual and physical truth.</note>, and so that, recognizing them, they may flee from their company. 

Now let us see what the Philosophers understand by this term, namely, **ferment** <term>fermentum</term> <note>Just as yeast leavens bread, "ferment" in alchemy is a substance that "ripens" or transforms the base metal into the nature of the ferment itself (usually gold or silver).</note>. They say they have used it in two ways. In the first sense, they make a comparison of our divine work to metals, in no other way than we see a little bread leaven convert much flour into its own nature and dough. Similarly, our divine work converts metals to its own nature; and because it is **gold** <note>original: "aurum"</note>, it also transmutes them into gold. 

But because they rarely use it in this sense (in which there is no difficulty), we shall come to the second meaning, in which the greatest difficulty of our science lies. By **ferment**, therefore, in this second way, they understand the true body and true matter which completes our divine work—a thing which is indeed unknown to the eyes and perceptible only to the intellect.

<summary>Dionysius Zacharius critiques practitioners who attempt a physical separation of elements, arguing that true elements are intertwined. He introduces the concept of "ferment," explaining it both as a catalyst that transforms metals into gold and as a hidden, intellectualized "true matter."</summary>

<keywords>Dionysius Zacharius, Valerandus, elements, air, oil, ferment, gold, transmutation, sons of science, divine work</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of "ferment" on page 102. It clarifies the specific alchemical definitions of "Body," "Soul," and "Spirit," distinguishing them from common philosophical or Aristotelian usage to explain how the volatile matter is fixed through the "divine work."</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

<header>LITTLE TREATISE</header>

For indeed, from the beginning our **matter** <term>materia</term> <note>The raw, foundational substance used by alchemists to begin their work.</note> appears volatile (as we have sufficiently declared before), which it is necessary for us to join to its own **body** <note>original: "corpus"</note>, so that by this means it may retain the **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note>. Through this already-completed conjunction, and by the mediation of the **spirit** <note>original: "spiritus"</note>, the soul demonstrates its divine operations in our divine work.

As it is read in the **Assembly of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, presented as a gathering of ancient sages.</note> in this very manner: The **Body** has more strength than its two brothers, which they call the **Spirit** and the **Soul**. Not that they understand these terms in the way **Aristotle** <note>The alchemist is distinguishing his technical chemical definitions from Aristotle’s biological or metaphysical definitions of the soul.</note> and other Philosophers declared (which is very worthy of note), but rather they called "body" every simple substance which, by its own nature, can endure the fire without any loss: this they otherwise call the **fixed** <term>fixum</term>.

They called "soul" every simple substance that is volatile by itself, having the power of seizing the body with it and carrying it away from the fire: this they called by another name, the **volatile**.

They say the "spirit" is that which has the power of retaining the body and the soul, and of joining these two together so much that they can no longer be separated, whether they be perfect or imperfect. 

However, in our divine work, nothing new enters from the outside from the beginning (I mean after the first preparation), nor in the middle of the work, nor at the end. But the Philosophers, for various reasons and considerations, have called one and the same thing "body," "soul," and "spirit," as was abundantly said above. First, when our matter was volatile, they called it "soul" because it carried the body away with it. After that which was hidden was made manifest in our **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>A process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a fluid to extract its essence or ripen it.</note>, then at last the body openly showed its strength by means of the spirit; that is, it retained the soul, [binding] this to its own...

<summary>The author defines the alchemical "trinity" of Body, Soul, and Spirit, explaining how these terms describe the changing states of a single substance—from volatile to fixed—during the heating process.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Body-Soul-Spirit, Turba Philosophorum, Aristotle, volatile, fixed, decoction, matter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 103, where the "spirit" was defined as the mediator between the "body" and "soul." Here, the author explains how this mediation leads to the final "red" stage of the Great Work through the symbolic marriage of the Sun and Moon.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

-> ### DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS <-

...by bringing it back to its own nature (that is, into gold) and it has fixed it by its own power, through the help of our art. By this very fact it is exactly declared what **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy.</note> wrote: that no **tincture** <term>tinctura</term> <note>An alchemical substance capable of "dyeing" or transforming base metals into gold.</note> is made without the **red stone** <note>original: "lapidem rubeum"; the final, most potent state of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

For as **Rosinus** <note>Likely a Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, an early alchemist.</note> says: Our true sun appears white and imperfect in our **decoction** <term>decoctio</term> <note>The process of heating a substance in a liquid or its own moisture to extract its essence.</note>, and it is perfect in its red color. It is the **ferment** <note>A substance added to "ripen" the alchemical matter.</note> of which **Arnaldus de Villanova** <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemical author.</note> speaks in his *Great Rosary*, which appears in those two colors, untouched and unmixed with any other matter, as might be understood through those writings.

**Anaxagoras** <note>Though a Greek philosopher, he is frequently cited in medieval alchemy as an authority on the composition of matter.</note> testifies this to be true, saying: Our sun is red and burning, which is joined to the white soul and the nature of the moon, by means of the spirit; although in truth the whole thing is nothing other than the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <term>argentum vivum Philosophorum</term> <note>Not common mercury (quicksilver), but the refined, "living" essence of matter used in the Work.</note>.

**Morienus** <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist.</note> declares: It would be impossible (he says) to reach the perfection of our science until the moon is joined to the sun, without which our science is useless, as Hermes and all the rest of the Philosophers say. Through these things can be understood what **Rhazes** <note>original: "Rasis," a Persian polymath and influential alchemical writer.</note> says in the *Book of Lights*: The **red servant** (he says) has taken the **white woman** as his wife, at the end of the perfection of our divine work <note>A classic alchemical allegory for the union of gold (the red servant) and silver (the white woman).</note>.

Similarly, what **Lilius** <note>Refers to the author of the *Lilium Intelligentiae*, a common alchemical text.</note> holds: The true union of body and soul occurs in the white and red color, yet by the same medium, at a certain time, through the help of our decoction, governed in such a way that our matter is not destroyed; for, as it is found written in the **Assembly** <note>original: "Turba," referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note>, both the profit and the loss of our divine work proceed from the **management of the fire**. Therefore I would advise, along with Rhazes, that no one should rashly undertake the task of this practice in this science of ours, before...

<meta>catchword: than</meta>

<summary>This page synthesizes the views of various alchemical authorities to explain the "divine work" as a union of opposites, specifically the red "Sun" (gold) and the white "Moon" (silver). It emphasizes that the transition to the perfect red stone depends entirely on the precise management of fire during the final stages.</summary>

<keywords>red stone, tincture, Sun and Moon, Mercury of the Philosophers, Rhazes, Arnaldus de Villanova, management of fire, alchemical marriage, decoction</keywords>

<page-num>105</page-num>

-> ### LITTLE TREATISE <-
<note>original: "OPUSCULUM"</note>

...so that he may know very well how all the regimens of fire (because they are diverse) are especially necessary in the composition of our **divine work** <note>original: "operis nostri diuini"; alchemy was often termed the "Divine Work" as it was seen as mirroring God's creation.</note>; otherwise the "limit," which they call **poison** <term>venenum</term>, will be applied to it, which usually happens in the second operation, as was said before. No one should think because of this that anything poisonous must be added to our matter, nor **Theriaca** <term>Theriaca</term> <note>A famous ancient medical concoction used as a universal antidote; here used metaphorically.</note>, much less anything foreign (as some have thought, considering only the literal word). 

Rather, it is that the operator must be industrious and vigilant, so that he does not miss the limit and the hour of the birth of our **mercurial water** <note>original: "aquæ nostrę mercurialis"; the purified essence of Mercury.</note>, so that he may join to it its own body, which we previously called **ferment** <term>fermentum</term> and now call **poison** <term>venenum</term>. This is for two reasons: The first concerns us, because—not unlike a poison to the human body—it can bring nothing but harm if we do not join it at its determined hour in this place; it brings us nothing but loss and waste, as we said above. The second concerns the mercury (which we call mercurial water), because this "poison" slays it and **fixes** it <note>In alchemy, "fixation" is the process of making a volatile substance stable and solid so it can withstand heat.</note>. 

By this indeed is interpreted what **Haimech** <note>An authority frequently cited in medieval alchemical texts, sometimes associated with Arabic traditions.</note> wrote, saying: "When our matter reaches its limit, it is joined to its deadly poison." Likewise what **Rosinus** says: "This poison is of very great price." **Haly**, **Morienus**, and all the rest attest to the same. By the same comparison they called it **theriaca**, as the same **Morienus** <note>A legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist said to have taught the art to the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> says: "Because it performs the same thing in the body of metals that theriaca does in our own." Although what they wrote can also be adapted to the joining of the perfect ferment, when the determined hour has occurred, because through it our divine work...

<summary>This page explains that "poison" (venenum) is a symbolic term for the ferment added at a precise moment to "slay" and solidify the liquid mercury. It warns the practitioner that while this substance is necessary to "fix" the metal, it must be timed perfectly to avoid ruining the work.</summary>

<keywords>regimen of fire, poison, venenum, Theriaca, mercurial water, ferment, fixation, Morienus, Rosinus, Haimech</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 105 regarding the "limit" or "poison" used in the second operation. The author is explaining that alchemical terms must be understood allegorically, specifically focusing on the relationship between the "mercury" and its "rennet" or "ferment."</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS</header>

...is completed. Therefore, similar authorities should be understood in an allegorical sense, and not from the literal appearance of the words, as most have falsely believed. Such is the interpretation of this final term, which is more commonly used but less understood: for the greater part of people understand by "divine work" our work only when it is perfected. They say (not otherwise than how a little **rennet** <term>coagulum</term> <note>original: "coaguli"; the substance used to curdle milk, here used as a metaphor for the alchemical agent of fixation.</note> congeals a great deal of milk) that a tiny amount of our matter projected upon **quicksilver** <note>original: "viuum argétum"; liquid mercury.</note> congeals it and leads it to its own nature. But they are deceived, wandering from the truth: for by this they conclude that our matter cannot be compared to metals, because they are already congealed.

Furthermore, it must be known that when our mercury appears simple, it is volatile <note>original: "labilis"; literally slippery or gliding, referring to the elusive nature of mercury.</note>, and the Philosophers called it **milk** <note>original: "lac".</note>. They call its **rennet** <term>coagulum</term> that which we above called **ferment**, **poison**, and **theriaca**. Because just as rennet does not differ from milk except by a little **boiling** <note>original: "decoctione"; the alchemical process of heating a substance in a closed vessel to ripen it.</note>, our rennet also does not differ from our mercury except through the boiling which it previously acquired. This is a great and supernatural secret, for which reason the Philosophers said our science is divine, because human reasoning fails in it, as we have recounted above. This is the rennet that **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note> called the **flower of gold**, about which they wish to be understood when they say: *In the congealing of the spirit, the true dissolution of the body is made; and conversely, in the dissolution of the body, the true congealing of the spirit is made.* Because through its medium the whole work is completed, as **Senior** <note>Senior Zadith (Ibn Umail), a 10th-century alchemist.</note> says: *When I saw our water (that is, our mercury) congeal by itself, I firmly believed our science to be true.* For the same rea—

<meta>catchword: fam (likely referring to "fama" or "familiari" on the following page)</meta>

<summary>Zacharius explains the metaphor of milk and rennet to describe how purified mercury is "congealed" by a ferment of its own nature. He emphasizes that alchemy is a divine science where the spirit and body are dissolved and congealed through one another.</summary>
<keywords>coagulum, rennet, mercury, quicksilver, ferment, Hermes Trismegistus, Senior Zadith, decoction, divine work, flower of gold</keywords>

<header>->SMALL TREATISE<-</header>
<note>original: "OPVSCVLVM."</note>

For Alexander writes that in our science nothing is
created, except that which is made from the male and the
female. He calls our **rennet** <term>coagulum</term> <note>The agent used to "fix" or solidify volatile substances.</note> the male,
because it is that which acts, and the Philosophers
attributed action to the male, and passion <note>In the sense of "being acted upon" or receiving an impression.</note> to the female, whom
they say is our **mercury**, for the reason that the aforementioned
rennet acts upon it, demonstrating its own power.
For this reason they said that the female
has wings, because our mercury is simple and
volatile: yet it is retained by its said rennet.
For that reason they wrote: Make the female ascend 
above the male, and afterwards the male 
above the female, understanding this to be the same thing as in the 
**Assembly of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts.</note>: that our King is to be honored, 
along with the Queen his wife, and most diligently 
one must beware lest they be burned, that is, lest our 
**boiling** <term>decoctio</term> <note>The process of slow, steady heating in a sealed vessel.</note> be accelerated. For, as Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist.</note> says in 
his great **Rosary** <note>original: "Rosario"; refers to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*.</note>: The chief error in the practice of our 
divine work is an accelerated or hasty boiling. 
The ancient Philosophers used similar and varied terms 
in their writings, and because these which we have 
declared are the principal ones, we shall put an end 
to the interpretation of terms: for once these are 
rightly known, the true matter cannot fail to be 
recognized, and thereafter the books of the Philosophers 
are made easy, as the good Trevisan <note>Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century alchemist known for his clarity on the "one matter."</note> says. I shall conclude 
therefore with all the Philosophers, whose writings 
I have hitherto reduced into a better order than I could, 
that there is only one single matter, from 
which our divine work is fulfilled. 
For it is composed of only simple mercury (which 
the Philosophers called **mercurial water** <note>original: "mercurialem aquam"</note> by their 
own proper term, without any ambiguity).

<note>sig: H</note>

<summary>The author explains the male/female (rennet/mercury) allegory and warns that the most common mistake in alchemy is rushing the heating process. He concludes that all alchemical terminology points to a single, simple substance: mercurial water.</summary>

<page-num>108</page-num>

<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIUS</header>
<meta>catchword: lovers,</meta>

coagulated by the completion of its own sulphur (which
Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the founder of alchemy.</note> appropriately called the **flower of gold**) which,
by our long and continuous **boiling** <term>decoctio</term>, has acquired
such great and excellent perfection that this work of
ours is able to transmute all imperfect metallic bodies—
with which it is joined by **projection** <term>proiectio</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where a small amount of the "Philosopher's Stone" or elixir is cast onto molten base metal to transform it.</note>—into
pure and unadulterated gold similar to mineral gold. This
is for various reasons previously deduced and cited,
by which we have sufficiently and amply declared how
it happens that imperfect metals are perfected through it.
Since indeed no simple things that are different or
contrary in qualities can be joined or perfectly mixed
together, likewise our divine work—because it consists
solely of **animated quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum animatum</term> <note>Mercury that has been "awakened" or prepared through alchemical labor, rather than common quicksilver.</note>—
can in no way endure a union with the sulphur that
remained in the imperfect metals due to a lack of
**digestion** <term>digestio</term>, as we noted above. But truly, since
it is most powerful and most perfect through the highest
digestion, it separates the said sulphur from the metals,
and perfects the quicksilver that remains within them
into gold. It is proven by experiment: when we perform
a projection of it upon common quicksilver, we find
that almost all of it is converted into gold. We see the
opposite effect when projected onto [other] metals: for
from a half-pound of some, barely six ounces are
obtained <note>A loss of weight occurs because the "impurities" or "crude sulphur" are burned away or separated, leaving only the transformed mercury/gold.</note>; yet the more they have been boiled, the less
they are diminished in this way. Therefore, making an
end to this second part of my little treatise, I shall finally
enter upon the third and last part, in which I will explain the
true and perfect practice of our divine science under
various allegories. Which God will manifest to His faithful
and to the diligent **lovers**

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>LITTLE WORK <note>original: "OPVSCVLVM"</note></header>
<sig>H 2</sig>

...to the readers <note>The Latin text begins with the fragment "-toribus," concluding the word "lectoribus" (readers) from the previous page.</note>, who will diligently read through these writings of mine, giving true understanding through His Holy Spirit <note>original: "Spiritum S. [Sanctum]"</note>, to His glory and honor: to whom be praise forever.

## THE THIRD PART OF THE LITTLE WORK OF
## Dionysius Zacharius, concerning the Practice
## of the divine work.

<image-desc>Woodcut decorative initial V containing a human figure amidst scrolling floral and foliage patterns.</image-desc>THE WHOLE earth is divided by philosophers and cosmographers <term>cosmographos</term> <note>Scholars who studied the general features of the universe and earth, combining geography, astronomy, and natural history.</note> into three principal parts: into Asia, Africa, and Europe, situated under the four quarters or regions—East, West, South, and North—and under the dominion of various Emperors, Kings, Princes, and powerful Lords. Each of these possesses various and diverse things as delights, both on account of their rarity and their singular value; the latter cause is not equally pleasing as the former, which I have learned from experience while traveling through various and distant regions. Indeed, where there was a great crowd of learned men, I saw there (to my great misfortune) that the wise were exceedingly wretched and cast down; on the contrary, the most ignorant were very wealthy and held in the highest esteem. But where there was a scarcity of learned and skilled men, and the greatest part were utterly ignorant and inept, in that place the wise were most especially honored by everyone, and I observed that they were treated with the highest favor and greatest respect, particularly by the superiors <note>original: "maioribus," referring to those in high social or political standing.</note>. In no different way, the lack of wealth and minerals (from which gold and other metals are shared with us) in those places where they are not found, [is] most... <note>The sentence ends in a fragment "maxi-" and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the second part of Zacharius’s alchemical treatise and begins the third, shifting into a narrative about his travels and the social status of learned men across Europe, Asia, and Africa.</summary>
<keywords>Dionysius Zacharius, Practice of the divine work, geography, social status of scholars, precious metals, travel, Asia, Africa, Europe</keywords>

<header>DIONYSIUS ZACHARIAS</header>

...total estimation of these things. For abundance brings with it a disregard for whatever the wealthy possess in great quantity; and the cheapest things, or those of no value, having nothing of perfection except in outward appearance, have always blinded their eyes, so that they could not recognize what is truly perfect and precious.

For this reason, the wise were filled with indignation when they saw that the ignorant were held in high regard <note>The Latin *praelatos* suggests those placed in high positions or preferred over others.</note>. They took themselves elsewhere, intending to demonstrate the strength and power of their wisdom in other lands. This was once done by a certain great-hearted Governor, who proposed never to stop until he had brought the rest of the world—beyond that which he already ruled—under his control, aided by the protection of his resources and especially by the counsel of his faithful steward <term>oeconomi</term> <note>original: *oeconomi*. Historically, a steward or manager; in alchemical allegory, this often represents a specific substance or "agent" that manages the transformation of the matter.</note>.

While he was turning these thoughts over in his mind, he took several different strangers into his service. Because these men were not at all faithful, they imagined they would be more generously received by Emperors, Kings, and other Princes if they betrayed him. Consequently, acting as spies do, they deserted him and made his plans known to those rulers. These lords made light of the news, believing that no earthly power could resist their own, much less the intentions of the aforementioned Governor. 

While, therefore, in the great Halls and Palaces, nothing was heard but laughter and singing, time was wasted uselessly on flirtations, masquerades <note>original: *laruis*, literally "masks" or "ghosts," referring here to courtly masked balls.</note>, horsemanship, dances, and various games. Favor was shown to flatterers and whisperers, while wise men were mockingly addressed by the title "philosopher"—a title which great Monarchs and Powers <term>Potentatus</term> of old did not disdain, nor would they do so now if they were still willing to listen to sound counsel as they once did. Meanwhile, that good Prince and Governor, surrounded by his own forces and auxiliary...

<summary>Dionysius Zacharias uses an allegory of a Governor and his faithful Steward to describe how true wisdom is ignored by the wealthy and the powerful, who prefer worldly distractions over the "philosophical" truths of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, Governor, Steward, alchemy, courtly life, wisdom vs ignorance, Dionysius Zacharias</keywords>

...or with their own forces, he had already besieged one of the most famous cities of the Empire. The Emperor, having conscripted a most abundant soldiery and accompanied by many Kings and Princes, was preparing even now to join his standards in conflict. However, the good Prince Governor—by the persuasion of his faithful steward <term>oeconomus</term> <note>original: *oeconomi*. The "steward" in alchemical allegory represents the agent or solvent that manages the transformation of the central substance.</note> and others of his house—abandoned the siege and safely withdrew alone into a most fortified citadel. Meanwhile, his forces occupied the surrounding fields, resisting the enemy vigorously day by day. 

The Emperor had brought fifty thousand foot-soldiers, six thousand horsemen, and innumerable engines of war <term>tormenta bellica</term> <note>original: *tormenta bellica*. These refer to heavy artillery or siege engines like cannons and catapults.</note>. Therefore, the Prince, forsaking the city which was defended by an iron tower, moved camp with his men in orderly military ranks. The rear-guard fought strenuously behind him against the enemy. Had they crossed into the river right then and looked out for themselves and their Prince by breaking the bridges, the greatest danger would have loomed over them; yet they all escaped the hands of the enemies unharmed. 

The next day, as the enemies pursued them manfully, the Prince was compelled—assenting to the counsel of his followers—to withdraw into an impregnable place, and all the soldiery went with him. This place was fortified by a valley and ramparts, in the middle of which sat a never-conquerable, perfectly round citadel atop a rock, surrounded by walls. Next to it stood another very high tower, through which the steward was able to introduce everything necessary for both food and war by way of tunnels excavated underground <term>cuniculis</term>, while the enemies remained unaware. 

This was similar to what once occurred at Nauplia <note>original: *Neapoli Romaniae*. Literally "Naples of Romania," the historical name for the Greek city of Nafplio, a Venetian stronghold.</note>, while Suleiman, the King of Turkey, besieged it for twenty years and could not perceive from where the supplies of food and other necessities reached the city. The Prince, however, having received his men within the ramparts, withdrew into the citadel, and into a small round cham...

<summary>The Governor (an alchemical allegory for the prime matter) retreats from a massive imperial army into a perfectly round, impregnable citadel. He is sustained by a faithful steward through secret underground passages, representing the protected environment of the alchemical vessel.</summary>

<keywords>allegory, Governor, steward, siege, engines of war, Suleiman the Magnificent, Nauplia, round citadel, underground tunnels, protection</keywords>

...to remain <note>original: *per-dum*. Likely a continuation of a verb like *perdurandum* (to endure) or *permanendum* (to remain) from the previous page.</note>, it was also most highly decorated with every individual thing worthy of such a Prince, and in this place he remained for the entire time that the siege lasted. He took the greatest delight in that refuge, for it had been skillfully constructed, just as one may see in the Duchy of Lorraine <note>The author, Denis Zacaire, often uses French geographical references to ground his alchemical allegories in the real world.</note>. From there, through four windows, every scheme the enemies devised to intercept him was visible to him. Truly, they were unable to enter, because the principal door of his residence was closed in such a way that no one was strong enough to open it, except for the faithful steward <term>oeconomus</term> <note>original: *oeconomum*. In alchemical symbolism, the steward is often the "secret fire" or the specific solvent that alone has the "key" to open the vessel (the door) and interact with the matter (the Prince).</note>, who arranged and provided for every detail so prudently that nothing was lacking for an entire year of the siege.

On account of the various and most harsh assaults made daily by the Emperor's soldiers, the Prince was forced to divide his army into five troops <note>These "five troops" likely represent the four elements plus the quintessence, or five specific stages of the chemical "regime" required to stabilize the matter against the "heat" of the furnace.</note>, so that each might keep watch in its turn. The leaders of the Emperor's army persuaded him in this manner not to move camp: "If we leave the Prince," they said, "he will deservedly mock us, especially because he was once of our own rank; he might say he withdrew from us for the reason that he was being treated with less dignity than he deserved. Therefore, if he escapes our hands, he will seek every opportunity for vengeance."

These and similar words moved the Emperor so that he deliberated with a completely stubborn mind to take the Prince captive through starvation, or by any other way he could. But as winter approached, having withdrawn a portion of his camp for a while, the Emperor left the remainder in the siege under the leadership <term>ducatu</term> of a most powerful Governor <term>satrapae</term> <note>original: *Satrapæ*. A high-ranking official or viceroy. In alchemy, this often represents a specific mineral or planetary force—perhaps Saturn—assigned to maintain a steady, lower heat during the "winter" of the process.</note>, who made many attacks daily against the soldiers of the good Prince. The Emperor's plan was that his men should not move camp before a full year had passed. When the Prince understood this—

<summary>The alchemical "Prince" successfully withstands a year-long siege within a fortified, four-windowed stronghold, managed by a faithful steward, while the "Emperor" plots to starve him out.</summary>

# THE LITTLE WORK
### H 4

-> <page-num>113</page-num> <-

Through his **steward** <term>oeconomus</term> <note>The "steward" is the vital agent, often interpreted as the secret solvent or the fire of the philosophers, who manages the transformation of the matter.</note>, he commanded his troops that each one should bring back a banner seized by force of arms from the Emperor’s army, under threat of the penalty of his greatest indignation if they failed to carry out his orders. But if they succeeded, he promised a massive reward for everyone; furthermore, he promised to come forth in his own person, accompanied by his faithful steward, and strike a blow against the enemy so vigorously that he would either lose his life or bring back the Emperor's primary banner. He would then enrich with spoils all those who followed him, setting them above all his enemies who were besieging him. 

Therefore, with their courage renewed, before the appointed time had slipped away, as many of the Emperor’s standards as he had desired were brought to him by his troops, by means of the **doubling of the circle** <note>original: *duplicationis circuli*. Likely an alchemical reference to a specific cycle of distillation or the "squaring of the circle" meant to stabilize the matter.</note> which a certain **Prince of France** <note>Denis Zacaire, the author, was French; this may refer to a specific alchemical school or authority he respected.</note> had taught his steward.

*   The **first banner** belonged to the black German knights, whom they call the **Black Riders** <note>original: *Reuthorum atrorum*. In alchemy, this represents the *nigredo* or black stage, the initial "death" and putrefaction of the matter.</note>.
*   The **second** was shot through with various colors <note>This represents the *cauda pavonis* or "peacock's tail," a stage where many colors appear in the vessel.</note>.
*   The **third** was not so very different from the banner of the King of France <note>Presumably blue and gold.</note>.
*   The **fourth** was marked with a **crescent Moon** <note>This represents the *albedo* or white stage, associated with silver and the moon.</note>.
*   The **fifth** was truly similar to the greatest sign of the Emperor <note>This represents the final *rubedo* or red stage, the perfection of the Philosopher's Stone, associated with gold and the Sun.</note>.

This indeed increased the Prince’s courage, so that on the following day he went out to the walls with his steward and there fought vigorously beyond measure, until he obtained the greatest Imperial banner. Returning at last from the fight and exhausted, he was refreshed by the steward with the initial foods from the start of the siege that were left over. With his strength restored, on the next day he leaped manfully upon the enemy with his men and his steward; with the help of his generals, who all belonged to their Prince, he partly laid low and partly put to flight the entire army of the Emperor...

<summary>The Prince (alchemical matter) commands his troops to capture five banners representing the stages of the Great Work, culminating in a successful sortie against the Emperor's besieging army. This allegory describes the transition from the black stage (nigredo) through the multicolored and white stages to the final red perfection (rubedo).</summary>
<keywords>five banners, oeconomus, Black Riders, nigredo, albedo, rubedo, doubling of the circle, Prince of France, alchemical allegory</keywords>

-> DIONYSIUS ZACHARIAS <-

<note>original: "pis coloribus" — likely "typicis coloribus"</note> ...they were illustrated with these symbolic colors. From this, it followed that every Great Power—whether it be the Pope, or the Emperor, or a King, Prince, or even the **Sultan** <note>original: *Solimanus*, referring to the Ottoman Sultan, symbolizing the greatest worldly power of the East.</note>, however many there are—must yield the palm of victory to this one **Prince Gold** with the greatest applause. This is by God's command and will, to whom be praise, honor, and everlasting glory. It remains for us to declare how the **projection** <term>proiectio: the act of casting the alchemical tincture onto base metals to transform them</term> of our divine work upon metals, the tinting of gemstones, and the medicine for the human body are contained within it.

### *On the projection upon metals.*

When the strength of our **King** <note>In this context, the "King" refers to the completed Philosopher’s Stone or the perfected Red Elixir.</note> has been multiplied and refreshed with food, let one ounce be projected upon four ounces of pure, melted gold. From this, a brittle material will be formed; once this is pulverized, let it be cooked in a **closed mountain** <note>original: *monte clauso*. This refers to a sealed furnace or the "Philosophical Egg" (a glass vessel) that keeps the spirit from escaping.</note> with the heat of the "final assault," the vessel being very tightly sealed, for a period of three days. 

Let one ounce of this powder be projected upon twenty-five marks of silver or copper, or upon eighteen marks of lead or tin, or upon fifteen marks of common **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: mercury</term> heated in a crucible or congealed with lead. The material will soon be covered with a thick foam; when the operation is complete, a cracking sound will occur, as if the crucible itself were breaking. Finally, let this material be melted, and it will be converted into gold. 

However, if the stated weight was not observed, or the material did not change from its previous color, it must be refined in a large **cupel** <term>cineritio: a vessel made of bone ash used to refine precious metals</term> without lead for the space of three hours, so that the material which was not transmuted may be consumed. That which remains pure at the bottom must be further purified for six hours with **royal cement** <term>cimento regale: a mixture of salts and minerals used to strip silver and other impurities from gold</term>. By this path, one shall obtain total gold.

<meta>catchword: works</meta>

<summary>Dionysius Zacharias concludes his allegory of the Prince and provides specific technical instructions for using the Philosopher's Stone to transmute base metals like lead, tin, and mercury into pure gold.</summary>
<keywords>projection, transmutation, Philosopher's Stone, cupellation, royal cement, alchemy, gold, mercury, Denis Zacaire</keywords>

...converted by the works of our great King, similar to and equivalent to any mineral. And this is the way taught by Raymond Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher. Many alchemical texts were attributed to him after his death, forming a body of work known as "Pseudo-Lullian" alchemy.</note> in his *Little Codex* <note>original: *Codicillo*</note>, regarding the **projection** <term>projection: the final stage of the Great Work, where the alchemical medicine is cast upon base metal to transform it.</term> The second way, for the **tincture of gems**, he teaches in his *Testament*.

### *On the Tincture of Gems and Pearls.*

Our great King <note>In this context, the "King" refers to the prepared alchemical substance or the Philosopher's Stone.</note> must be washed and refreshed immediately after the white sign marked with the waxing moon is brought to him; the end of the siege <note>Alchemical "siege" refers to the long period of heating or digestion in the furnace.</note> is not to be expected, but rather from the first feeding of his refreshment. This is the **mercury**, which Raymond Lull called **exuberated quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum viuum exuberatum*. This refers to mercury that has been "animated" or purified through several distillations to make it more potent.</note>. Let two or three ounces of this be placed over ashes in a small, well-closed **alembic** <term>alembic: a two-part distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling flask and a cap with a spout to collect vapors.</term> Let them be distilled first with a slow fire. When nothing more wishes to drip at this heat, the receiver should be changed and sealed with clay, and whatever can ascend should finally be driven off with a stronger fire.

This second liquid should be distilled in a fresh alembic using a **water bath** <note>original: *balneum Mariae*, or "Bain-Marie," a vessel of hot water used to provide gentle, even heat to the glass flask.</note>. A third time, **cohobate** <term>cohobation: the process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto the solid remains left in the flask and re-distilling it.</term> the liquid over the viscous dregs, and they will be dissolved in their own time by the water. On the third time, however, let the whole be distilled over ashes, and afterwards, let it be distilled four times more in a fresh alembic by a water bath, keeping the dregs separate each time, until the distilled water is clear and shining with the white brilliance of pearls. 

Its use here is for **pearls** <note>original: *vniones*. In classical and alchemical Latin, this specifically refers to large, high-quality single pearls.</note>. Let these, even if they are very small, be placed in a small **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation or digestion.</term>, over which enough of the said water is poured so that it stands slightly above them. Let it be covered with a **blind head** <note>original: *galea caeca*, a still-cap without a spout, used when the chemist wants the vapors to circulate and fall back down rather than be collected.</note>. After three hours, they will be resolved into a white paste, and a very clear liquid will float on top; let this be poured off gently so as not to disturb it. The cucurbit with the paste...

...let it be placed, covered, in a **Mary's bath** <term>balneum Mariae: a water bath used to provide gentle, even heat for chemical processes</term> for three days of **digestion** <term>digestion: a process of gentle heating to encourage chemical change</term>, and afterward let it be removed. Let a round silver mold be fashioned, gilded on the inside, which can be opened down the middle into two equal parts. A gold or silver-gilded wire should be fitted between the two cavities. Finally, with a golden spatula, let both halves of the mold be filled with the aforementioned paste. The wire is to be placed through the middle of its opening, and the mold closed. The wire should then be pushed back and forth so that the **pearl** <term>vnio: a large, unique pearl; here, a man-made one</term> is well-pierced through its center. Once the mold is opened, let the formed pearl be removed and placed in a golden dish covered by another similar one. It must not be touched by hand. Let it be dried in the shade, not in the sun. When you have several of these, let them all be strung on a wire and placed in a glass tube that has a wide opening on one side and a hole no larger than the gold wire on the other. Suspend them together in a **flask** <note>original: *bocia*</note> or a clean glass **urinal** <note>A common shape for alchemical vessels, resembling a medical urine-collection flask.</note> above the aforementioned sublimated liquid. Seal the vessel with **lute** <term>lute: a clay-based paste used to seal laboratory apparatus airtight</term> and keep it in the air for eight days, then in the sun for three days. Move the glass every three hours so that the vapor rises. By this artifice, you will perfect as many pearls as you desire. In the same manner, **rubies** and **carbuncles** <term>carbuncle: a deep red gemstone, usually a garnet cut in a cabochon shape</term> can be made using the **red mercury** <note>The "red mercury" refers to a more advanced state of the alchemical substance compared to the "white" mercury used for pearls.</note> after it has been refreshed once.

<header>LITTLE WORK.</header>

...it will be cured in a day; if the disease has lasted one month, it will be cured within twelve days; or if it has lasted a year, within a month, even in cases where the disease would otherwise have been very long-lasting. To preserve health, a small amount of it should be taken twice every year, at the beginning of spring and autumn. By this means, one lives healthily and with vitality until the predestined end of life, God granting, to whom be praise, honor, and glory now and always, forever. Amen.
