# SOME
## NOTES FROM
### NICHOLAS
### FLAMEL, A
### FRENCH AUTHOR.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'A' featuring intricate floral and foliage patterns.</image-desc>
For a true knowledge of metals, it must not be ignored that while they still exist in their first forms—namely in **sulfur** and in **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the common name for mercury</term>—they possess two forms: one masculine, the other feminine. These two seeds of the male and female (initially of the sulfur) contain two masculine elements: fire and air. Subsequently, the other two feminine elements are contained: earth and water. These are compared in the **Art** <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Great Work.</note> to two **serpents**, one winged and terrible, the other indeed lacking wings and much more horrible. If these two seeds are joined by nature in the belly of **mercury** <note>original: *mercurii*</note>, which because of its heat is called the **fire of nature** <term>ignis naturae: the internal, creative heat that drives alchemical transformation</term>, and by some the **devouring lion** <note>original: *leo deuorans*; a common alchemical symbol for a powerful solvent or the "green lion" that consumes metals</note>, and its belly, although

<meta>catchword: although</meta>

<summary>This page concludes Dionysius Zachary's treatise with instructions on using the Philosopher's Stone as a universal medicine for longevity before introducing Nicholas Flamel’s theories on the masculine and feminine "seeds" of metals.</summary>
<keywords>Dionysius Zachary, Nicholas Flamel, universal medicine, longevity, sulfur, quicksilver, masculine and feminine principles, alchemical serpents, fire of nature, devouring lion</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical notes of Nicholas Flamel. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the seeds of the male and female (sulfur and mercury) joining in the "belly" of mercury. This page explains how mercury acts as the mother or tree from which the "fruit" of gold and silver grows.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>

...gold and silver enter into it, they are not consumed, because they are harder and more perfect by nature than it. Mercury is imperfect, yet common gold and silver come forth from it. Silver is the substance of the lower **moon** <note>In alchemy, the Moon (Luna) always represents Silver.</note>, just as gold is the substance of the lower **sun** <note>The Sun (Sol) always represents Gold.</note>. Therefore, it is necessary for the mother, **mercury**, to be killed and to die through the aforementioned seeds; once it has been **mortified** <term>mortificatus: the alchemical process of 'death' or breakdown, often resulting in a blackening of the matter, necessary before it can be 'reborn' in a higher state</term> by nature, it can never again return to life. For even if it is only slightly congealed within the veins of the earth, there is soon present within it a fixed grain of gold, which produces the true sprout of mercury from the two seeds.

As can be seen in the caverns of lead, where there is no mineral to be found anywhere that does not clearly contain a true grain of **fixed** <note>A "fixed" substance in alchemy is one that can withstand the heat of the fire without evaporating.</note> metal—that is, a grain of gold or silver. Indeed, the first congealing of mercury is the mineral of lead, in which it has been placed by nature. This can truly be **multiplied** to its perfection without doubt or error, provided it remains in its mercury and is not separated from its ore. For a metal existing in its own mineral is mercury; if the grain is separated from it, it will be like an unripe apple taken from its tree <note>original: "urbe," which means city, but context suggests a typo for "arbore" (tree) to match the apple metaphor</note>, which is then utterly destroyed. 

The fixed grain is like the apple, and mercury is the tree. The fruit, therefore, must not be separated from the tree, because it cannot receive nourishment from any other source than its own mercury. It is a great foolishness to place gold and silver into mercury, no differently than trying to re-attach a plucked apple to a tree. Therefore, so that this work may be completed properly, the tree must be taken together with its fruit, so that it may be planted anew—without removing the fruit—into more fertile and noble earth...

<meta>catchword: rema [likely the start of "remanet" or "remanebit" on the next page]</meta>

<summary>Nicholas Flamel explains that gold and silver grow within mercury like fruit on a tree. He warns that the alchemist must not separate the "fixed grain" (the metal) from its "tree" (mercury) during the process, as the metal requires the mercury for nourishment and perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Nicholas Flamel, mercury, gold, silver, lead, mortification, fixed grain, alchemical metaphor, transmutation, seed of metals</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "philosophical garden" and the growth of metals. <page-num>119</page-num>
<header>ANNOTATIONS. 119</header>

...thing, which will give more nourishment in a single day than the former field would have provided in a hundred years, because of the constant agitation of the winds. Another earth, because it is close to the sun, causes a tree to grow and thrive with continuous dew and the constant sun in the **philosophical garden** <note>original: *horto philosophico*; a symbolic space representing the alchemical process where substances are cultivated like plants</note>, shining morning and evening, day and night, and every single hour without interruption; and the tree is sprinkled and nourished by the sweetest **dew** <note>original: *ros*; refers to the condensed 'mercurial water' or steam within the vessel</note>, more in one year than in ten thousand in the previous earth. 

Therefore, when both are placed in a **furnace** <term>furno: the 'athanor' or alchemical furnace designed to maintain a steady, gentle heat over long periods</term> of continuous heat day and night, its vapor, which is the dew, prevents combustion. The heat from wood or coal is useless—indeed, it is harmful. Thus, the wise Philosophers did not wish to work with anything other than nature herself, along with the **moon** <note>In alchemy, the Moon (*Luna*) represents Silver</note> herself, in the mother suited to mercury. From which they finally produced a general mercury, far more powerful and stronger than that natural kind. 

Ascend, therefore, into the mountain, so that you may see the vegetable, saturnine, royal, and mineral herb. For its juice is taken pure once the dregs have been cast aside. Indeed, from it you will be able to complete the greatest part of your work. This is the true **mercury of the Philosophers** <term>mercurius Philosophorum: the essential, refined 'spirit' or solvent of the work, distinct from common liquid quicksilver</term>. 

First, then, do not wash your mercury, but join it with its father—that is, with fire—upon the ashes, which are the straw, in a single glass vessel, without anything else, which is the nest, in the furnace, which is the house. From the nest a chick will be born, who will heal all people with its blood <note>This allegory describes the birth of the "Philosopher's Stone." The "chick" is the refined matter, and its "blood" is the power of transmutation and healing.</note>.

### The Fountain of Chemistry.

The **fountain** <note>original: *Fons*</note> and cavern of all metals is the mother, from whom those seven are born and proceed as if from her own nature: likewise **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>,

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>OF NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>

Mars and the Moon. Lead, intellectually speaking, is **leprous gold** <note>original: *aurum leprosum*; alchemists believed lead was essentially gold that was "diseased" or incomplete, needing to be "healed" or purified into perfection</note>, etc. But the fountain of nature, if it receives the mercury placed within it, will from that point produce a most excellent medicine. A mercurial mass of lead is born in the earth, which, after being shut in a prison, should later be set free; otherwise, the weight and measure which is desired cannot be known. However, you will be able to know the method for it. 

The mercury of the Sun <note>Gold</note> is not like the mercury of the Moon <note>Silver</note>, since the latter must always remain white so that it may procreate its like. But the mercury of the Sun must be **reddened** <note>original: *rubificandus*; the process of "rubedo," or the final reddening stage of the alchemical work</note>, and this is the first labor; afterward, they can be gathered together. 

It must be noted that the **quintessence** <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth essence" or purest spiritual core of a substance, beyond the four elements of earth, air, fire, and water</term> is especially necessary to us in this art, and that this mercury is found most particularly in that place where the sun is in its greatest heat and produces many flowers; for after the flowers, the fruits follow. 

In a similar way, it is found in the Moon when sought within her; in Saturn <note>Lead</note>, Mars <note>Iron</note>, Venus <note>Copper</note>, and more safely in Mercury <note>Quicksilver</note>. But I (says Flamel) found and worked it in the Sun. Therefore, when these individual parts come together in this **philosophical work** <note>the *opus philosophicum* or Great Work of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, where each is similar to the other, then the offspring devours the mother and suffocates its father in a single lake, in which flowers, milk, fruit, and blood are found all together. 

Therefore, this one metal must be placed in a single vessel and in a single furnace. It is Mercury of which I speak, and nothing else. To shorten the work, there is one single metal in this world in which this mercury of ours is found in abundance. Gold is hot and dry, but the Moon is of a cold nature. Saturn is heavy and soft; for this very reason, being similar to the sun, it is called **leprous gold**. Venus is similar to the Moon in weight and malleability; Mercury is cold...

...warm and moist, like Jupiter, who is born from it; Mars, however, is hard, heavy, and cold. In these two things, therefore, this whole business consists: namely, that by means of fire you make your **sulfur** <term>sulfur: in alchemy, the active, masculine, and combustible principle of a substance</term> penetrative and attractive, so that it may be able to devour its own mother; then you will have completed the work. Graft the mother into the belly of her offspring, whom she brought forth before; then, finally, he will be a father to himself, and a son made into a perfect spirit from the two. After your sulfur has been devoured and its **mercury** <term>mercury: the passive, feminine, and fluid principle</term> has been **mortified** <note>original: *mortificatus*; the process of "killing" or chemically altering a substance's outward form to release its inner essence</note>, let it be cast into a prison. There let it remain for forty days, and you will see what you desire, according to these verses:

> *If you dissolve the fixed, and make the dissolved fly,*
> *And if you fix the bird, it will make you live in safety.*
> <note>original: *Si fixum soluas, faciasq́; volare solutum, Et volucrem figas, faciet te viuere tutum.* This is a classic alchemical maxim: to turn solids into vapors (volatilization) and vapors back into solids (fixation).</note>

->*Jean de Meung, On the Lamentations of Nature.*<-

What is contained beneath the Moon is called "heaven" in this place, because matter desires form, no differently than a woman desires a man. Matter is composed only of the four elements, out of which **chaos** is made, and from that chaos the King and Queen are generated. First, indeed, fire was created by God, which He placed beneath the Moon; it is free from corruption and contains within itself the purest part of the **quintessence** <term>essentiæ quintæ: the "fifth essence," a celestial substance superior to the four earthly elements</term>. Afterward, the subtle air was made, in which God likewise placed a portion of the quintessence—though not as much as in fire; there is less in water, and in earth the very least. Therefore, just as fire acts upon the air, so the air acts upon the water and the earth. Earth is the mother and the nourisher of all things which she conceives in her womb from fire: she fosters this very thing, even into her very center, without ceasing. Since, therefore, nature alone has the power of reducing the four elements into a **first matter** <term>primam materiam: the original, undifferentiated primeval substance from which all things are made</term> generally mixed with any elements, it would be impossible for any operator to achieve this except for nature herself, who alone provides the matter and the nature.

<page-num>122</page-num>
# NICOLAS FLAMEL

For the **fifth essence** <term>fifth essence: also known as the "quintessence," it is the purest, incorruptible element that binds the four physical elements together</term>, which is called the **golden chain** <note>original: *cathena aurea*; a reference to the "Golden Chain of Homer," symbolizing the connection between the divine/celestial realm and the material world</note>, connects and harmonizes opposites in such a way that they may never disagree again.

One must first consider gold, which receives its most beautiful form in heaven and its best matter in the earth; the same is true of gems. Therefore, heaven informs the fifth essence with the form and matter contained within the element, through which the form is held fast; this is perfected through purification and the passage of a long time.

Thus, gold and gems are composed by me, Nature, alone; for no one besides me knows the correct proportion. That which you seek is indeed the highest secret of nature, descending from celestial power. For this reason, the whole of science is owed to Nature, by which the simplicity of the element is drawn down from heaven. In heaven, this is called the "primitive essence," and in the element, it is called the "fifth," which Nature herself produces through reductions and prolonged **circulations** <term>circulation: a process where a liquid is heated, turned to vapor, and condensed back into a liquid repeatedly to achieve extreme purity</term>, by converting the lower into the higher, and preserving the cold and dry within the moist and warm. She preserves stones and metals beneath their **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture: the fundamental, life-giving fluid or "sap" believed to reside within the heart of all matter</term> through the celestial movements of the stars, by which the elements are governed and whose influences they obey: so that the purer the matter is, the more excellent the work produced by nature becomes.

Celestial motion is therefore the corresponding intelligence in lower things, in which, through its influences, it brings all things into being. Nature therefore uses matter in an unfailing fire of any kind, because from that it receives all its power. Likewise, the water proceeding from a dry origin, which wets nothing that it touches, and is neither retreating nor volatile, is the **incombustible oil** <note>original: *oleum incombustibile*; a legendary alchemical substance representing a permanent, spiritual fire that cannot be extinguished or consumed</note>. No artificial fire could infuse as much heat as that which comes from heaven. But in

It introduces the concept of "incombustible oil" and the superiority of heavenly heat over man-made fire in the alchemical process.</summary>

<page-num>123</page-num>

### ANNOTATIONS.

One must not be ignorant of these things: this entire work of ours is made from a cheap, homogeneous <term>homogeneous: consisting of parts all of the same kind; here meaning a single starting substance rather than a complex mixture</term> matter, enclosed in a single vessel, and contained in a single furnace which brings it to completion. For it is perfected by **governance** <term>governance: the careful management of heat and time during the alchemical process</term> alone, by imitating nature.

In the generation of a human being, Nature forms the body with great subtlety from **quicksilver** <note>original: *argẽto viuo* (argentum vivum); in alchemy, this refers to the fluid, "feminine" principle of matter, not just the metal mercury</note> and an active masculine **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphure*; the "masculine" principle representing heat, dry action, and form</note>. This occurs in a single vessel—namely, the contained **womb** <note>original: *matrice*</note>—whose furnace is the belly. Although a man assists through his art when he is burning with his own nature by providing the appropriate matter, he can perform nothing else beyond this.

It is no different in our art. If one knows how to choose the kind of matter in which nature delights, and concludes that matter, rightly prepared, in its vessel and furnace, "I," says Nature, "and he [the artist] shall reach perfection without delay." This is provided that he administers the required fire: a fire that is **natural**, **against nature**, **not natural**, and without burning <note>This paradoxical "fire" refers to different types of alchemical heat: physical fire, chemical acidity, and the internal heat of the matter itself</note>. It is a fire, I say, that is hot, dry, moist, and cold. Consider, therefore, what kind of fire this must be; for without fire and matter, nothing is accomplished in this science.

"I give you the matter," says Nature; "the form is what you prepare for yourself." I do not speak here of a substantial or accidental form, but of the construction of the vessel and the furnace. Next, prepare your matter, reduced into a powder, with its own **water** <note>likely referring to a solvent derived from the same substance</note>, and enclosed in a single vessel. Govern it with a tempered heat; this heat will promote the action, while the coldness will promote **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: the "death" or breakdown of the matter, usually turning it black, which is necessary before it can be reborn as a higher substance</term>.

Indeed, because of the great coldness, the dryness cannot prevent it, nor can it resist this agent, so that the quicksilver is immediately reduced into the **first matter** <term>first matter: *prima materia*; the original, formless state of substance from which all things are created</term> through an integral connection. Therefore, before you work, Nature says, "Learn to know me, for I am the Mother, without whom no creature can be brought forth."

Without me, who provides the matter, you will never accomplish anything; and without you, who serves as the attendant, I cannot carry out this work alone. The substance is similar to gold, but the essence is dissimilar. By converting its elements, you will find what you seek: by sublimating <term>sublimating: the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used here to mean "refining" or "raising" the spiritual quality of the matter</term> the lower things, and by converting the higher things into the lower. Take, therefore, this **quicksilver** <note>original: *viuum argentum* (argentum vivum)</note> mixed with active **sulfur**, and enclosed in a single vessel and a single furnace; let the vessel be buried up to a third of its height, and let it be kept over the **philosophical fire**. Let every other species be cast aside, except for the matter alone from which the mineral takes its origin.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding the nature of matter and form. Having established the relationship between Nature and the Artist on the previous page, the text now warns against the futile attempt to use finished gold as a starting material, emphasizing that once nature has perfected a substance, it cannot be "unmade" to create something new.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>

# ANNOTATIONS

...changes, so that it may inform the matter with a new form. For the **object** <note>original: *obiectum*; here used in the scholastic sense of the thing acted upon</note> is the subject in **potentiality** <term>potentiality: (Latin: *potentia*) the capacity of matter to take on a new form or state of being</term>, because it supports the substance in **act** <term>act: (Latin: *actu*) the fully realized, completed state of a thing</term>, which was previously in potentiality. **Gold** is known to be the treasure of all minerals, yet it has neither the form nor the matter of such great potentiality that it might exceed its own perfection: for it has no greater power than that of perfecting itself, no matter what the craftsman strives for. To destroy it and reduce it back to its [primal] being would be a foolish work, since from it nothing more of virtue or power can be obtained than what it possesses from its own nature. No reduction takes place of those things which nature has perfected into a species or an individual, unless they are first **corrupted** <note>original: *corrumpantur*; in alchemy, "corruption" is the necessary decay or breakdown of a substance before it can be reborn or "generated" into a higher state</note>. Even after corruption, **generation** does not become like the species unless there is a return to this genus. Therefore, the destruction of gold does not pave the way to constructing it, because nothing can be constructed through its own destruction: for once it is dead, its substance likewise dies, so that no more **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum viuum*</note> or metal can be born from it. And because the element of fire is contained in the earth, and air in the water—the quantity, quality, and proportion of whose perfect conjunction no one knows—the elements must not be separated, since they cannot be repaired. Therefore, only that which nature has generated and left to us is to be taken for the art. In summary, the separation of elements is not effectively done for quicksilver, nor for the **sun** <note>original: *solis*; the alchemical symbol and name for gold</note>, who is the father of the art, imparting spirit to his son.

Be solicitous, therefore, regarding the true mercury, which nature has generated, and which dwells prosperously with its father, and makes the imperfect metals prosperous.

### *The Testament of Arnaldus de Villa Nova.*

The **STONE** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311) was a famous physician and alchemist; his "Testament" is a classic text in the hermetic tradition</note>, generated and risen from the earth, is perfected through elevation, satiated by a draft of water. Within twelve hours, at the minimum, it is inflated and visibly swells all around. Afterwards, placed in a bath or in an airy **hypocaust** <term>hypocaust: (Latin: *hypocaušto*) a furnace or heating chamber, modeled after Roman underfloor heating, used here to provide steady, indirect heat</term> moderately hot and dry, and purified from foreign vapor, it acquires the solidity of its parts; and having been thinned of its superfluous moisture, it becomes fit for mixing. When this is done, the **virgin's milk** <note>original: *lac virgineum*; a symbolic name for the pure, white mercurial liquid that appears during the refinement process</note> is squeezed out from its purer parts. Which, soon after being placed in the **philosophical egg** <term>philosophical egg: (Latin: *ouum philosophicum*) a round or oval glass vessel that is hermetically sealed to allow the matter to circulate during heating</term>, should be fostered by its own continuous heat, just as for the hatching of chicks. Stripped of the variety of its colors, it rejoices with its partner, tinged with a color like snow. Thereafter, it resists the violence <note>The word "violence" (original: *violentiæ*) is split by the page break</note> of fire without danger—

[the completion] of the divine philosophical work consists in these two operations, namely in **solution** and **coagulation**. **Solution** <term>solution: (Latin: *solutio*) the process of turning a solid into a liquid; in alchemy, this often represents the "death" of the material's old form</term>, therefore, according to the opinion of **Geber** <note>Geber: the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the 8th-century father of chemistry</note>, is the reduction of a dry thing into water.

### THE STAIRWAY OF THE PHILOSOPHERS.
<note>original: *Scala Philosophorum*; a well-known alchemical treatise that describes the stages of the Great Work as steps on a ladder</note>

The solution of the **stone** <note>the "Stone" here refers to the matter of the work at any stage, not just the final Philosopher's Stone</note> is its reduction into mineral water.

### VILLANOVANUS.
<note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note>

We dissolve (he says) when we divide bodies; we make them simple and subtle, and resolve them into their first nature and matter.

### AVICENNA *concerning the first matter.*
<note>Avicenna: the Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and chemistry were foundational in Europe</note>

The **first matter** <term>first matter: (Latin: *materia prima*) the formless, original substance from which all things are created</term> is twofold: proximate and remote. The proximate is **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: (Latin: *argentum vivum*) mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals</term>; the remote is water. Because quicksilver was first water, and thereafter quicksilver.

<insert>Raymond</insert> - ### LULLY.
<note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315); many alchemical texts were attributed to him, though he likely did not write them</note>

Dissolution has its primary aim to destroy [the substance] with the **lunaria** <term>lunaria: literally "of the moon"; likely referring to a specialized alchemical solvent or the silver-based stage of the process</term>.

### THE SOUND OF THE TRUMPET.
<note>original: *Clangor Buccinae*; an influential 14th-century alchemical text</note>

The **first matter** cannot be obtained except through artificial operations, which are contrary to natural operations.

### AN UNKNOWN AUTHOR.

How difficult a thing dissolution is, those who have applied themselves to solution can testify. Many, through their unskillfulness, have been unable to reach it. He who dissolves otherwise than through our **artifice** <note>original: *artificium*; refers to the specific, secret techniques of the alchemical "Art"</note>, therefore, dissolves in vain.

### THE CLANGOR.
<note>Likely referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* (The Sound of the Trumpet), a 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>

Solution is a work of nature, and not of art: the craftsman is only nature's servant and helper.

### AVICENNA.
<note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and chemistry were foundational to European science.</note>

Just as water is the prior and closer matter of the stone, so the other matter of the stone is the spirit of metals.

### THE ROSARY.
<note>original: *Rosarius*; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), one of the most famous illustrated alchemical texts.</note>

You who desire to investigate the secret of this art, it is necessary for you to know the **first matter** of metals, otherwise you will be frustrated in your labor.

### VILLANOVANUS.
<note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist.</note>

Dissolution is the perfection of wisdom: for it is necessary that wisdom itself be sought; otherwise it will not be found. Therefore **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed</note> said: Man cannot have, nor find dissolution, except through the fire of divine genius.

### AVICENNA.

The true beginning of our work is the dissolution of the stone. The same author says: The first governance <term>governance: (Latin: *regimen*) the method of directing the alchemical fire and process</term> of the stone is to dissolve the stone, and to reduce it into its first nature.

### VILLANOVANUS.

Because the first step of the operation is that **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury (Latin: *argentum vivum*)</term> may be made from them.

### THE CHILDREN’S GAME.
<note>original: *Ludus Puerorum*; a title used for several alchemical treatises, suggesting that once the secret of the work is understood, the process is as easy as a child's game.</note>

The craftsman of our work must first dissolve the stone, then coagulate it: since our work

<header>ANNOTATIONS.</header>

## THE LADDER OF THE PHILOSOPHERS
<note>original: *Scala Philosophorum*; a well-known alchemical treatise often attributed to various authors, describing the stages of the Great Work.</note>

The solution of the stone is the revelation of what is hidden, the thinning of density, and the reduction of a hard and dry thing into a liquid substance.

## VILLANOVA
<note>Referring to Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>

Gold and silver are found in the inner recesses of the earth as hard, dry, and most tightly bound. However, through artificial dissolution, they are made soft, flowing, and a liquid substance. The same author says: Nature hides the <term>tincture: the essential soul or color-shifting quality of the stone</term> within its own body, which art makes manifest by "tormenting" and "twisting" the body, sometimes by water, and sometimes by fire. Also: Let it not weary you to torment and corrupt the body by grinding and soaking it until you have extracted the water, and the body has been made thin and impalpable to the touch.

## THE CLANGOR
<note>Referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* (The Sound of the Trumpet), an influential alchemical text.</note>

The bodies of metals are dissolved by dissolution.

## VILLANOVA

The <term>Water of the Philosophers: a specialized solvent, often called "mercurial water," used to break down metals without destroying their essential nature</term> dissolves metals kindly and naturally, for it is joined with them by a bond that is neither unwelcome nor contrary. The same author says: Within the Water of the Philosophers there is a moist power, possessing a property that "twists" the body without causing it loss or destruction.

## BERNARD
<note>Likely Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a nobleman and alchemist.</note>

Man and gold are generated through art in a similar fashion; nevertheless, the sperm and seeds of these cannot be generated by art, because art does not know how to compose that specific proportion of mixing required to produce seeds. <note>Bernard is arguing that while the alchemist can facilitate the "growth" of gold, the original "seed" or life-force remains a mystery of nature that the artist can only imitate or assist.</note>

## THE ASSEMBLY OF PHILOSOPHERS.
<note>original: *Congregatio Philosophorum*; likely referring to a collection of aphorisms from various alchemical masters.</note>

Nature completes that step which it is able to complete, but not that which it cannot surpass. Another says: Nature contains within itself those things which it needs: but it is not brought to perfection unless it is moved by art and by operation. Likewise: The first dispositions of the stone can be induced by no art; nature alone can do this. Likewise: The matter of the stone is made by nature. To make the matter through any artifice is impossible. For the stone is found created by nature, nor does it need anything else except that what is superfluous in it be removed—that is, in its matter. <note>The alchemist is often described not as a creator, but as a "handmaid" to nature who simply clears away impurities so the natural process can finish.</note>

### AVICENNA.
<note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were foundational, though many alchemical works attributed to him are now considered "Pseudo-Avicenna."</note>

The stone does not bear fruit until it comes to nothing, like running water.

### THE ASSEMBLY.
<note>original: *Turba*; referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of ancient sages.</note>

Our whole <term>mastery: the *magisterium*, or the total process of the Great Work</term> is done with our water; from it, and of it, all necessary things are made. For it dissolves bodies with a true and philosophical solution, so that they may be converted into the water from which they were in the beginning. Elsewhere: Unless everything is turned into water, there is no way to reach perfection.

### ALPHIDIUS.
<note>A common authority cited in medieval alchemy, likely an Arabic-to-Latin translation of an author named al-Fididi.</note>

Unless you thin the natures, making them into water, you can color nothing: therefore, make the bodies into water by liquefying them, which is called the "water of wisdom" among the Philosophers.

### THE ROSARY.
<note>original: *Rosarius*; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a highly influential 16th-century anthology of alchemical quotes and illustrations.</note>

Permanent water <note>original: *aqua permanens*; a central alchemical concept for a solvent that does not wet the hands but dissolves metals</note>, or perennial water, or burning wine, is called the "water of the body"—that is, when the body

Dissolve the perfect body into mercury; from there you will have the hidden virtue, that is, the **philosophical sulfur**, digested and cooked through the work of nature, just as it is digested and cooked in the mine.

### CHILD’S PLAY.

It is necessary for our stone to be reduced to the **first matter**, and to the origin of sulfur and mercury.

<header>ANNOTATIONS.</header>

...that the purest substance might be drawn out and chosen from the sun and moon. <note>original: *ex sole & luna*; in alchemy, the "Sun" refers to gold and the "Moon" to silver.</note>

### THE CLANGOR
<note>Likely referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* (The Sound of the Trumpet), an influential alchemical text.</note>

Extract the <term>quicksilver: *argentum vivum*, the liquid metal mercury</term>, or the philosophers' stone, from both the [metallic] bodies and from the quicksilver itself: because they are of one nature, and you will have the mercury and the sulfur from that matter upon the earth from which gold and silver are generated within the earth.

### COMMON OPINION
<note>original: *Communis Sententia*; refers to a widely accepted axiom among alchemical practitioners.</note>

The Mercury of the Philosophers consists of quicksilver and sulfur.

### BERNARD
<note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist who spent his life and fortune seeking the Stone.</note>

Gold <note>original: *Sol* (the Sun)</note> is nothing other than sulfur and quicksilver. He says that they attribute Earth and Water to mercury, and Air and Fire to sulfur. Earth and water are the passive elements, while air and fire are the active. Earth and water have the nature of the woman, and air and fire have that of the man; nor is there any other external addition except for <term>digestion: the process of maturing a substance through gentle, steady heat</term>, by which means all four elements are encompassed within the gold and are ripened in equal proportion.

### COMMON OPINION

The corruption of one is the generation of another, and this holds true for artificial things as well as natural ones. Alternatively: According to nature, nothing is generated which is not first corrupted, and nothing rises again which has not first died.

### BERNARD

If gold is cooked in quicksilver, or dissolved in the proper manner and by the natural path, the quicksilver itself obtains the natural properties of gold... <meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence at "obti-", likely the beginning of "obtinebit" (will obtain).</meta>

<meta>This page continues the "Annotations" or "Notes" section regarding Nicolaus Flamel. <page-num>136</page-num>
->NICOLAUS FLAMEL<-
<meta>catchword: tionem (the end of "generationem" or "corruptionem")</meta>

...tion. The same author says: The <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</term> which is performed by the water of the Philosophers destroys the exterior form of the dissolved bodies, but it does not destroy the matter.

### ARNALD OF VILLANOVA
<note>Arnaldus de Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>

That which dissolves the body is from the spirit; however, that which <term>coagulates: the process of thickening or solidifying a liquid</term> the spirit is from the body. Elsewhere he says: The spirit dissolves, the body coagulates. The same author says: Insofar as the body is dissolved, so much is the spirit coagulated. For if the body were not dissolved, the spirit would not be thickened. Elsewhere: That which exits from the dissolved body is a radiant moisture; that which dissolves the body proceeds from the spirit, and its power is <term>germinating: original: *germinosa*; implying the ability to sprout or grow like a seed</term>. The same author says elsewhere: The solution of the body occurs alongside the coagulation of the spirit, and the coagulation of the spirit alongside the solution of the body. Then the body is mixed with the spirit and becomes one with it, and they are never separated, no more than water mixed with water.

### THE CLANGOR
<note>Short for *Clangor Buccinae* (The Sound of the Trumpet), a 15th-century alchemical text.</note>

When the seed <note>original: *sperma*</note> of mercury is joined with the earth of the body, the body is dissolved into the water of the seed, and it becomes one water without any division.

### ARNALD OF VILLANOVA

Understand, moreover, that the solution of the body and the coagulation of the spirit happen simultaneously and at the same time.

### THE ROSARY ACCORDING TO CALID
<note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers) citing the legendary Arab alchemist Calid (Khalid ibn Yazid).</note>

The solution of the body and the coagulation of the spirit are two things, but they have a single operation. Because the spirit is not coagulated except through the solution of the body, nor is the body dissolved except through the coagulation of the spirit. Therefore, between the solution of the body and the coagula-

<summary>This page explores the alchemical axiom that dissolving a solid (body) and solidifying a liquid (spirit) are two sides of the same simultaneous process. It cites authorities like Arnald of Villanova to explain that this union creates an inseparable substance.</summary>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Annotations" section, concluding a discussion on the interaction of spirit and matter before citing three major alchemical authorities—Ramon Llull, Roger Bacon, and Bernard of Trevisan—who all warn against using harsh mineral acids (like nitric acid) for the "Great Work."</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>ANNOTATIONS.</header>

...tion <note>original: *tionem*; likely completing "solution" or "generation" from the previous page</note> of the spirit, there is no difference, nor is there a different work in anything, so that one is made without the other. Just as we see happening when ordinary water tries to make earth more subtle and dissolve it into its own matter: for at the same time the water becomes denser and thicker, while the earth becomes more subtle and rarer.

## LULLY
<note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note>

Many of the Philosophers dissolved the bodies of metals in common <term>aqua fortis: literally "strong water"; nitric acid</term>, made from <term>vitriol: a sulfate mineral</term> and <term>saltpeter: potassium nitrate</term>. They then drew off that water through evaporation; then a <term>calx: the powdery oxide or residue left after a substance has been heated or corroded</term> remained at the bottom of the vessel, which they afterwards washed with common fresh water. Then they removed that water through evaporation and dissolved the calx with a <term>menstruum: a specialized alchemical solvent</term> to complete their works—which, as it happened, did not achieve the desired end. For the dissolution that is made with aqua fortis is not as nature requires. The same author says elsewhere: Gold and silver are dissolved in radical things of their own kind <note>i.e., substances that share the same essential "root" or nature as the metals</note>, but not with strong waters, which corrupt the metals, because those waters are repugnant to nature.

## ROGER BACON
<note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), an English friar and scientist renowned for his work on nature and alchemy.</note>

Nothing adheres to metals, nor is joined to them, nor transmutes them, except that which is of their own kind.

## BERNARD
<note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), an alchemist who spent his life and fortune searching for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

The foolish make corrosive waters from minerals by dissolution, into which they put species of metals and corrode them; they do not dissolve them by a natural <term>solution: the process of turning a solid into a liquid</term>, which requires the permanence of both—namely, the solvent and the dissolved substance. <note>In "natural" alchemy, the solvent and the metal should merge into a new substance without the metal's "soul" being destroyed by harsh acids.</note>

<summary>This page features a collection of warnings from famous alchemists against using corrosive mineral acids, arguing that true transmutation requires "natural" solvents that preserve the essence of the metal.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Roger Bacon, Bernard of Trevisan, aqua fortis, dissolution, calx, corrosive acids, natural solution, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Annotations" section of Nicholas Flamel’s work, specifically focusing on the concept of "dissolution"—the process of breaking down a substance into its liquid state. It follows the previous page’s warnings against using harsh, non-metallic acids, providing further citations from respected alchemical authorities.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL.</header>

**The same author:** <note>original: *Idem*; referring back to Ramon Llull from the previous page.</note> No water dissolves a metallic species through a natural reduction, except for that water which remains with the metallic species in matter and form, and through which the metals themselves can be <term>re-congealed: returned from a liquid state to a solid state</term>. This is not the case with <term>strong waters: corrosive mineral acids like nitric acid</term>, which do not belong to the dissolved bodies. Indeed, bodies are corroded by strong waters, but they are not truly dissolved. Furthermore, the more they are corroded, the further they are alienated from their metallic nature.

-> *ARNOLD OF VILLANOVA* <-
<note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>

Through dissolution, the body is mixed with the spirit and becomes one with it, so that one is never again separated from the other.

-> *THE CLANGOUR* <-
<note>original: *Clangor*; likely referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* (The Clangour of the Trumpet), a well-known alchemical text.</note>

The body is dissolved into the water of the seed, and from that, it becomes one water without any division.

-> *THE PHILOSOPHER* <-
<note>In alchemical texts, "The Philosopher" usually refers to Aristotle, but here it serves as a general citation of ancient authority.</note>

A voluntary dissolution is better than a violent one, and a temperate one is better than one that is too hasty: and therefore, one must take care lest the generative power of the <term>form: the internal organizing principle of a substance</term> be suffocated by corrosive and burning substances.

-> *THE ROSARY* <-
<note>original: *Rosarius*; referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a seminal 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>

In our stone, the <term>Sun: gold</term> and the <term>Moon: silver</term> are alive, and they can produce other suns and moons from themselves. Other gold and silver [different from ours] are dead: for their force and power remains in the dissolution made by our water. Any other water would have destroyed them and rendered them sterile.

-> *ARNOLD OF VILLANOVA* <-

Dissolution only occurs by means of <term>quicksilver: mercury</term>; for it alone can [bring] the sun and moon back to their first...

<summary>This page presents a collection of authoritative quotes arguing that true alchemical "dissolution" must be a natural, gentle process using substances of the same nature as the metals, rather than violent destruction with harsh acids.</summary>
<keywords>dissolution, Nicholas Flamel, Arnold of Villanova, Rosarium Philosophorum, quicksilver, gold and silver, alchemical theory, strong waters</keywords>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>ANNOTATIONS.</header>

...to reduce the matter. **The same author:** <note>original: *Idem*; referring to Ramon Llull from the previous page.</note> The whole benefit of this art consists in mercury, <term>the sun: gold</term>, and <term>the moon: silver</term>.

### ARISTOTLE
<note>Aristotle was widely regarded by alchemists as the foundational authority on the composition of matter and the four elements.</note>

If we received nothing else from mercury except that it refines bodies to its own nature, it would provide us with no small benefit.

### GEBER
<note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a foundational figure in early chemistry and alchemy.</note>

The perfection of every <term>dissolution: the process of turning a solid substance into a liquid</term> is achieved with subtle waters, and especially those that are sharp, pungent, <term>astringent: "pontic," or having a harsh, drying quality</term>, and containing no dregs; such as distilled vinegar, sour grapes, very bitter pears, distilled pomegranates, and similar things.

### VILLANOVA
<note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311) was a Catalan physician and a prominent alchemical author.</note>

The nature of the dissolved body and the dissolving water is one and the same, except that the nature of the body is complete, <term>digested: matured or fully processed by heat</term>, and <term>fixed: resistant to the action of fire</term>; whereas the nature of the water is incomplete, undigested, and <term>volatile: easily evaporated or changed by heat</term>.

### REGOR
<note>This name likely refers to an alchemical authority, possibly a variation of "Roger" (referring to Roger Bacon) or a corruption of an Arabic name.</note>

<term>Quicksilver: mercury</term> <note>original: *Argentum vivum*</note> is a fire that burns, mortifies, and shatters bodies.

### ROSINUS
<note>Rosinus is the Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century Egyptian alchemist whose works were central to the tradition.</note>

Water is as much hotter than fire as fire is hotter than water.

### THE ROSARY
<note>Refers to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rosary of the Philosophers), a highly influential 16th-century alchemical text.</note>

Our water is stronger than fire, because it makes a pure spirit out of the body of gold, which fire cannot do. **The same author:** The spirit is entirely fiery, dissolving and dividing bodies through its fiery nature.

### THE TURBA
<note>Refers to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between ancient philosophers.</note>

Burn our <term>bronze: or copper</term> <note>original: *æs*</note> with a most powerful fire.

<header>[WORKS OF] NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>

### CALIDUS
<note>Calidus refers to Khalid ibn Yazid, an Umayyad prince credited with being one of the earliest patrons of alchemy.</note>

A gentle heat wears everything down, but it accomplishes less than our water, which dissolves the body in a way that fire alone cannot achieve.

### LLULL
<note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and mystic to whom many alchemical works were later attributed.</note>

It is known that the <term>prime matter: the fundamental, original substance needed for the alchemical work</term> of our Stone consists of the nature of <term>living mercury: also called quicksilver</term> and sulfur; for this matter cannot be destroyed by fire, water, or air.

### ROSARIUS
<note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers) is a seminal 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>

Some have claimed that <term>common quicksilver: ordinary mercury found in nature</term> bonds with metallic bodies, but this is false. When Geber says, "In our searching, we have found nothing that aligns more closely with the nature of bodies than quicksilver," he is referring entirely to <term>philosophical mercury: a refined, spiritualized form of mercury believed to be the "soul" of metals</term>. Only this philosophical mercury truly bonds with the metals. Neither the ancient nor the modern philosophers will ever find another substance that joins with bodies in this way, except philosophical mercury. Common quicksilver does not bond with bodies, and because of this, many seekers are misled by attempting to work with the common quicksilver found in shops.

### LLULL

Those who attempt dissolution using <term>aqua fortis: literally "strong water," referring to corrosive mineral acids like nitric acid</term> are ignorant of the <term>vegetable: in alchemy, substances derived from "living" or organic growth</term> components that sharpen our <term>menstruum: a solvent used to extract the essence of a substance</term>. They are also ignorant of the water of common mercury which I described in my work, the *Testament*.

### VILLANOVA
<note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>

For nature to change and transform another nature, it requires only that which is of its own kind. Furthermore, that which
<meta>catchword: dissolves</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>ANNOTATIONS.</header>

that which dissolves, and that which is dissolved, are of one and the same kind and nature. The same author says: There is no true or philosophical solution <note>original: *solutio*, the process of turning a solid into a liquid</note> of the body, nor a coagulation of the spirit, if any foreign matter or nature is present during the dissolution.

### DASTIN
<note>John Dastin (c. 1293–1386) was one of the most famous English alchemists, known for his correspondence with Pope John XXII regarding the legitimacy of alchemy.</note>

It is necessary that the elements of the water be of the same nature as the elements of the metal that is to be transformed; otherwise, the offspring will be degenerate. This is because the diversity of the whole is born from the diversity of its parts. Similarly, a likeness exists between those things which are of one genus and sprout from a single root.

### THE ROSARY
<note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century collection of alchemical texts.</note>

The Philosophers’ Stone is made from three things: namely, the <term>sun: representing gold</term>, the <term>moon: representing silver</term>, and mercury. Likewise: Create mercury from the sun and moon in its own essence, without the mercury of the common people <note>original: *mercurio vulgi*</note>, but rather by the philosophical path.

### LLULL
<note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher whose name was attached to many influential alchemical works.</note>

The Stone is composed of the <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> of metals, not from the quicksilver of the common people.

### THE NEW LIGHT
<note>Usually refers to *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (The New Chemical Light), published in 1604 and attributed to Michael Sendivogius.</note>

Our quicksilver is not silver in its own natural state, nor is it in the state to which it is brought by the mine, but rather it is in the state to which our craftsmanship has led it.

### RICHARD
<note>Likely Richard the Englishman (Richardus Anglicus), an alchemist often identified as a 14th-century physician.</note>

Raw mercury prepares bodies into the <term>first matter: the fundamental substance before it takes a specific form</term>, but the mercury extracted from bodies cannot do this. The same author says: Because through <term>congelation: the thickening or solidification of a substance</term>, that raw sulfur which was in it before (that is, before the solidification) is altered in its nature, and therefore it does not corrode like the first.

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>ON NICOLAS FLAMEL, PHILOSOPHER, IN GENERAL.</header>

Do not seek cleanliness from the unclean. It is foolish to seek from a thing what was never in it. Who will give what he does not have? What madness is it to wish to make from the inferior that which is made better; that is, to make perfect things from the imperfect? <note>Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a French scribe who became a legendary figure in alchemy; this passage warns that the "seed" of perfection must already exist in the starting materials.</note>

->LLULL<-
<note>Ramon Llull, a Majorcan philosopher whose name was frequently used as a pseudonym by alchemical writers.</note>

The heaven, the <term>lunaria: a symbolic "moon-plant" or liquid associated with silver</term>, the <term>menstruum: a solvent, named after the monthly cycle</term>, the vegetable liquor, and the water of quicksilver <note>original: *aqua argenti vivi*</note> rectified and sharpened with vegetables—it is with this that the natural dissolution of metals occurs.

->VILLANOVA<-
<note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>

Waters of solution made from salts, alums, and others of that kind <note>original: *id genus*, referring to common mineral acids</note>, which have no kinship with metals, do not preserve the species of metals; on the contrary, they destroy and scatter them. Opposite to these waters is the Water of the Philosophers, which preserves metals, makes them more subtle, then dissolves them, and by a great agreement of nature, transfers them into the <term>First Matter: the original, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made</term>.

->THE TURBA<-
<note>Referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the earliest alchemical texts translated into Latin.</note>

Take the black spirit that does not burn, and with it dissolve and divide the bodies.

->CLANGOR<-
<note>Referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* (The Blare of the Trumpet), a 16th-century alchemical work.</note>

In the Water of the Philosophers lies hidden the spirit of the <term>quintessence: the "fifth essence" or purest part of a substance</term>, whose role alone is to expertly corrupt and transform things into their own First Matter.

->THE ROSARY<-
<note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum*.</note>

The juice of the lunaria is made from our wine, which is known to few of our sons <note>original: *filiis nostris*, a standard term for students of alchemy</note>, and with it our solution is made, and our approved gold is produced, by means of...

### LLULL
<note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan polymath. Alchemical works attributed to him emphasize the use of "menstruums" or specialized solvents.</note>

The <term>menstruum: a philosophical solvent, named for its cyclical or life-giving properties</term> cannot generate the philosopher’s stone without gold and silver, nor vice versa. Where those are joined together, the solvent moves the metals toward dissolution. In turn, the metals move the solvent to produce the fire within the stones.

### THE CLANGOR
<note>original: *Clangor Buccinae*, or "The Sound of the Trumpet," a famous 15th-century alchemical treatise.</note>

If there is one pound (twelve ounces) of the imperfect body—that is, of the earth—it is fixed by its own <term>imbibitions: the process of repeatedly adding liquid to a solid until it is saturated</term> over ninety days.

### LLULL

In the unique water of the prepared red body—that is, of our prepared red earth <note>The "red earth" or "red body" typically refers to the purified state of the matter, often associated with gold or the final stage of the Work.</note>, shaped into thin plates or reduced to a powder of filings—place two drachmas, which is the fourth part of one ounce. If you are able to dissolve more, add more; however, do not exceed one ounce. If any of the earth remains undissolved, leave it in the glass vessel; set aside what has been dissolved. Afterward, let the earth that remained undissolved be placed with new material to be dissolved. Of this new earth, do not add two drachmas, but less: specifically, as much as the remaining earth weighs. Understand this, unless you perceive that the water is capable of dissolving more.

### GEBER
<note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), the foundational figure of Arabic alchemy. Medieval European "Pseudo-Geber" texts focused heavily on laboratory precision.</note>

The method of solution is twofold: namely, by warm dung and by boiling water. These share one purpose and one effect. The method by dung <note>Dung was used as a natural, steady heat source for long-term digestion of materials.</note> is this: place the <term>calcined: reduced to a fine powder through high heat</term> material into a flask...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for dissolving "the earth" or "the body" (the base material of the Work) into a liquid state. It transitions from instructions attributed to Nicholas Flamel to two other authoritative texts: the "Semita Semitae" and Arnald of Villanova.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>
<note>original: "NICOLAI FLAMELLI." Nicholas Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a French scribe who posthumously gained a reputation as a legendary alchemist who discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

...glass vessel, and over that is poured a quantity of **distilled vinegar**, or double the amount of something similar. Let the top of it be well sealed so it does not breathe, and let it be left to dissolve in <term>warm dung: "sub fimo tepido," a common alchemical method for providing gentle, steady heat by burying a vessel in a fermenting manure pile</term>. Afterwards, let what is dissolved be removed by <term>filtration: "per destillationem filtri," literally distillation by filter, referring to the wicking of liquid through a cloth</term>. What is not dissolved, however, should be <term>calcined: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term> again. After calcination, let it be dissolved again in the same way, until by repeating the work upon it, the whole is dissolved. 

The method of solution which is done by boiling water is faster: it is so arranged that the calcined matter is placed in a vial with vinegar, with the opening sealed so it does not breathe. Let this vial be buried in a kettle full of water and straw, and afterwards let a fire be lit under it until the water boils for an hour. After this, let the dissolved part be <term>distilled: in this context, to separate or drip out</term> and kept separately; let the undissolved part be calcined again, and dissolved again in the same order, until through reiteration the whole is dissolved.

### THE PATH OF THE PATH
<note>original: "SEMITA SEMITAE." A title often associated with alchemical works attributed to Albertus Magnus, detailing the "path" to the Stone.</note>

Take one pound of the <note>original: "pretij," literally "of the prize" or "of the price," referring to the valuable material being processed.</note> **prize**, with four pounds of water: grind and incorporate it with a little salt and vinegar, until the whole becomes an **amalgam**. Once the prize is excellently amalgamated, place it in a large quantity of the <term>water of life: "aqua vitæ," though usually meaning brandy/alcohol, the text here specifies it is mercury</term>, that is, of mercury. Place it over ashes, and let there be a very gentle fire for a <term>natural day: a full 24-hour period</term>. Let the vessel cool, and strain the water through a linen cloth, so that what has been dissolved from the body may pass through. What remains in the cloth, place back with new <term>blessed water: "aqua benedicta," a specialized alchemical solvent</term> over the fire for a natural day; strain as before. Repeat this until the entire body is converted into water.

### ARNALD OF VILLANOVA
<note>original: "VILLANOVANVS." Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311) was a celebrated physician and alchemist known for his works on wine and medicine.</note>

Grind one pound of unclean filings in a mor-

<header>ANNOTATIONS.</header>
<sig>K 4</sig>

...mortar, with four pounds of <term>mercury: "mercurij," the liquid metal used here as a base for the solution</term>. To that pound, give twelve pounds of <term>aqua vitae: "aquæ vitæ," literally "water of life," usually referring to concentrated alcohol or a strong solvent</term>. Put the whole thing in a <term>flask: "vrinali," a glass vessel shaped like a specimen bottle</term>, and place it over a weak and slow <term>ash fire: "ignem cinerum," a method of heating where the vessel sits in warm ashes to ensure a gentle, even temperature</term>, and cook it for a day. When the vessel has cooled, let the liquid be strained so that whatever has been dissolved from the "body" and the filings passes through with it; set that liquid aside. And that which is not dissolved, put back over the fire with new <term>blessed water: "aqua benedicta," a term for a specialized alchemical solvent</term> for a day and a night. Strain what is dissolved as before, and put this second liquid with the first. Do this until all the filings are converted into water—that is, into the <term>first matter: "primam materiam," the original, undifferentiated state of substance sought by alchemists</term>.

->*LULLIUS in the Light of Mercuries.*<-
<note>original: "LVLLIVS in luce mercuriorum." Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher; many alchemical works were pseudonymously attributed to him.</note>

Take one ounce of the purest <term>gold: "solis," literally "of the sun," the alchemical designation for gold</term>, and put that ounce into a glass vessel. Pour over it one ounce of <term>celestial menstruum: "cœlestis menstrui," a potent solvent, often a highly purified alcohol or acid</term>, or two at most, and immediately close the vessel so it does not "breathe" <note>i.e., so that no vapors escape</note>. Place the vessel in an ash fire for a <term>natural day: "diem naturalem," a full 24-hour period</term> to boil gently. By dissolving the gold, the solvent will be colored with a resplendent lemon-yellow hue. Afterward, once the gold is dissolved, place it in a <term>Mary’s bath: "balneo Mariæ," a double boiler or water bath used for gentle heating</term> for another natural day. Afterward, by pouring from one vessel into another, put the liquid colored in this way into a different vessel, and keep it aside in a sealed container. Then, over the gold which remained undissolved at the bottom of the vessel, pour more of the new solvent, as we said above. And finally, with the vessel closed, place it in the ash fire for another day. Then extract the dissolved portion by pouring it off, as was done before into the other vessel with the other dissolved gold. And so, with new solvent, repeat the aforementioned dissolution until the solvent is no longer colored; at the bottom of the vessel there will remain the <term>earth of gold: "terra solis," the physical residue or "body" of the metal</term> deprived of its <term>soul: "absque anima," the essence or color that has been extracted into the solvent</term>.

[...if you have dis]solved it, place it in a <term>flask: "ampulla," a glass vessel</term> which has a long neck. Pour <term>moon water: "aquam lunariam," a specialized alchemical solvent, often associated with silver or lunar properties</term> over it, so that it floats four fingers above. Place it upon the <term>ashes: "cineres," an ash-bath used to provide a steady, indirect heat</term>, where it may boil for a <note>natural day: a full 24-hour period</note>. Then place it in <term>dung: "fimo," horse manure, which alchemists used as a natural, low-temperature incubator due to the heat produced by fermentation</term> for two natural days, so that it may be better digested, and so the matter may be separated by its subtle parts from its grossness. Once the vessel is well-sealed and the evacuation is complete, pour over it another water similar to the first, which should float above as before, and place it in the dung. You shall repeat these operations until the body is emptied of its spirits.

The same author says: Take gold, and cast it into <term>vegetable water: "aquam vegetabilem," a solvent usually distilled from wine or plant matter</term>. <meta>This page continues the series of alchemical instructions regarding the refinement of metals. It transitions from the general procedures of the previous page into a series of "Annotations" or authoritative quotes from famous alchemical masters such as Geber, Ramon Lull, and Albertus Magnus, specifically focusing on the cycles of calcination (reduction to ash) and dissolution (liquefying).</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>ANNOTATIONS. 147</header>

### GEBER.

That which is not dissolved is <term>calcined: 'calcinatur,' reduced to powder through heat</term> again, and after calcination, it is dissolved again.

### THE COUNT. <note>original: 'COMES,' likely referring to the Count of Trevisan, a famous alchemical figure</note>

The work of <term>dissolution: 'dissolutionis,' the process of turning a solid metal into a liquid state</term> is heavy and difficult; however, it is expedient to dissolve bodies through water alone: that is, through <term>quicksilver: 'argentum viuum,' mercury</term> without calcination. This method is safer, even if it is slower in the operation; therefore, do not be in a hurry to extract the <term>tincture: 'tincturam,' the spiritual essence or 'soul' of the metal that provides its color and power</term>. Haste burns all things and leads one far from the goal. Endure powerfully, cook, and let it not weary you.

### ALPHIDIUS.

The multitude and diversity <note>of substances or methods</note> must be left behind, and a single <term>arrangement: 'dispositio'</term> must be sought.

### LULL. <note>Ramon Lull, a major figure in medieval alchemy</note>

When the <term>red earth: 'terra rubea,' a specific stage of the 'Great Work' where the matter takes on a deep red hue</term> has been purified and cleansed, and shaped into thin plates, it must be placed in water. There, it will not at first be dissolved into water, but will only be calcined, and reduced in a certain way into a red powder without being touched. Likewise, dry that earth according to its own method, so that no dust falls upon it, until you have brought another water in which it can be dissolved.

### THE LADDER. <note>original: 'SCALA,' referring to the text 'Scala Philosophorum' or The Ladder of Philosophers</note>

The <term>Sun and Moon: 'Sol & luna,' alchemical code for gold and silver</term> are calcined <term>philosophically: 'philosophicè,' meaning according to alchemical secrets rather than common chemistry</term> with the "first water," so that the bodies may be opened and become spongy and subtle, so that the "second water" may better enter to perform its work.

### ALBERTUS. <note>Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century philosopher and scientist</note>

Our <term>mercury: 'mercurius noster,' the specialized 'philosophical' mercury, distinct from common quicksilver</term> is made from perfect bodies, and—

<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>

...not from imperfect substances: that is, with the <term>second water: 'aqua secunda,' a specialized solvent used after the initial processing</term>, after they have been properly <term>calcined: 'calcinata,' reduced to a powder through the action of heat</term> by the first water.

### THE ROSARY <note>original: 'ROSARIVS,' likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note>

The fixed becomes volatile in the space of one month.

### THE CLANGOR <note>original: 'CLANGOR,' likely referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* or 'The Sound of the Trumpet,' another influential alchemical text</note>

In performing the <term>dissolution: 'dissolutione,' the process of liquefying a solid</term>, the earth must be ground so many times—that is, kept in the fire—and water must be added to it, until you clearly see that nothing more remains that can be dissolved.

### THE ROSARY

In the solution of the body, if there is more of the non-fixed substance—that is, of the <term>spirit: 'spiritu,' the volatile or vaporous element</term>—the solution will be faster than if there is more of the fixed—that is, of the <term>body: 'corpore,' the solid material</term>. If the opposite is true, the solution will be slower; therefore, for one wishing to dissolve things quickly, much water is needed. The same author says: It is necessary, however, that there be more of the volatile than the fixed, from double the amount up to fivefold, sixfold, or even tenfold, and no further.

### THE COUNT <note>Likely the Count of Trevisan</note>

In dissolving bodies, see that the white body is not mixed with the red, nor the red with the white, but let each be dissolved separately, and do not mix the water of one with the water of the other. <note>The 'white' and 'red' refers to the two main paths or stages of the Great Work: the *albedo* (whiteness) and the *rubedo* (redness).</note>

### LULL *in Natural Magic.* <note>Ramon Lull, a prolific medieval philosopher and alchemist</note>

If water fails you, take the <term>cucurbit: 'cucurbitâ,' a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used for distillation</term> in which all the liquefactions are, and place an <term>alembic: 'alembicum,' a cap or hood used in distilling to collect vapors</term> on top. Distill the water with a slow fire, namely by means of a <term>bath: 'balneum,' a water-bath or bain-marie used for gentle heating</term>, until you have two parts of the liquefaction. Of this water, place some upon the matter of the body, which is in the <term>vial: 'ampulla,' a glass flask</term>, in which the solution began to take

->ANNOTATIONS<-

...until it returns to its original quantity. And so you shall repeat the process until all the "earth" <note>the solid matter remaining after a reaction</note> is completely exhausted.

### ROGER <note>original: 'REGOR,' likely referring to Roger Bacon, a 13th-century philosopher and scientist often associated with early alchemy</note>

Care should be taken that the <term>vinegar: 'acetum,' a general alchemical term for a sharp, acidic solvent or spirit</term> is not converted into smoke and lost.

### ROSINUS <note>The Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, a 3rd-century Greco-Egyptian alchemist</note>

Be careful that your vinegar does not perish through evaporation.

### LULLY in the *Repertory* <note>original: 'LVLLIVS in Repertorio,' referring to Raymond Lull, a famous Majorcan polymath to whom many alchemical works were attributed</note>

Solutions of water are to be placed in a <term>bath: 'balneo,' specifically a water bath or 'bain-marie'</term> or in manure <note>rotting manure was used as a source of gentle, consistent heat</note>; from there, whatever can be distilled should be drawn out with a light heat. The distillation vessels must be tightly joined together so that they do not exhale anything outward. Of three parts of water, only two are to be summoned forth through distillation; the <term>water of the sun: 'aqua solis,' the essence or liquid form of gold</term> and the <term>water of the moon: 'aqua lunae,' the essence of silver</term> must be received separately. The glass vessel should be cast from stone rather than from flint: namely, so that it is not corroded by the biting and sharpness of the water. And lest the spirits vanish, those vessels are to be faithfully sealed. Then, water of this kind is more excellent in power and quality than it was before.

### THE LADDER <note>original: 'SCALA,' likely referring to the *Scala Philosophorum* or 'The Ladder of the Philosophers'</note>

He who does not dissolve and coagulate errs in many ways. Also: <term>Congelation: 'congelatio,' the thickening or solidifying of a substance</term> is the hardening of a soft thing, the hiding of moisture, and the binding together of diverse elements.

### THE CLANGOR <note>original: 'CLANGOR,' likely referring to the *Clangor Buccinae* or 'The Sound of the Trumpet'</note>

To dissolve is to convert the <term>body: 'corpus,' the solid or fixed material</term> into the nature of the spirit. To <term>coagulate: 'coagulare,' the process of turning a liquid into a solid</term> is to make the spirit bodily. Dissolution makes the fixed body <term>volatile: 'volatile,' able to be vaporized or raised by heat</term>. Coagulation, on the contrary, makes the volatile body fixed. The same author says: The spirit—that is, the water—is not coagulated, except by means of—

<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on alchemical dissolution and the relationship between solids (bodies) and liquids (mercury/water). It features a series of authoritative maxims from famous alchemical figures like Avicenna and Geber, focusing on the preparation of "salts" and the three fundamental materials of the Great Work.</meta>

->NICHOLAS FLAMEL<-

...the body, which is dissolved within it. For bodies are like the <term>curd: 'coagulum,' the solidifying agent that turns liquid to solid</term> of this milk—that is, of mercury. This curd, however, is either the sun <note>gold</note> or the moon <note>silver</note>, dissolved in mercury.

## MENABDES <note>A philosopher frequently cited in the 'Turba Philosophorum,' an early Latin alchemical text</note>

I command those who follow to make bodies into non-bodies, and incorporeal things into bodies: the former is achieved through dissolution, the latter through gentle <term>decoction: 'decoctionem,' the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</term>.

## AVICENNA <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina, a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were fundamental to medieval science</note>

Salts are the roots of the work. He also says: Salts of every kind are contrary to our art, except for the salt of our <term>lunaria: 'lunaria,' literally 'moon-plant,' referring here to a specific substance associated with silver or the lunar stage of the work</term>, which our lunaria has left behind from dissolved bodies.

## GEBER <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, considered the father of Islamic alchemy</note>

We require nothing but one vessel, one furnace, and one arrangement. Understand this to apply after the preparation of the first stone.

## THE COUNT <note>likely the Count of Trevisan</note>

When you have evaporated the water with great skill and the subtle wisdom of your mind, take your salt—thus <term>calcined: 'calcinatum,' reduced to a powder by thorough heating</term>—and the finest, impalpable powder, and store it cleanly in a glass vessel until it becomes necessary to add it to the work.

## THE ROSARY <note>original: 'ROSARIVS,' referring to the 'Rosarium Philosophorum' or 'Rosary of the Philosophers,' a famous 16th-century alchemical florilegium</note>

There are three stones and three salts from which the entire <term>mastery: 'magisterium,' the successful completion of the alchemical process</term> consists. The material of the stone is threefold: gold, silver, and philosophical water. There are three waters: solar, lunar, and mercurial; the last of these dissolves the bodies of the sun and moon into their own waters.

It highlights the transition of substances between solid (body) and liquid (spirit) states through controlled heating.</summary>
