<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on alchemical dissolution and the relationship between solids and liquids. It further categorizes the "mercury" described on the previous page into three distinct types and begins a series of citations from authoritative alchemical texts to define the "prime matter."</meta>

ANNOTATIONS. 151

The <term>mercury of the Philosophers: 'mercurius philosophorum,' the essential, living spirit of matter sought by alchemists</term> is threefold: the first calcinates bodies <note>reduces them to powder through heat</note>, the second dissolves and <term>sublimes: 'sublimans,' the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then solidifies again</term>, and the third is joined from these two.

## THE CLANGOR OF THE TRUMPET <note>original: 'Clangor Buccinae,' a famous 15th-century alchemical treatise</note>

<term>Quicksilver: 'argentum vivum,' or mercury</term> is twofold, and the names of the <term>primary matter: 'materia prima,' the original, formless substance from which all things are created</term> are many. The first is called "preparing" mercury, which dissolves the body through the "water of the Philosophers." The other is "tinting" mercury, which is extracted from perfected bodies <note>usually gold or silver</note> through dissolution; and such quicksilver is called "incombustible oil," "soul," "air," and the "splendor of bodies," because it grants incorruptible life to dead and imperfect metallic bodies and illuminates them.

### GEBER <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath considered the father of alchemy</note>

The principal natural things in the work are the "stinking spirit"—or <term>sulphur: in alchemy, the active, masculine principle representing heat and combustion</term>—and "living water."

### CLANGOR <note>Referring back to the 'Clangor Buccinae'</note>

The spirit is the "water of the Philosophers," the seat of the soul. The soul is the <term>tincture: 'tinctura,' the transformative essence or color-changing agent</term> drawn from perfected bodies. The water of the Philosophers is twofold, solar <note>associated with gold</note> and lunar <note>associated with silver</note>, from whose union is made the "Philosophical Mercury" and the <term>virgin’s milk: 'lac virginis,' a white liquid produced during the chemical marriage of substances</term>, from which comes the white and red stone <note>the two stages of the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

### TREVISAN <note>Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century Italian alchemist known for his search for the Stone</note>

Let all those remain silent who affirm that any other tincture besides ours is of any importance, as well as those who preach a sulphur other than our own, which is hidden in <term>magnesia: in alchemy, this refers not to the modern element, but to the 'raw' material or 'lead' of the philosophers</term>. Likewise, all those who attempt to extract a quicksilver from anything other than the <term>red servant: 'servus rubeus,' a cryptic term for a specific mineral or the 'earthy' part of the gold</term>, and any water other than ours, which is permanent and in no way joins itself...

<summary>This page defines the different types of philosophical mercury and quicksilver, emphasizing their roles in dissolving and reviving "dead" metals. It cites authorities to argue that the true Stone can only be produced from specific, "natural" primary materials like the stinking spirit and the red servant.</summary>

<keywords>mercury of the philosophers, quicksilver, primary matter, stinking spirit, sulphur, virgin's milk, red servant, Clangor Buccinae, Geber, Bernard of Trevisan</keywords>

...to another than its own nature, and it moistens nothing else except that which belongs to the union of its own proper nature. ### MICHAEL SCOT <note>A 13th-century mathematician and scholar often cited in alchemical texts as an authority on the nature of metals.</note>

Coagulated <term>quicksilver: 'argentum vivum,' or mercury</term> and sulfurous quicksilver are the primary matter of metals.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'C' in a square frame featuring foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>

It is most certain that no foreign substance can bring imperfect metals to perfection, or effect any transmutation from them. For this reason, it is the opinion of foolish men that animal and vegetable substances can accomplish anything in this art, since minerals are found to be [more] closely related to metals. Yet it is to be believed that the Philosophers described the art using those [terms] only through analogy <note>original: 'per similitudinem duntaxat'; a warning that alchemical recipes mentioning blood, eggs, or plants are usually metaphors for mineral processes</note>. For from only two things—namely, <term>sulphur: the active, masculine principle of heat and combustion</term> and quicksilver—do any metals arise, and not from other things. Nothing even adheres to them, nor transmutates them, unless it has taken its origin from them. It is clear, therefore, that only these two mentioned things are necessary for constructing the <term>stone: 'lapidem,' the Philosopher's Stone</term>. Nevertheless, no metal can be generated from either one in isolation, but from the conjunction of both, prepared in different ways, the various metals are produced. This, indeed, is the most hidden secret of all,

<summary>This page defines the "prime matter" of alchemy as a mineral union of sulphur and mercury, rejecting the use of organic animal or vegetable materials as "foolish." It introduces a collection of ancient authorities, starting with Michael Scot, to reinforce the theory that only substances sharing a metallic nature can perfect metals.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Scot, Sulphur-Mercury theory, primary matter, transmutation, Philosopher's Stone, mineralia, alchemical vinegar</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Collections from Ancient Writers" started on the previous page, further narrowing down the material requirements for the Philosopher's Stone by process of elimination. It moves from dismissing animal/vegetable sources to discussing why pure gold and silver, while perfect, cannot be used in their solid, metallic state without first being "reduced" to their principles.</meta>

### ANNOTATIONS.

...from which mineral substance it should more closely be made. If it were to be made from vegetables, it would first be necessary to produce <term>sulphur: the active, masculine principle of heat and "combustibility" in metals</term> and <term>quicksilver: 'argentum vivum' or mercury, the passive, feminine principle of fluidity and metallic shine</term> from them by art, which would be impossible. We are therefore excused from using those [vegetables], because nature prepares those two [principles] for us. 

But if we were to choose quicksilver and sulfur in their own nature, just as they are created naturally, it would be necessary to join them in a proportion that is unknown to all human ingenuity; we are therefore excused from those as well. This is especially true since we have "bodies" <note>In alchemy, 'bodies' usually refers to solid metals.</note> in which they are found already proportioned in the proper way by nature: in this, the whole secret lies hidden. 

Gold is a perfect masculine body, and silver is also perfect; if these could achieve perfection simply by melting and mixing with other imperfect metals, they would be <term>elixirs: substances capable of transmuting base metals into gold or silver</term>—one for the <term>white: the 'albedo' stage, producing silver</term> and the other for the <term>red: the 'rubedo' stage, producing gold</term>. But they are not of that kind, because they are simply perfected by nature. If, however, they were ten times or a hundred times "more than perfect," they would be able to perfect imperfect metals to that same degree. But because nature works only a simple perfection in them, they remain in that state always, unless perhaps by art they were reduced to the primary nature of sulfur and quicksilver. 

Since, therefore, perfect natural bodies consist of the purest quicksilver and a similar sulfur, why should we not choose them as our matter for the white and red elixir? The answer: Because these bodies are so compact and strongly united that we find that fire is hardly able to act upon anything in them, nor upon their spirits. And although we are not ignorant of fire, we still could not reach their innermost <term>purification: 'mundificatio,' the process of stripping away the external metallic form to reach the internal essence</term> by this means, on account of their extremely strong natural composition. For which reason we are also excused...

<vocab>sulphur, argentum viuum, aurum, elixir album, elixir rubeum, mundificatio, mineralia, corpora perfecta</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that while gold and silver are made of the correct principles (mercury and sulfur), they are too "compact" in their natural state to be used as a transmuting elixir without being first reduced to their primary essences. The author eliminates vegetable sources and raw elemental sulfur/mercury as viable starting points due to the impossibility of matching nature's proportions.</summary>

<keywords>sulfur, quicksilver, gold, silver, white elixir, red elixir, purification, mineral principles, perfection of metals</keywords>

-># ANNOTATIONS.<-

...from which mineral substance it ought more closely to be made. Yet if they were more-than-perfect by ten or a hundredfold, they would perfect the imperfect by that much. Truly, because nature works a simple perfection in them, they remain in that state forever, unless perhaps they were reduced by art to the <term>first nature: the original state of the matter before it became a fixed metal</term> of sulfur and quicksilver. It is most certain that no foreign substance is able to perfect imperfect metals <note>such as lead, tin, or copper</note>, or to effect any transmutation from them. Therefore, it is the opinion of insane men that animals and vegetables can accomplish anything in this art, even though minerals are found to be closely related to metals. Yet it is to be believed that the Philosophers placed the art in those [animal or vegetable substances] by way of analogy <note>original: 'similitudinem', meaning they used them as metaphors</note> only. For from only two things—namely, sulfur and quicksilver—any metals whatever arise, and not from others. Furthermore, nothing adheres to them, nor transmutates them, except that which took its origin from them. It is clear, therefore, that only the two things which have been mentioned are necessary for constructing the <term>Stone: the Philosopher's Stone</term>. Nevertheless, no metal can be generated from either one of these separately, but from the conjunction of both—prepared in different ways—different metals are generated. This, however, is hidden above all else...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "first matter" of the Great Work. Following the previous page’s assertion that only sulfur and quicksilver create metals, the author now systematically evaluates potential sources for these components, dismissing vegetable matter and the raw forms of the minerals in favor of a more nuanced approach to gold and silver.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
-># ANNOTATIONS.<-

...from which mineral substance it ought more closely to be made. If it were made from vegetables, it would first be necessary to create <term>sulfur: 'sulphur', the active, combustible, masculine principle in alchemy</term> and <term>quicksilver: 'argentum vivum' or mercury, the passive, fluid, feminine principle</term> from them by art, which would be impossible; we are therefore excused <note>The author uses 'excusamur' to mean we are justified in setting aside or spared the labor of using these materials.</note> from those, because nature prepares those two for us. But if we choose quicksilver and sulfur in their own nature, just as they were naturally created, it would be necessary to join them in a proportion that all human ingenuity is ignorant of; we are therefore also excused from these: especially since we have bodies in which they are found already proportioned by nature in the proper way: in which the whole secret lies hidden. Gold is a perfect masculine body, and silver is also perfect; if they could perfect [other metals] by simple melting and mixing with other imperfect ones, they would be <term>elixirs: 'elixira', substances capable of transmuting base metals into noble ones</term>, one for the white and the other for the red <note>The 'white elixir' transmutates metals into silver, while the 'red elixir' transmutates them into gold.</note>, but they are not of that kind, because they are simply perfected by nature. If, however, they were "more-than-perfect" by ten or a hundred times, they would to that degree perfect the imperfect ones. Truly, because nature simply works a simple perfection in them, they always remain in that state, unless perhaps they were reduced by art to the first nature of sulfur and quicksilver. Since, therefore, perfect natural bodies consist of the cleanest quicksilver and a similar sulfur, why should we not choose those for our matter for the white and red elixir? The Answer: Because these bodies are so compact and strongly united that we find fire is hardly able to act upon them, nor upon their spirits. And although we are not ignorant of fire, we still could not reach their innermost <term>purification: 'mundificatio', the process of cleansing the matter of its impurities</term> through it, because of their very strong natural composition. For which reason we are also excused [from them]...

<vocab>sulfur, quicksilver, elixir, gold, silver, nature, purification, perfect bodies</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that while gold and silver are the most "perfect" forms of sulfur and mercury, they are too tightly bound by nature to be easily used as the Elixir's starting material. The author systematically rejects vegetable matter and raw minerals, leading toward the necessity of "reducing" perfect metals to their primary state.</summary>

<header>OF NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>

...from the choice of those two bodies <note>Referring to gold and silver, mentioned on the previous page.</note>, since we are able to find other bodies in which clean **quicksilver** and a similar **sulfur** are contained, upon which nature has worked only a little. Thus, by our artificial fire—through the appropriate <term>cleansing: 'mundificatio', the process of purifying a substance</term>, separation, and <term>fixation: 'fixatio', making a volatile substance stable under heat</term>, and by an ingenious <term>decoction: 'decoctio', a process of controlled heating or "cooking" to mature a substance</term> of our work continued upon them—we are able to reach our goal. Therefore, a matter is to be chosen in which is found a <term>quicksilver: 'argentum vivum' or mercury, the fluid, feminine principle</term> that is clean, pure, clear, and white, not yet brought to its full completion, but mixed equally and proportionally, united with such a white or red <term>sulfur: the active, masculine principle</term>. By our natural wit and our artificial fire, we may arrive at their inward cleansing and complete purity; once this work is finished, they shall be a thousand times more perfect and stronger than the bodies in which nature has simply worked <note>That is, the resulting "more-than-perfect" stone will be far more potent than natural gold or silver.</note>. From these things which have been said, provided you are ingenious, you will taste this delightful nectar, upon which the intention of the **Philosophers** is founded.

Since, therefore, it is necessary to render the matter revealed above "more-than-perfect" by our artificial labor, the method is not to be ignored, but must be learned from nature. A frequent and continuous operation perfects these metals by the instrument of heat alone—the same heat which exists in the mountains of minerals <note>Alchemists believed that metals "grew" in the earth through the influence of steady, subterranean heat.</note>—so that the thickness of the water is cooked and thickened for a time, until at last, in its course or circle of time, it passes into **quicksilver**. Likewise, from the "fatness of the earth," by that same continuous decoction and heat, **sulfur** is generated. By this same continued heat acting upon these two joined together, according to their purity or impurity, there are generated either perfect or im-

<meta>catchword: perfect [perfecta]</meta>

<summary>Flamel advises the seeker to find a raw mineral matter that nature has only partially refined, containing a balanced mix of mercury and sulfur. By mimicking the natural heat of the earth through "artificial fire," the alchemist can create a substance "more-than-perfect" and capable of transmutation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions following the selection of the "matter" on page 154. Flamel is now transitioning from the theory of why nature must be followed to the practical, unified method of the alchemical "Work."</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
# ANNOTATIONS

...perfected metals. What madness holds the minds of those working in this art, that they wish to proceed by a path other than that of nature herself: attempting to refute or overcome her, whom they can scarcely imitate by any industry. God demonstrates the right path to us through nature herself, namely, a simple and continuous <term>decoction: 'decoctio', a process of controlled heating or "cooking" to mature a substance</term> performed with a slow fire. And although we always speak of a slow fire, nevertheless, in the governance of this work, it must be increased little by little. There is, therefore, one stone, one matter, and one medicine, in which the whole <term>mastery: 'magisterium', the complete alchemical process or its final result</term> consists, to which we add no extraneous thing, nor do we take anything away from it, but in the preparation we remove what is superfluous: and this is done in the purification, which is perfected through <term>solution: 'solutio', the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</term>. When we wish to perform our <term>sublimation: 'sublimatio', heating a substance so it rises as vapor and solidifies again</term> of the stone, we place it with its whole substance into our vessel, the mouth of which we close firmly so that it cannot breathe <note>Meaning the vessel must be hermetically sealed, often with a "lute" or clay sealant, to prevent the escape of vapors.</note>, and we place it in the furnace among the ashes: finally we stir up the fire for so long, until the whole matter is dissolved into a mercurial water. Afterward, govern it by a slow fire, and for such a time until the greater part is turned into a black powder. In this manner, all those operations that follow are completed: namely, *Solution, Distillation, Descent, Washing,* and *Calcination.* The Philosophers have placed a specific chapter for each of these so that they might hide the art. For when our stone is in our vessel, and our matter feels our sun <note>original: "solem nostrum". In alchemical imagery, the heat of the furnace is often called the "sun" that acts upon the "microcosm" inside the glass.</note>, it is soon resolved into water, which ascends into heaven—that is, into the high part at the top of the vessel—and afterward descends to the earth, or the bottom. Likewise, seeing such water thicken and be converted to the black earth, which stood thick in the beginning...

->L<-

<summary>Flamel argues that the Great Work requires following nature's simple path of continuous heating rather than complex, artificial methods. He explains that all alchemical stages—like solution and calcination—actually happen within a single sealed vessel using one primary matter.</summary>

...beginning over the water, and finally by little and little beginning to thicken, they saw the earth sink under the water and reside in the bottom of the vessel, black and dreggy; they said this was the operation of **putrefaction**. Seeing also the earth, saffron-colored, blackish, and dreggy, standing under the water for a long space of time, losing its black color and evil stench in our sun, some of them called that operation **Ablution** <term>Ablutio: a cleansing or washing away of impurities</term> and **Ceration** <term>Ceratio: the process of softening a substance until it has the consistency of wax</term>, of which Morienus <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century alchemist, said to be the teacher of the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> says: *Know that this whole mastery is nothing else but the extraction of water from the earth, and the reduction of the same water again into the earth, until the earth itself putrefies by its own water and is purified; which after it has been purified by the help of divinity, the whole mastery shall be directed.* The Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Aristotle, often called "The Philosopher" in medieval texts, though here the context is specifically alchemical.</note> says: the earth is waxed and imbibed by water, and by a temperate decoction of the sun it is dried, and the whole matter is turned into earth, if anyone knows how to execute the operation perfectly. For Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note> says: *Its power is entire, if it be turned into earth.* <note>original: "Vis eius integra est, si versa fuerit in terram." This is a famous line from the Emerald Tablet.</note> Seeing therefore the whole matter turned into earth, and reduced into a solid substance, which did not flow, but stood constrained in itself, they said it was a perfect **congelation**. Thence Plato: *Dissolve (he says) our stone, and afterward congeal it, that is, turn it into earth with great caution, thus you shall have the whole mastery.* Which is nothing else but to make a right solution and a perfect congelation, as all Philosophers say. Therefore, so that metals may be changed, it is necessary that they be reduced to their <term>first matter: 'materia prima', the primitive, formless base of all substance</term> closest to the metallic genus, that is, into a flowing gum. Then at last this kind of first matter, by its innate aptitude,

> Every possible body, through operations contrary to its nature, is reduced to its **prime matter**; namely, through the **corruption** <note>In this context, "corruption" does not mean rot, but the breakdown of a thing's current form so it can become something new.</note> of its form, because every operation of a contrary is the corruption of that thing, and the corruption of one thing is the generation of prime matter.

**Demetrius** <note>Likely referring to Demetrius of Phalerum or another late-antique author cited in alchemical compendiums.</note> demonstrating the method of purification, or the **putrefaction** of both—that is, of the body and the spirit—before their perfect mixture, says:

> But perfection is never obtained from that spirit until, through a perfect mixture, it has led its body (which is its brother) into spirituality along with itself, so that both are made one, without separation forever.

This is achieved through the separation itself, which causes it to rise again from the dead; then, due to the uniformity of their natures, they shall be joined together and bonded by a strong adherence. And this is the specific form of our **dissolution** <note>original: "dissolutionis." The dissolving of a solid into a liquid or vaporous state.</note>; and this is the way in which nature's lap is revealed, by lifting her garment and making manifest what lies hidden beneath it. 

One must take care, however, that the garment is not lifted beyond its midpoint, whereby the moisture might act upon too little of the dry [substance]; and that which has been raised should be pressed down, and the whiteness of the elevated smoke should be hidden again in the innermost caverns of things, so that the moist may be joined with the restraint of the dry earth, coagulated, and retained. To reach this result, you should by no means be weary of repeating the operation—namely, by restraining the spirit... <note>The text continues on the next page regarding the "repressing" or "restraining" of the vaporous spirit to fix it into the body.</note>

...refining, and by hiding its brightness <note>original: "candorem." This refers to the inner purity or light of the material.</note> for so long, and then manifesting it again, until it arrives at that **substantial whiteness** <note>The albedo stage, where the matter becomes a brilliant white, signifying a high degree of purity.</note>, which can no longer be hidden, nor altered, nor varied, nor converted into other colors, except into red once the **digestion** <term>digestion: the process of exposing the substance to steady, gentle heat over a long period to mature it</term> is complete. And then finally, at that step, occurs the **conjunction** of body and soul, a commingling, or rather a union which is never separated.

Aristotle says that **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: "argentum viuum," also known as mercury</term> is the element of all liquefiable things: for when they are melted, they are converted into it. It is clear, therefore, that the operation of this medicine is an operation of nature, because it is composed solely from nature. For it is most certain that everything is composed of those things into which it is resolved, just as frost or ice is resolved by heat into water, because they were previously frozen from water by cold. In no other way is our **stone** resolved into quicksilver through our **mastery** <term>mastery: "magisterium," the specialized alchemical process or the Great Work itself</term>; therefore it was itself previously quicksilver. Indeed, the first principle is the reduction of the body into quicksilver, and they have called this **solution**, which is the foundation of the art. 

The philosopher Rosinus <note>Rosinus is the Latinized name often used in the Middle Ages to refer to Zosimos of Panopolis, an early alchemist.</note> says: "Dissolve the bodies and the spirits, and join them; then for the rest they cannot be separated by fire." Hermes says: "The first dissolved body, and the perennial water, congeal mercury with a perpetual congelation," which all the Philosophers also say. He who has ears to hear, let him hear. All molten metals behave like mercury, and if they could remain in that molten state while cold, they would be mercury itself. One must consider that moist metals, such as **Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for lead.</note> and **Jupiter** <note>The alchemical name for tin.</note>, are easily reduced into their primitive mercury. <meta>This page continues the discourse on the transmutation of metals, likely still quoting the philosopher Rosinus mentioned on the previous page. It details the color changes (the "Great Work") and the philosophical requirement to reduce metals to their first matter.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
->ANNOTATIONS.<-

...prepared, it consumes every body and reduces it into itself. For heat acting upon the moist first produces **blackness** <note>original: "nigredinem," referring to the *nigredo* or "blacker than black" stage where the matter decays</note>, and acting upon the dry it produces **whiteness** <note>original: "albedinem," the *albedo* stage</note>; then it works upon the white to produce **yellowness** <note>original: "citrinitatem," the *citrinitas* stage</note> and **redness** <note>original: "rubedinem," the final *rubedo* stage</note>. This may be seen in lead by **calcinating** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder or ash</term> it: first it is burned into a black ash, afterwards into white, and finally into yellow and red.

Indeed, the true principle of our work is the **dissolution of the stone**, because bodies that are dissolved are reduced into the nature of spirit, except that they are more fixed than spirit. Likewise: the **solution** <term>solution: the process of turning a solid body into a liquid or "spirit" form</term> of the body occurs with the **congelation** of the spirit, and conversely the congelation of the spirit occurs with the solution of the body. Then finally the body is mixed with the spirits and becomes one with them, so that they are never separated, no more than water mixed with water: because then all things are simultaneously reduced to their own homogeneity.

For this reason Aristotle said that the bodies of metals cannot be transmuted unless they are first reduced to their **prime matter** <term>prime matter: "materia prima," the original, formless substance from which all things are made</term>; if that is done, they well receive another form, to which reason is not opposed. But a transition from one extreme to another does not happen except through a medium. **Sulphur** and **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note> are the extremes of all metals: therefore metals will be the media between sulfur and quicksilver.

Consider therefore if you are able to digest the pure substance of quicksilver without combustion; otherwise your work is in vain. It is necessary, therefore, for the artist to use considerations whose certainty is handed down in other faculties upon which he relies. And so that his study may be directed toward the goal of experiment, he must especially labor and be occupied with two things: first, that [he knows] what [sort of thing is] the matter to be cured or **sublimated** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance so it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</term>...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the Great Work, specifically discussing the maturation of the material. It follows the previous page's conclusion that all metals must be reduced to their "homogeneity" or prime matter before they can be changed.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>
### NICOLAS FLAMEL
<meta>catchword: citur; (completes the word "perficitur" on the next page)</meta>

...[homogenei]ty, let him search most diligently for the elemental quality and its proportion. The second requirement is that he must take into account the "healing thing"—that is, the principal agent and transmuting power. For it is necessary for Art, using a most powerful agent, to prepare the matter so that the natural virtues of **active digestion** <term>digestion: in alchemy, a process of slow maturation through heat, similar to how a stomach "cooks" food</term> prevail over the passive matter.

For we see at times that through an insufficient agent, every digested thing struggles—that is, it suffers from an **imperfect digestion**, or **crudity** <note>original: "cruditatem," referring to raw or unprocessed material that has not yet been refined by heat</note>. This happens because of the mixture of raw, undigested matter, or because of the weakness of the digesting and terminating heat. Therefore, the greatest care must be taken so that the nature of the principal agent is so strong in its own nature, and fortified in its own way, that by its proper and natural duty it is capable of digesting and **converting the matter into gold**.

Furthermore, this nature must be disposed in such a way—through **refinement** <note>original: "subtiliationem"</note> and **sublimation**—that by its virtues acquired through art, it approaches the qualities of its agent and of gold. Thus, by means of these things, the principal **yellowing agent** <note>original: "citrinans," referring to the *citrinitas* stage of the work which follows the white stage</note> may, by its sharpness, convert the matter into the **Stone of the Philosophers** and into gold.

Let the natural **artist** <term>artifex: the alchemist, seen as a partner to nature</term> therefore examine his own intellect, so that by means of the condition of the aforementioned nature (which he may have been ignorant of), he may find it. Because we [authors] hand down to ourselves and our sons who understand that disposition—that is, the certain and necessary way of working—the method of fortifying nature as well, and of investigating causes. Therefore, the craftsmanship of this work would be impossible to discover unless the intellect of the artist is first truly informed by nature through his own **experiences**. Through these, nature demonstrates that she is known by herself, as well as her disposition, by which she is [perfected]...

<summary>This page explains the necessity of a "principal agent" (a catalyst) and proper "digestion" (heat treatment) to transform raw matter into gold, emphasizing that the alchemist must learn through direct experience with nature.</summary>

...is perfected: and it teaches how to find the measure of its approximation to perfection. Therefore, the proper work requires a proper disposition, since it is of that very nature which gives form to its own **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: *argentum vivum*, or mercury; here referring to the fluid, metallic principle of the work rather than the common metal</term>, and works upon its contrary qualities: namely, heat with its tempered dryness acting upon coldness and humidity. It does this by taking away the superfluous and adding or supplying what is absent and required, so that by this process it must necessarily receive the form of the **complete medicine** <note>The "complete medicine" is another name for the Philosopher's Stone in its final, curative state</note>. Nor does the aforesaid nature move in any other way, unless it is aided by art and artificial operation—namely, by a disposition proper to itself, without which nothing is accomplished.

When you have found this disposition, hide it away under the cloak of philosophy, just as your predecessors have done, who commanded that it be most strictly concealed; for it is the secret and **arcane mystery** <term>arcane mystery: *arcanum*, a deep secret of nature hidden from the uninitiated</term> of this whole art, which as of yet has been revealed in writing by no Philosopher.

It must be known, therefore, that when they say: "Dissolve the bodies in water," they do not mean, nor do they care for, waters that cling to or moisten what touches them. Instead, they mean **permanent water** <note>original: *aquam permanentem*; a metaphorical "water" or solvent that does not wet the hands, often identified with the "spirit" of the metal</note>, which truly does not cling to the touch, but runs across the surface without moistening anything: just as our quicksilver is not common quicksilver. For this matter desires nothing else to be joined to it except that which is of itself: for indeed, it contains within itself everything that it needs. This is said of our **Stone** <note>The "stone" here refers to the primary matter of the Great Work</note>, and not of gold or common quicksilver, and so forth.

Some people wrongly distill **corrosive waters** <note>original: *aquas corrosivas*; likely referring to strong mineral acids like nitric acid (aqua fortis), which the author considers too violent for true alchemy</note> from the lesser minerals, into which they place various types of metals; they corrode them and think they have been dissolved by a natural dissolution. This is false: for a **philosophical solution** <term>philosophical solution: a "natural" dissolution where the metal is reduced to its prime matter without destroying its generative power</term> requires a simultaneous permanence...

->### ANNOTATIONS ###<-

The wise say that the water of mercury is the said stone liquefied with its own proper water, which is fixed in the stone itself, and which is then running and white like water. Likewise, the airy humidity that is in **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: *argentum vivum*; the fluid, metallic principle or Mercury</term> is one thing with the aforementioned earth, and with the other elements existing in said earth: which humidity, although it is little, is nonetheless sufficient for nourishing the said stone in which it is, and for bringing it to completion. The **sun** and **moon** <note>original: *sol & luna*; the alchemical principles of Gold (Sulfur/Male) and Silver (Mercury/Female)</note> are present in the said composition. And yet, it is neither common sun nor common moon, but better than in their vulgar nature: they are present there potentially, not visibly. This stone or composite is one thing only, and of one nature (as all the Philosophers say), and it is a pure and clear nature from its own proper minerals, which transmutates under the governance of fire. Likewise, our medicine is drawn from metals, which naturally transmute metals, and in no other way. For it is necessary that the **elixir** <term>elixir: the perfected substance or "medicine" capable of transmuting base metals into gold or healing the body</term> of the Philosophers be of the principle, root, and nature of metals, and the medicine becomes more noble and more precious than the metals themselves, etc. From this it is clear that where the Philosophers speak of quicksilver and other dissolving humidities, they do not mean common things given simply by nature, but only of the quicksilver separated from bodies through the artifice of **putrefaction** <note>putrefaction: the essential first stage of the Great Work, symbolized by the color black, where the matter is broken down to its primary essence</note>, which is otherwise called **Azoc** <term>Azoc: a term often used for the Mercury of the Philosophers; the text provides a folk-etymology here relating it to *alumen* (alum)</term>: interpreted in Latin as a "powerful alum" capable of illuminating and clarifying every body with its own brightness. Therefore, since such quicksilver, or Azoc, or the **water of life washing the latten** <term>latonem abluens: "washing the latten"; latten (*laton*) refers to the impure, darkened metallic matter. Washing it represents the purification process that leads to the "Whiteness."</term>, cannot be extracted from bodies through generation except after putrefaction: therefore, its putrefaction will be its generation and disposition. Of mixtures and vegetables...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 165 regarding the composition of the "Stone." <page-num>166</page-num>

<header>OF NICHOLAS FLAMEL</header>

The birth <note>original: "nativitas"; refers here to the generation or origin of substances</note> is varied and diverse. For those things are called "simply mixed" which are born beneath the earth beyond our sight, or near its surface, from the mere mixing of elements composed with one another: and there are many degrees of this first and simple natural mixture. 

The first is the bare composition of the four elements, in which no change or alteration of one element into another has occurred. The second follows the first, because once the aforementioned stones—namely, the elements—are resolved, the **minerals** <note>original: "mineralia"</note> of which we now speak are generated: from which more noble subterranean species proceed, because in this, their action and **transmutation** <term>transmutation: the fundamental change of one substance into another, such as a base metal into gold</term> begin. 

To join them together, the **Philosophers** <note>the ancient and medieval alchemical authorities</note> have commanded that, after removing everything external, only heat should be applied. This heat, by assisting, excites the internal natural heat of the **gold** to direct and actively ripen the **mercury** <term>mercury: *mercurium*; here referring to the philosophical liquid principle</term>. Since, therefore, such **seeds** <note>original: "spermata, seu semina"</note> cannot be generated by art—because art does not know how to compose the mixture in the right proportions to produce seeds, neither in man nor in the earth, which is the proper place for their natural generation—these seeds, having been made naturally, are joined together artificially. 

And in this proportional action of nature and the digestion of mercury, the masculine and feminine natures remain together, enclosed as one: the feminine, such as earth and water; the masculine, such as air and fire. The Philosophers said that in our stone, the earth and water are as mercury, and the air and fire are as **sulfur** <term>sulfur: *sulphur*; the active, combustible, and masculine principle in alchemy</term>. Besides these two, nothing else enters, nor generates, nor multiplies, as is clear from the preceding statements. And this dissolution is only called the liquefaction of the compo-

<meta>catchword: sition,</meta>

<summary>Flamel explains that while the "seeds" of metals are created naturally by the earth, the alchemist’s role is to use heat to join and ripen these seeds artificially. He identifies Mercury as the feminine principle (earth and water) and Sulfur as the masculine principle (air and fire).</summary>

### WHAT WE HAVE GATHERED FROM DEMOCRITUS
->*it seemed right to add, so that the matter might*<-
->*become clearer from the opinions of many*<-
->*authors.*<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'S' featuring a floral vase motif within a square frame.</image-desc>

**O Wise one**, fix the **mercury** into the body of **magnesia** <note>a general term in early alchemy for a mineral substance or "mother" of metals</note>, or into the body of **Italian stimmi** <term>stimmi: an ancient name for antimony, often used as a pigment or to purify gold</term>, or into the body of **sulphur** that has not experienced fire, or into **silver foam** <note>litharge or lead oxide</note>, or into **quicklime**, or into **alum from Melos**, or into **arsenic**, or as you know; and cast in the **white earth of Venus** <note>original: "Veneris"; the alchemical name for copper</note>, and you will have a clear **Venus**; cast in **Luna** <note>original: "Lunam"; silver</note>, and you will have **gold**, and it will be **chrysocorallum** <note>a gold-colored alloy, literally "gold-coral"</note> reduced into a body. The same is also achieved by **yellow arsenic** and prepared **sandarac** <term>sandarac: here referring to realgar, a reddish-orange arsenic sulfide mineral</term>, and well-crushed **cinnabar** <note>mercury sulfide</note>. However, only **quicksilver** makes for splendid **bronze** <note>original: "æs"</note>. **For nature overcomes nature.** <note>original: "Natura enim naturam vincit"; a fundamental alchemical axiom suggesting that the internal essence of a substance can transform its external qualities</note>

But if you truly wish to know the matter exactly, apply your mind to two catalogs, because it must be known above all that **mercury** is placed both in the **yellow**—that is, in the **Sun** <note>gold</note>—and in the **white**—that is, in the **Moon** <note>silver</note>. And in the Sun indeed, he said, mercury is from **cinnabar**; but in the Moon he said mercury is from **arsenic**, or **sandarac**, or those things which follow.

<meta>This page continues the discourse attributed to "Democritus" regarding the transformative powers of Mercury. It follows the previous page's discussion on casting Mercury into various "bodies" to achieve a silver-like state (argenteam).</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>

<header>NICOLAS FLAMEL</header>

I come into Egypt <note>Egypt was considered the legendary birthplace of alchemy, or the "Hermetic art"</note> bringing natural things, so that you might despise that which is superfluous and confused matter. **For nature truly rejoices in nature, and nature overcomes nature, and nature retains nature.** <note>original: "Natura siquidem natura gaudet, & natura naturam vincit, & natura naturam retinet"—a foundational axiom of alchemy suggesting that only like-natured substances can transform one another</note>

**Mercury**, therefore, puts on all forms, just as wax takes all seals; so also the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>a purified, "living" form of mercury, not common quicksilver</note> whitens all things, and draws the souls of all to itself, cooks them, and seizes them. Being fitted for the chemical instruments, and having humors within itself, and even undergoing the process of corruption, it changes all colors; it remains while the colors themselves do not remain; indeed, even when the mercury itself does not seem to persist, it is then contained by the governance of the bodies and subjects. I speak of the composition of the **four bodies**, and those things which are akin to them, just as their matters are, and the souls of their matters are their own.

As a craftsman raising a statue from wood, or from bronze, or stone, contributes nothing of his own substance but only the form upon the matter; so also the **Mercury** elaborated by us through our art takes on every form. Once it is fixed—as has been said—to a body consisting of the four types, it remains strong and firm, both containing the body and being contained by it. For this reason, **Pibichius** <note>an early alchemical authority often cited in the "Turba Philosophorum"</note> said it has a great affinity. 

By the mercury derived from **cinnabar** <note>mercury sulfide</note>, from **arsenic**, **chalcanthum** <term>chalcanthum: blue vitriol or copper sulfate</term>, **ceruse** <term>ceruse: white lead, used as a pigment and alchemical base</term>, **magnesia**, or **Italian stimmi** <term>stimmi: antimony, used for purifying gold</term>, and the rest, all white or red bodies are to be understood. He also calls the body of magnesia **chryscocolla** <note>original: "chrysocollam"; literally "gold-glue," referring to borax or a solder used for gold</note>, or **gold-coral**. 

It is necessary, therefore, first to liquefy the species of things, and to convert them through smelting into a single color, and to reduce the two mercuries—the white and the red—into **quicksilver**, and to separate them for the stage of **putrefaction** <note>the stage of "blackness" or decay required before rebirth in the alchemical process</note>.

Therefore, **marcasite** (which the Greeks call **pyrite**...

Taking therefore the mercury, etc. Mercury is placed first, for it is because of it that the others are extracted. There are three principal things in this art: **Chrysolite** <note>a yellow-green gemstone, here likely representing a specific purified state of matter</note>, Mercury, and the yellow or white **honeycomb** <note>original: "fauus"—likely referring to the porous, crystalline structure of a chemical precipitate</note>, etc., for nature rejoices in nature. The Body of Magnesia is composed of four corporeal elements. The **Divine Water** <term>Divine Water: a transformative alchemical solvent, often symbolic of a universal spirit or a potent acid</term> is the nature which overcomes and retains its own rejoicing nature; it is overcome and retained by it along with the others. For is the nature of the thicker and earthly body not overcome by the nature of the chrysolite, which is spiritual and airy? Is it not overcome by the astringent Divine Water, so that—once joined—**Gold** and **Silver** <note>original: "sol & luna" (Sun and Moon)</note> may retain **Iron** or **Copper** <note>original: "marti vel veneri" (Mars or Venus)</note>, and so forth? Truly, when joined to these or others by themselves, they do not hold; yet...

# NICOLAI FLAMELLI

I, however, come into Egypt <note>Egypt is often used in alchemical texts as a metaphor for the land of hidden knowledge or the beginning of the "Great Work."</note> bringing natural things, so that you may disregard matter that is superfluous and confused. For **nature truly rejoices in nature, and nature overcomes nature, and nature retains nature.** <note>original: "Natura siquidem natura gaudet, & natura naturam vincit, & natura naturam retinet." This is one of the most famous axioms in alchemy, attributed to the pseudo-Democritus, suggesting that only substances of a similar essence can transform one another.</note>

Therefore, **Mercury** takes on all forms, just as wax takes on all seals; so too the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <term>Mercury of the Philosophers: not common quicksilver, but a purified "first matter" believed to be the soul of all metals</term> whitens all things, and draws the souls of all things to itself, cooks them, and carries them away. Adapted therefore to the instruments, and having humors within itself, even thoroughly undergoing corruption <note>In alchemy, "corruption" or "putrefaction" is a necessary stage of breakdown before a substance can be reborn in a purified state.</note>, it changes through all colors, and it remains while those colors do not remain; indeed, even while it does not subsist by itself, it is then also contained by the governance of bodies and subjects.

I say, it is a composition of four bodies, or by composition, and by those things which are related, just as their materials are, and the souls of those materials are their own. Just as a craftsman erecting a statue from wood, or from bronze, or stone, presents nothing of himself except the form into the matter; so also the mercury processed by us through art takes on every form, and once fixed—as has been said—to a body consisting of four kinds, it remains strong and firm, containing and contained. And for this reason **Pibichius** <note>likely a reference to an early Greco-Egyptian alchemist</note> said it has a great affinity. 

By mercury from **cinnabar** <term>cinnabar: mercury sulfide, the primary ore of mercury, valued for its bright red color</term>, from **arsenic**, **vitriol** <note>original: "calcanto"</note>, **white lead** <note>original: "cerussa"</note>, **magnesia**, or **Italian antimony** <note>original: "stimmi Italico"</note>, and the rest, all white or red bodies are to be understood. He also calls the **body of magnesia** <term>magnesia: a "maternal" mineral complex used as a base for the philosopher's stone</term> **gold-glue** <note>original: "chrysocollam"</note> or **gold-coral** <note>original: "chrysocorallum"</note>. 

It is necessary, therefore, first to liquefy the species of things, and by meltings to convert similar things into one color, and to reduce the two mercuries—the white and the red—into quicksilver, and to separate them for putrefaction. Therefore, **marcasite** (which the Greeks call **pyrite** <note>original: "pyriten"</note>...

genteam) <note>This catchword completes the word "argenteam" (silver) on the following page.</note>

Govern it (I say) until it becomes fire-resistant, casting off its blackness. Govern it with brine, or uncorrupted urine, or sea water, or **oxymel** <term>Oxymel: a medicinal mixture of honey and vinegar, used here as an acidic purifying agent</term>, as you know. **Mercury** indeed is white in its current state, but in its inner power it is lemon-colored.

Therefore, they labor in vain who explore mere materials and do not seek the natures of the bodies of **magnesia**. <term>Magnesia: in alchemy, this is not the modern element magnesium, but a "maternal" mineral base or a crude mixture of metals from which the Stone is born</term> For the body of magnesia is a mixture of four kinds of substances, as was said before. Taking therefore mercury, etc. Mercury is set over them, for because of it they are extracted. He also adds **chrysocolla**, which is **frog-herb** <term>batrachium: literally "frog-herb" or crowfoot; an alchemical reagent used for its corrosive or transformative properties</term>; found among stones is sulfur that has not experienced fire; masculine **claudianum** <term>claudianum: an ancient term for a specific alloy or mineral, possibly a type of bronze or a compound of lead and copper</term>; yellow arsenic from cinnabar; **androdamas** <term>androdamas: a legendary stone whose name means "man-tamer," possibly hematite or a variety of pyrite</term>; **cadmia** <term>cadmia: an ore containing zinc or cobalt</term>; fire-resistant sulfur, etc. These are names of the highest or "masculine" body.

There are three principal things of the Art: **Chrysolite** <note>original: "Chrysolitus" — a gold-colored stone, here representing a specific stage or ingredient in the process</note>, **mercury**, and the **yellow honeycomb** or **white honeycomb** <note>Metaphors for the crystalline appearance of the matter during its refinement</note>, etc., with nature rejoicing. The body of magnesia is composed of four bodily elements. **Divine Water** <note>original: "Aqua divina" — a powerful alchemical solvent, often a distilled acid or "mercurial water"</note> is the nature which overcomes and retains its own rejoicing nature; it is overcome and retained by it along with others. Is the nature of the coarser and terrestrial body not overcome by the nature of the chrysolite, which is spiritual and airy? Is it not overcome by the astringent Divine Water, so that **Gold and Silver** <note>original: "sol & luna" — the Sun and the Moon</note>, joined to **Iron or Copper** <note>original: "marti vel veneri" — Mars and Venus</note>, etc., might retain them? Truly, joined by themselves to these or others, they do not retain; even though...

O nature above nature, overcoming all natures! You are one whole nature, and you are the same through whom every work is accomplished. For your entire adornment is skillfully declared through a superfluous number, reaching its limit in a unity similar to divinity. For the center of unity remains immovable and stable. Indeed, it shows a circular and spherical contemplation of numbers within itself in the **greater work** <note>original: "opere maiore"</note>.

But let us return to where we started. The synonyms for **magnesia** are: sea milk, curd, eastern moon-foam <note>original: "aphroſellinum Orientis" — likely referring to a pearly or lunar substance like selenite</note>, magnesia of Lydia, Italian **antimony** <note>original: "Italicum ſtibium"</note>, pyrites of Achaia, and so on. Its flow is a mystery that lies hidden within it: a precious pearl, a flaming splendor, a garment woven with gold, a golden brilliance, and a Victorious Warrior. These terms can also be understood as referring to the whiteness of the **Great Work**.

The **chemical field** <note>original: "Ager chimicus"</note> is threefold. The **four brothers** <note>likely referring to the four classical elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note> are one part. The **Great Stone** is another part: the seed sown in the cleansed field, in its own earth. The third part is another **Mercury**, either yellow or white, joined to the body of magnesia. Taking therefore, etc.

...if you water it with a moist vapor, you will achieve a **divine work**. For it should be known that all substances and metals exist as, and are created from, vapors: for when watered, vapors become moist.

The **Scythian comaris** <note>original: "Scythica comaris" — likely referring to the strawberry tree (Arbutus unedo) or a specific mineral substance associated with Scythia in alchemical lore</note> also, when mixed with a new vapor, perfects all things.

There is a great difference between **wordy alchemy**, which is contained within a multitude of speeches, and that which is secret, hidden, or **mystical**. For the latter proceeds solely by the logic of creation, so that man—looking toward God and having been created by God—may learn the correct operation through theological and mystical discourses. For this operation is primarily celestial, because it consists of celestial materials and forms.

The methods of **mystical alchemy** are the figures of the heavens, according to whose rule this work is guided. Thus also metallic bodies, transmuted from their contrary nature, become smooth and airy.

Therefore, the seed sown in the **earth of the moon** <note>referring to a specific silver-like or lunar stage of the material</note> also perfects the **sun** <note>gold</note>; and because of the divine operation, ashes and **zinc ore** <term>cadmia: a term used for various metallic oxides or ores, often zinc oxide, found on the walls of smelting furnaces</term> provide this. Likewise, the divine work and the pure **divine water** <note>original: "aqua illibata"</note> with its ash lead to **regeneration**. Listen to **Mercury** <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> saying: "If you see all things turned to ash, consider that everything has been rightly prepared." For ash possesses great power and accomplishes much. For just as wood converted into ash becomes henceforth incombustible, yet serves in various marvelous works, such as the making of glass and other things; so too metals converted into ash—or rather **lime** <term>calx: the powder or "ash" produced when a metal or mineral is roasted (calcined)</term>—are returned once more into a certain power and operation. Since their nature imitates the entire universe and even the elements themselves, from which they are generated again, they possess a participation in a certain spirit, and

# THE WORKS OF NICHOLAS FLAMEL

...just as with things that are made of a material spirit. In this way, **our copper**—or if you prefer, the **body of magnesia** (or understand it in another way)—possesses a soul and a spirit just as a human does. For these malleable bodies and metals, after they have been turned into ash, resume their spirit while remaining in the fire; the fire supplies this to them, which they draw from the air, the creator of all these things. 

Just as humans and all living beings draw their vital spirit and soul from the air, so too does our burnt copper, having been roasted and subjected to all things until it becomes a dry soot, become an **aethesian stone** <note>original: "lapis æthesius"—likely referring to a stone of a burnt or "aethal" (sooty) color, or perhaps a corruption of "aetites" (eagle-stone)</note> in color, like a certain flashing aethesian. 

After it is roasted, it is "extinguished" <note>a technical term for cooling a hot substance by plunging it into liquid</note> in a divine manner, using what they call **divine water**. It takes on the consistency of a dissolved oil or the thickness of wine. Remaining there for forty days under gentle heat, in a vessel that does not allow vapors to escape, the material then undergoes **putrefaction** <term>putrefactio: the stage of the alchemical process where the matter decomposes and turns black, often considered a necessary death before rebirth</term>. This perfects the untouched mystery of the divine water—that very thing which is sought. And this is the **stone** which performs these wonders. 

Note well: **All things are one, and through this one, all things exist.** <note>original: "Vnum sunt omnia, per quod omnia"</note> Indeed, the work begins from unity or union, and it ends in the unity of the completed goal. For although there are many things in the natural world, this work is called "one" because of the most **divine water**; it is one nature conquering all others—not by its outward appearance, but by the secret art and rational science that remains hidden from many. 

**O heavenly natures, creators of natures!** By these words, an incorporeal nature is suggested. For unless you strip the matter of its outward form and make corporeal bodies incorporeal; unless you shake them out and dissolve them through corruption to bring that which is hidden and "breathable" <note>volatile or spiritual</note> into the light; and unless you make things incorporeal through this preparatory path, you will never give form to these natures, nor will you create [true] bodies.

# ANNOTATIONS

...devoid of [bodies] <note>The text begins mid-sentence: "ris expertes," likely referring to things devoid of bodies</note>, into which bodies introduce themselves, and by the benefit of heavenly wisdom are given form, in which they remain, and afterwards embrace the bodies themselves. Therefore, it is necessary to strip the matter, and by extracting its **soul**, thereafter to make the **bodies into non-bodies** <note>original: "corpora non corpora"—a classic alchemical paradox referring to the volatilization of solid matter into a gaseous or "spiritual" state</note> through it, to contain and to form them. Thus, matter must be stripped so that it may arrive at a stable perfection.

Therefore, the definition of this philosophy is: the dissolution of the body and the **separation of the soul from the body**, so that the **tincture** <term>tinctura: the transforming essence or "dye" capable of changing the properties and color of a metal</term> may introduce itself into the body, and so that the kinship of nature may delight nature—retaining, conquering, and remaining within it. This happens when that which is internal is pushed outward with pleasure, along with the entire earthly substance, and when through the **raising up of the water** <note>meaning distillation or evaporation</note> you have created an incorporeal composition. Likewise, it happens when, through the roastings, you have seen the **soot** extracted, and thus carried as if through a cloud. 

For an incorporeal nature cannot adhere except to higher things; for it is accustomed to be carried on high, together with that which is similar and related to itself. Therefore, the loved and loving nature are increased together, delighting and being delighted, and shining; both natures of the natural and one thing are one matter—otherwise a united nature—which conquers every thing of the whole world; wherefore it is said: **All things are one.** <note>original: "Vnum sunt omnia"—a fundamental Hermetic maxim</note> 

Moreover, consider this "one" and "single thing" as a composite of soul and body—making the body a non-body—into a tempered "one through one." Therefore, the white and pure **moon** <note>symbolizing silver or the purified white stage of the work</note> is the lunar form, the soot which is properly wiped away and washed by the **divine water**. 

Nothing, therefore, is missing except the cloud and the raising of the water: a work completed by the **governance** <term>regimen: the controlled management of heat and time during the alchemical process</term>. First, regarding this cloud: namely, of the soot [carried] on high to...

# OF NICHOLAS FLAMEL

...heaven, elevated through divine and heavenly water.

The **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> therefore, known to all, cheap and yet extremely precious, is joined from combative **Venus** <note>original: "pugnaci venere"—referring to copper, which in alchemy is associated with the goddess Venus and is considered reactive or "combative" in chemical processes.</note> with **mercury**, that is, the **male and the female** <note>original: "mare & fœmina"—the text likely intends "mas" (male) to pair with female, a common alchemical metaphor for the union of opposites.</note>. Therefore, you must cause the **red copper** to enter, and tinge the rust of gold. The battle of Venus with the battle of mercury: Venus, by battling with the **moon** <note>Silver</note>, is corrupted by her, and the moon is fixed by her conjunction with Venus; that is, the mercury is fixed through the sooty vapor of **magnesia** <term>magnesia: in alchemy, this does not refer to the modern element magnesium, but to a mysterious "primitive matter" or a dark mineral ore used as a starting point for the Stone.</term>.

Therefore, the "body" of the copper ought to be corrupted by the moon or by "lunar liquor," and remarkably ground down for as long as the art itself demands. For if anyone should make this body yellow through the **spirit**, and extract its powerful nature by preparing it gently according to the art, it **tinges** every body <note>To "tinge" means to fundamentally change the color and nature of a metal, a sign of successful transmutation.</note>. If anyone, destroying the body by "death" <note>Referring to *putrefaction*, the stage where matter breaks down and turns black.</note> and dissolving it, should turn it lemon-yellow through the soot by the benefit of the spirit, it tinges every body. For in this way the spirit is made from the body of soot—free from matter, the vigor and splendor of the body—by the command of its creator. Taking therefore the mercury, fix it to the body of magnesia.

The whole composition is made through mercury, and the body of magnesia through the male and female. The moon is mixed and ground together in the body, which is completed through the mist and soot: she, being reasonably governed, calls forth the powerful nature lurking within, tinging every body, **whitening Venus**, and taking away the **shrillness from Jupiter** <note>original: "Ioui ſtridorem auferentem." In alchemy, Jupiter represents the metal tin. Tin is famous for its "cry"—a crackling sound it makes when bent. To "take away the shrillness" means to transmute or "fix" the tin into a more stable, noble metal.</note>. 

A great mystery lies hidden in these things, which when you have seen, praise God. **Sulphurous things** are contained by sulphurous things; that is, the heavenly and fiery elements are contained by the earthly ones, from which those things can be obtained.

### ANNOTATIONS

...of **mercury**, namely, let it be joined with the water of terrestrial **magnesia** <term>magnesia: in alchemy, this refers to the "primitive matter" or a dark mineral ore used as a starting point for the Stone, rather than the modern chemical element.</term> through its own earth. In this way, the separation of moistures from dryness occurs, that is, the **souls of copper** <note>original: "animarum æris"—the 'soul' refers to the volatile essence or spirit extracted from the base metal.</note> from the bodies.

The **second key** is when the **moon** <note>Silver</note> and water are joined with the fire of the so-called **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti viui"—mercury</note>, through the air of copper in the rising of the mist, which is the reduction of cold things with moist things, and the mixing of higher things with lower things. Earth, that is, the **dross of copper** <note>original: "scoria æris"—the waste material or impurities left over during smelting.</note>, is joined with the **solar air** <note>The spiritual or volatile aspect associated with gold.</note> through fire, that is, through quicksilver.

The **third key** is when, through the parching of **putrefaction** <note>original: "putrefactionis"—the stage of 'death' or decomposition in the alchemical process, usually signaled by the matter turning black.</note>, the dross is joined with **divine water** through fire: that is, through the moon; the air of the sun is also joined with the earth, that is, with the dross through water, that is, through moisture.

The **fourth key**, indeed, occurs by the conjunction of air with earth, dissolved, that is, through putrefaction and the **roasting** <note>original: "assationem"—a process of heating the substance until it is dry.</note> of **seven reductions** <note>Likely referring to seven cycles of refinement or distillation.</note>, all being driven into one water, and existing in the **philosophical cinnabar** <note>original: "cinabari philosophica"—not the common mineral ore of mercury, but a perfected red state of the matter.</note>, which consists of the said mercury and the sulfur of magnesia. Once these things are completed in this manner, the fire becomes hot and dry again, generating the heat of air and the dryness of earth. Similarly, the water, when it becomes moist and cold, generates the moisture of air and the coldness of earth. Earth, when it becomes cold and dry, generates the coldness of water and the dryness of fire. Air, however, when it becomes hot and moist, generates the heat of fire and the moisture of water. Since each individual thing possesses two qualities, they become an **element** <note>original: "elemẽtum"—the tilde over the 'e' indicates a missing 'n' in the Latin shorthand.</note> consisting of three elements. All collected together become **twelve**, produced from three into four, according to the **twelve signs** <note>The signs of the Zodiac.</note>, and the four sea- <note>The text breaks here at "statio-", likely for "stationes" (seasons or quarters of the year).</note>

# OF NICHOLAS FLAMEL

...calcinations, as those of Springtime are airy, those of Summer are fiery, those of Autumn are watery, and those of Winter are earthy. This is the true composition of the elements through the art. Likewise, the seven colors appearing in the work are called the bodies of the seven planets of that same composition; these are now present, then later absent in the signs of the work, just as in the heavens they now enter their signs and afterwards exit them. Therefore, through its seven stars, which are called planets, and through its twelve fixed signs <note>original: "non errantia signa"—literally 'non-wandering signs,' referring to the constellations of the zodiac which provide the framework for the alchemical process.</note>, the true mystery of the Philosophers is perfected.

The colors of the elements in the work are as follows: from the feet even to the knees, the earthy element, which is black; from the knees even to the navel, the watery element, shining and white; from the navel even to the heart, the airy element, a yellow or citron-color <note>original: "citrinum"—a pale yellow-green color associated with the stage of the work between the white and the red.</note>; and from the heart to the neck, the fiery element, glowing and red. 

Next, when these four elements have been mixed among themselves, there is made from them the **lime** <term>calx: the powdery residue left after a substance has been "killed" or oxidized by high heat.</term> and the **ash of the Philosophers**, so that they may be perfectly and entirely joined through the final union, whitened, with the blackness utterly cast away. Therefore, this sacred and divine art and science of the Philosophers consists of one species and is perfected divinely and magnificently in this way: for if you make **bodies into non-bodies, and incorporeal things into bodies** <note>This is a fundamental alchemical maxim: to "spiritualize" the solid matter into a vapor and "fix" the spirit back into a solid form.</note>, you will work wonders, and you shall be the master of your wish, through the composition of the four elements; through the triple governance <note>original: "trinum regimen"—the three stages or methods of controlling the alchemical fire.</note>; the twelvefold mutation of the signs; through the aspects, species, and bodies of the seven stars; and through a certain singular, monodic, final union, you will glorify in trinity and unity the Son of God, the eternal Word, who perfected this lower world from four elements, and...

# ANNOTATIONS

...red work <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "this sacred work."</note> has adorned the circle of the year from the four changes <note>original: "ex quatuor mutationibus"—referring to the four seasons.</note>, and after seven ages he will lead those who are called and chosen into eternal rest: praising the unique and unfailing light in the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

There are, therefore, two things that consist of matter, since the **binary** <term>binarius: the number two, often representing duality or the division of the primordial unity into opposing forces like male and female or spirit and body.</term> number signifies matter. By their conjunction, through the mediation of a third, the causes of all things are made. Indeed, through the **triad** <term>ternarium: the number three, representing the reconciling force that unites the duality of the binary.</term>, the binary and every other number is reduced back to the simplicity of unity. 

Vapor, which is carried on high, is the matter of air; and smoke, or the smoky exhalation of dry things, is the matter of fire. These two do not agree with the remaining two—namely, water and earth—except through a third: that is, a bond and a fiery nature. These three, therefore, by the aforementioned triple rule, constitute the **nonary** <note>original: "nouenarium"—the number nine.</note>, which is closest to the second unity—namely, the **denary** <note>original: "denario"—the number ten, considered a "new unity" in Pythagorean thought.</note>. 

Wherefore, by a certain Sibylline enigma, this mystery is noted in this manner: *I have thrice three letters, I am four-syllabled, consider me. Each of the first three syllables has two letters, but the remaining one has the rest; five are mutes <note>original: "mutæ"—in linguistics, these are consonants that cannot be sounded without a vowel, but in this context, it likely refers to specific letters in the cipher.</note>. Of the whole number, there are two hundreds, eight, and three times three decades with seven.* <note>The total described is 305 (200 + 8 + 90 + 7). This is a famous riddle from the Sibylline Oracles, often interpreted by alchemists as a code for a specific name or substance, possibly "Anestis" or a variation of a divine name.</note> Understanding who I am, you will not be unskilled or ignorant of that wisdom which is in me. 

Alternatively: *The hundreds of the whole number are twice eight, and thrice three, and four decades.* <note>This alternative calculation suggests a total of 1649 or 809 depending on the interpretation of "bis octo centenaria."</note> 

In addition: The principles of knowledge are sense and experience. The latter is the principle of active thought, but the former provides the thought of the appearing thing; from these follows the **intellection** <term>intellectio: a direct, spiritual understanding or intuition that transcends mere logic.</term> of the **world-soul** <term>anima mundi: the "soul of the world," an intrinsic spiritual force that permeates all nature.</term> and of this prudence. For since this soul is exempt from all things, the sensible world...

# OF NICOLAS FLAMEL

...completes <note>This completes the thought from the previous page regarding the "syllables" of the mystical name.</note>. Therefore, three triads [three groups of three] should be considered to make up the orders of immaterial substances.

Since there are also three things which pertain to the universal operation, from which a **quaternary** <note>A composition of four parts.</note> composition is later made. He says to our **Molybdocalcos** <term>Molybdocalcos: A Greco-Latin alchemical term for an alloy of lead and copper, often representing the "body" to be transformed.</term> lead: "Inject mercury until it becomes incorruptible and permanent; when that happens, it will no longer be lead." All these things are one, and the two **tinctures** <note>The substances used to change the color and quality of metals.</note> do not differ in color; you see only one and the same. And just as the whole is from one species, some parts are of **Venus** <note>Copper</note>, others of the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, and others of the **Sun** <note>Gold</note>. Only one water is seen, <term>Rhaponticum: Here used as a coded term for the "surface of the sea" through a fanciful etymology from the Greek 'rha' (surface/flow) and 'pontos' (sea).</term>, named from the sea [pontus], while *Rha* denotes the surface; therefore, the "surface of the waters" is understood. "Inject the yellow Moon," he says, "and it will be <term>chrysocorallum: literally 'gold-coral,' a symbolic name for a red-gold substance.</term>, etc." Taking therefore mercury from the male, fix it as is the custom; throw it upon Venus <note>Copper</note> or upon the sulfurous pyrite, and it will be whitened—that is, into dry soot. Make the bodies not bodies, and the incorporeal things bodies, by raising the compound and extracting the hidden nature. Corrupt the bodies through mercury, and <gloss>through</gloss> the cloud of **magnesia** <term>magnesia: In early alchemy, this was not the modern element magnesium, but a mysterious primary matter or "chaos" from which the Philosopher's Stone was made.</term> join the male and female, and calling forth the hidden nature again, you will finally make through it the formations of the bodies—that is, the colors of the planets <note>The different colors that appear during the alchemical process, each associated with a specific planet/metal.</note>. In this way, therefore, bodies are rendered incorporeal, and those [incorporeal] things become corporeal.

If all things are thinned by fire, and the soot is carried upward, various and unbreakable bodies would be formed. By "fire," understand mercury and the fiery **medicine** <note>original: "pharmacum"—referring to the transformative agent or elixir.</note>, which mercury burns, corrupts, and thins out by one and the same artifice. Venus [Copper], therefore, does not dye, but is dyed; and when she is dyed, she dyes. Nothing
